Issue 361

https://www.deeplearning.ai/the-batch/issue-361📅 2026-07-10 22:00 UTC+8

Dear friends, The key idea of agentic coding loops is to have an agent keep working until it satisfies a condition, such as achieving a product specification. Coming up with the spec, the evals, or test set is one of the hardest tasks for many AI systems, and a key place to inject human knowledge. Once you have a clear spec, the paths to fulfilling it (such as having a coding agent iterate) become clearer. I'd like to share useful practices for building product specs that drive coding loops when quickly building 0-to-1 applications. As I'll explain below, one organizing principle I use is: AI tokens are cheap; human tokens are gold. (Yes, I know humans don't literally tokenize, but I work so much with tokens that I now think of myself as providing tokens to my computers!) When building a large, complex system, it is worthwhile to spend time architecting it well. This involves deciding, for example, what database do you want to use? How do you break down the workload across different services? What third-party dependencies are you locking yourself into? What are the API boundaries between frontend and backend? Thinking through such questions in advance helps to avoid being stuck with a bad decision that is hard to change. But when building quick 0-to-1 prototypes or applications in domains I'm unfamiliar with, I often move faster and with less deliberation. I find it convenient to jump quickly into directing a coding agent to start building, so I can examine what it has built in order to make the next set of decisions. No need to spend an hour of human time mulling over a design spec when an AI agent will spend 20 minutes building a simple prototype; I'd rather spend 10 minutes writing an inferior spec, see what the agent has built, examine its assumptions, and then refine the spec and repeat the process. Instead of "spec drive development" becoming a new waterfall process where writing a spec is a gate to further progress, this allows me to more iteratively refine the spec. For example, the first version of many apps I’ve prototyped had confusing UI designs that I modified the spec to fix, and no amount of my time spent tweaking the initial spec would have led me to realize in advance that these changes would be needed. Coming up with those human tokens on my own would have been too expensive. It was easier (and cheaper) to see what went wrong in order to explain how to do it right. As a software project matures, coding agents will work longer (maybe hours) to build to more complex specs, But at this early stage, it is fine to throw away the entire codebase and restart from scratch, so nothing is hard to change. All that code was cheap, in human time and token costs. What we learned from testing it was more valuable. Further, having one implementation to react to (or sometimes I'll ask the agent for a few different designs) gives you an efficient way to get feedback (human tokens) to refine the spec to help drive later iterations. If you're building a 0-to-1 product, I encourage you to put the coding agent to work, maybe even earlier than might feel comfortable, see what it comes up with, and use that as a starting point. Then document your reactions and keep iterating.

Read full article →

73 words

A fully programmable secure web gateway

https://blog.cloudflare.com/programmable-gateway-dlp/📅 2026-10-16 01:11 UTC+8

Every secure web gateway on the market works the same way: traffic matches a condition. A terminal action fires. Allow, Block, Isolate, Log. This model has been the foundation of network security for nearly 30 years, and it handles the common case well. But it has a ceiling, and customers with complex environments hit that ceiling regularly. Earlier this year, we shared our vision to build a truly programmable SASE platform . We also outlined how the term programmability has become diluted by the security industry. Most vendors claim programmability because they have public APIs, Terraform providers, webhooks, and alerting. Cloudflare offers all of those things too, but that’s not enough. True programmability means running your own code — arbitrary logic that you wrote for your specific environment — inline in the policy evaluation path, at the moment a security decision is made. Today, we’re excited to announce Cloudflare is bringing true programmability to Cloudflare Gateway and DLP (Data Loss Prevention) by integrating it with the entirety of our edge computing platform. We are releasing a new type of Gateway action, Forward , and custom validation functions, available to all Zero Trust plans. These capabilities allow you to use a Cloudflare Worker to transform and manipulate traffic, or to bring your own data to policy evaluation. The trade-off that every security team faces For as long as firewalls have existed, businesses have faced a binary choice. You can maintain your own security detection engine — your own firewall, your own proxy infrastructure — with high maintenance overhead and low portability, in exchange for maximum customization. Or, you can accept the constraints of a vendor, offloading the considerable maintenance cost, at the expense of flexibility to solve problems specific to your environment. In six years of building Cloudflare Gateway, we have heard from thousands of customers struggling with this trade-off. A security team migrating access controls for a critical internal application needs to inject HTTP headers based on JSON Web Token (JWT) claims from their identity provider. The data exists in the request context, but Gateway cannot read the JWT and write the header. So the team stands up a pool of NGINX proxies solely to perform this transformation. That infrastructure now must be patched, monitored, and secured... indefinitely. Another team wants a Slack notification the moment an employee signs up for a new SaaS service. The policy engine can detect the sign-up event, but it can only Allow or Block. So the team builds a Logpush-to-SIEM-to-Slack pipeline with a 5 to 15 minute delay and no guarantee of delivery. And piles on yet another piece of infrastructure. Dozens of enterprise customers have asked for warning pages that prompt users for justification before allowing access to sensitive destinations. Most then want to customize the ensuing behavior based on additional context about who the user is and the level of access requested. But serving customized, dynamic responses from a policy engine is exceedingly difficult. We have offered customers many extension points over the years. But along with every other firewall vendor, it is impossible to build features to cover threat vectors as quickly as they can appear. However, we also recognize that the best solutions are those which are infinitely extensible to your environment. Why only Cloudflare can solve this problem Cloudflare is not just a firewall, or a secure web gateway, a content delivery network, or an edge service. Cloudflare is a composable compute platform. Cloudflare Workers already allows customers to run complex logic on our network edge from any of our 330+ locations worldwide. Cloudflare Gateway already evaluates and enforces security policy on user traffic at those same locations, on the same machines, in the same data centers. Only Cloudflare runs both distributed customer compute and a full-featured secure web gateway and firewall in the same globally distributed network. We have the unique opportunity and ability to combine those functions into the industry’s first completely programmable and customizable SASE platform. Gateway and Workers were built on the same infrastructure from the beginning. When Gateway forwards traffic to a Worker, that dispatch is a local function call. There is no hairpinning. There is no additional latency from crossing a network boundary. This is the first time that customers will have absolute control over how their proxied traffic is processed, without compromise, using code that they can write and execute without maintaining any sort of infrastructure. Standalone SASE providers without their own compute platform require you to deploy automation in a separate cloud, manually configure webhooks, and accept the round-trip latency and management overhead of stitching together disconnected systems. No other vendor in this space has a programmable edge compute platform co-located with their security gateway. As threats advance at AI-powered speed, this will provide much needed flexibility and agility for security organizations. Two Gateway entry points: Forward action and Custom Functions A new Gateway action type called Forward and a new policy resource called a Custom Function are two distinct ways to call a Worker from a Gateway policy. The Forward action is available in public beta today, and Custom Functions will be available before the end of the year. Forward action: traffic transformation and proxy You can customize HTTP traffic behavior with a new policy action called Forward that routes the matched HTTP request to a Worker as a proxy. Policies with the Forward action are evaluated for traffic allowed by HTTP policies. The first matching Forward rule will then be applied to your traffic, and the traffic will be sent to its bound Cloudflare Worker. The Worker acts as inline middleware: it receives the full headers and request body. Your logic can then choose to modify either one (or take another asynchronous action of some kind), forward the request to the origin, receive the response, can modify that too, and return the final response to the user. import { WorkerEntrypoint } from "cloudflare:workers"; import type { GatewayMiddlewareContext, GatewayMiddlewareNext, } from "@cloudflare/zt-types";

Deploy with Void

https://blog.cloudflare.com/deploy-with-void/📅 2026-10-15 22:00 UTC+8

VoidZero's tools such as Vite, Vitest, Oxc, and Rolldown — bundled in one toolchain as Vite+ — have made frontend development faster, but building full-stack applications requires more than just a tooling stack. These applications need server routes, databases, local development environments, and a fast and easy way to deploy ideas to production. We’re excited to open-source three new products that bring server routing, typed data access, and Cloudflare deployment into a unified Vite workflow. Void Platform: A shared deployment platform for teams to host Void apps on Cloudflare. Void SDK: A composable JavaScript SDK for building full-stack apps with Vite. Void CLI: A CLI for provisioning resources and deploying Vite apps to Cloudflare. The CLI deploys apps built with the SDK to the Platform. All three products are open-sourced as a single JavaScript package and Vite plugin that you can install via npm install void . With Void , you can build your server and client together, carry types from your database to the UI, and let Void automatically provision the Cloudflare resources that your app uses. We originally set out to build a single, private platform for all users to deploy apps to. After VoidZero joined Cloudflare , we adjusted our direction to open-source the platform and allow anyone to install because we saw a growing need: companies want to encourage vibe-coded internal apps, but are concerned about security and do not want to give cloud access to every employee . Void addresses this problem. Void Platform: a cloud platform for your company The Void Platform is fully open source and you can fork it and adapt it to your needs by integrating your company's systems, customize deployment workflows, build features for your customers, or make it work on other deployment platforms. Use the platform as it is, contribute improvements upstream, or fork it and build only the features you need. The open source code can be used out of the box or as a starting point. As an admin of a Void Platform, you control access, set resource limits, inspect deployments and usage through the Void CLI or admin dashboard, and provide guardrails so everyone else can ship serious and vibe coded apps to the same shared platform. By installing a Void Platform, your team or customers have a shared deployment environment in your Cloudflare account on top of Workers for Platforms . Void Platform enables: Faster prototyping . Your team can quickly build prototypes, deploy them, and share working apps for feedback, without setting up a separate Cloudflare account or provisioning resources for every project. This reduces the time to ship internal apps from days/weeks to minutes. Non-technical teams building . This workflow also lets teammates who do not normally write code work with coding agents to build and share their own apps. A designer can create an interactive prototype, or an operations teammate can build an internal dashboard, then deploy it to the same platform. All with the right security measures in place. To self-host a Void platform, you need: A Cloudflare account with Workers for Platforms and R2 enabled A PostgreSQL database An account with your chosen login provider A domain for your apps Then install Void as a cloud platform by running a single command: Once your platform is set up, approved users can connect to it via the Void CLI for deployment. void connect https://api.orange-company.dev A user builds their app using the Void SDK, which is what powers Void Platform benefits like authentication, framework detection, resource provisioning, and more. In other words, Void Platform only works because of the accompanying SDK and CLI. Void SDK: zero-config infrastructure Void turns a Vite app into a full-stack Cloudflare Workers app. You or your agents can write code and Void will automatically infer resource bindings and provision resources such as databases, queues, or AI bindings during deployment. To get started, add Void as a Vite plugin: import { defineConfig } from "vite"; import { voidPlugin } from "void"; import { voidReact } from "@void/react/plugin";

Cloudflare and OpenAI: Making AI discovery better for everyone

https://blog.cloudflare.com/cloudflare-openai-discovery-agentic-web/📅 2026-10-15 21:00 UTC+8

Optimizing your site for AI is of little use if AI can't find it. The answer to someone's question may already be on your website: the installation guide you spent a week writing, or a first-hand account of using a product, with the details that make the difference for whether they buy it. As more people turn to AI for help, you want those systems to find your work, represent it accurately, and send interested people your way. You also want control over how they access your site. Cloudflare and OpenAI launched a research pilot in July 2026 with those interests in mind. We wanted to find out whether signals from Cloudflare’s network could help ChatGPT users discover new content more efficiently. Today, we’re sharing early results. So far, the answer is a resounding yes: the pilot has brought previously unknown pages into OpenAI’s search index, giving ChatGPT more sources to draw on to help people find relevant, timely information. Along the way, we’ve learned more about what makes a page worth discovering and how AI systems use the web. Finding more of the web The web is full of specifics. Someone fixing a washing machine needs the manual for their exact model. Someone applying for a passport needs the current requirements and fees. Someone choosing a local restaurant needs its latest menu and opening hours. Finding the right page can be the difference between a wrong answer and one that actually helps. These pages can be valuable even if few other sites link to them. In the previous version of the web, discoverability was getting to the top of a search result page. It was then up to the human who visited your page to determine the value of the information, book that table, apply for that passport. As people increasingly rely on AI to find information and complete tasks, discoverability matters in new ways. AI systems need to find the right sources, understand what's useful, and connect people with relevant information – whether that's through an answer, a citation, or a visit to the website itself. Cloudflare's network provides signals that can help OpenAI’s search crawler discover more useful pages across the web to surface to ChatGPT users. OpenAI then independently evaluates and ranks how those pages appear based on how relevant and helpful they are. Together, this creates a feedback loop that can improve both discovery and the quality of information people find. This joint pilot, which used a participating cohort of ~1% of Cloudflare’s total network, has enabled OpenAI to discover and index hundreds of millions of new web pages, helping people discover more useful content and get more questions answered through ChatGPT. By linking back to relevant sources, ChatGPT Search also gives people a way to explore the original content and discover the websites and publishers behind it. With GPT-6 and Intelligent UI , OpenAI is also making ChatGPT an even better place to shop, learn, and find answers, with more visual, interactive experiences tailored to what people need. This work complements these advances by helping ChatGPT discover more useful, up-to-date information from across the web. From discovery to useful answers Finding a URL is only the start. We need to know whether it leads to a page someone can access, whether that page offers anything distinct, and what it might help someone do. We’ve learned, for example, how easily automated traffic can make a nonexistent page look interesting. Bots repeatedly ask for files that aren’t there. Some sites redirect almost any address to their homepage. Other addresses lead to copies of the same content. Recognizing these patterns gives us a way to cut wasted work before a crawler fetches the page, and improve efficiency in a way that benefits site owners. We're also testing signals that identify and flag potentially harmful pages, adding another layer of information to support safer, more reliable discovery. A legitimate site can have a single compromised page, such as a fake login form planted by an attacker. Flagging that one page, rather than the whole domain, keeps people safe without penalizing the site owner. Early results suggest these signals can help identify pages that should be kept out of an index. Making discovery easier As more people use AI to discover information, website owners have new opportunities to reach interested audiences. For those who choose to make their content available to AI systems, these improvements make discovery easier and more efficient. Keeping up by fetching every page more often would be expensive for everyone. For a website owner, a crawler that repeatedly downloads an unchanged page adds load without bringing any benefit. The signals above are how we avoid that. Filtering keeps crawlers away from pages that don't exist or that redirect elsewhere. Change signals tell them when a page is worth another visit, and when it isn't. There's a balance to get right. Finding new pages can initially increase crawling as those pages are evaluated and indexed. The goal is to keep discovering and stay current while reducing the work that takes. For Cloudflare customers, that means less unnecessary load. For OpenAI, it means more of its search resources are spent on information that helps people. Making it worthwhile to participate The next phase will build on what we’ve learned: improve the selection of pages, test faster and more reliable change signals, and measure what happens after content is discovered. We’re continuing to work with OpenAI to understand how better site discovery can translate into real business outcomes, and we’ll keep sharing what we learn along the way. Domain owners’ choices matter throughout. A signal about a page doesn’t grant permission to access it. This research uses metadata about the web for search and discovery, rather than sharing page content for model training. You can use Cloudflare’s AI Crawl Control to understand and manage how AI systems access your website.

Read full article →

986 words

Yandex outage: Data shows that instead of going dark, malicious bots went on the move

https://blog.cloudflare.com/yandex-outage/📅 2026-10-15 04:24 UTC+8

Following reported strikes on Yandex data centers, claims circulated on the Internet that Russian bot farms and influence operations had been knocked offline. HTTP traffic charts showed steep drops, fueling speculation that the infrastructure damage had stopped these malicious actors. However, aggregate traffic metrics can easily mislead. There are two autonomous system numbers ( ASNs ) associated with Yandex: AS13238 is used by Yandex’s search engine, while AS200350 runs its cloud services. Using Cloudflare Radar and Bot Management data, we analyzed how these two ASNs were affected — and while both saw drops in traffic, they saw differing impact. Yandex Search (AS13238) : The drop in traffic consisted almost entirely of verified search crawlers indexing web pages, images, and site resources. Yandex.Cloud (AS200350): There was a decline in malicious activities like vulnerability scanning and probing, but high-volume automation did not vanish — it simply migrated to other hosting providers. While malicious traffic may originate from a particular provider, it does not necessarily tell us who is responsible for the traffic. Understanding why the strikes had little lasting effect on malicious traffic requires analyzing request behavior, rather than just overall traffic patterns. What happened: the effect of the strikes and pre-event baseline Between October 7 and 11, reported Ukrainian drone strikes hit three Yandex facilities in Russia, progressively knocking data centers and network capacity offline. As seen on Cloudflare Radar , this culminated in a near-total collapse of the Yandex network on October 11 when traffic plummeted to 99% below baseline for Yandex Search (AS13238) and 90% for Yandex.Cloud (AS200350). Despite a brief partial recovery, both networks remained disrupted through October 13, settling at 86-90% below normal baselines. (As of October 14 at 18:00 UTC, traffic is at 85% of normal.) A drop in overall HTTP traffic only confirms that the network went down. It does not reveal whether malicious or evasive bots were disrupted. Looking at the pre-event automation baseline, we can see that before the strikes, automated requests accounted for 36.1% of all HTTP traffic observed globally across Cloudflare's network, and 35.8% of traffic from Russian IP addresses . The outage had different effects on automated activity on the two affected ASNs because they served different roles. Yandex Search ASN (AS13238) is used for search index crawling. Cloudflare classified 99.5% of its requests as automated, and 93.6% of its bot requests as Verified . Our analysis showed that traffic from this ASN indexed pages, rendered pages, and rendered images, which is consistent behavior for a search crawler. The Yandex Search ASN supplied about 7% of Russia-origin bot requests, and just 0.05% of the global total. Yandex.Cloud ASN (AS200350) is a cloud-hosting network and carries a very different mix of automation. Approximately 95.0% of its scored requests were bots, but none of its observed bot requests were verified by Cloudflare. Yandex.Cloud’s bots looked more like software apps: nearly two-thirds of their requests sent data to websites, some identified themselves as JavaScript, Python, or Go programs, and a larger share went to software companies than Yandex’s search bots. The Yandex.Cloud ASN supplied about 2% of Russia-origin bot requests, and just 0.01% of the global total. Impact of the event on overall bot traffic "Bot traffic" refers to automated requests to websites on Cloudflare's network, such as crawlers and scripts, not necessarily activity on social media platforms. As Yandex’s networks lost capacity, their bot requests fell too. That was expected: nearly all traffic from Yandex Search and Yandex.Cloud was automated. While we saw the obvious drop in total traffic, we did not see any evidence linking a global disruption in malicious traffic to the drop in traffic from Yandex. Russia-origin bot requests fell roughly in proportion to the Yandex traffic that went offline, since most requests from those networks were automated. Date UTC+3 Global bot requests (% of all HTTP requests)* Russia-origin bot requests (% of Russia-origin HTTP requests) October 1–6: pre-event baseline 39.4% 50.4% October 7–8: drone strike on Sasovo 39.2% (−0.2%) 48% (-2.4%) October 9: drone strike on Kaluga 39.4% (+0%) 42.5% (-7.9%) October 11: drone strike on Vladimir 39.5% (+0.1%) 42.2% (−8.2%) October 12 39.6% (+0.2%) 43.6% (−6.8%) October 13 39.5% (+0.1%) 42.1% (−8.3%) * Radar reports global traffic by both total HTTP requests, and by HTML requests (an approximation of a page view). The above table reflects total request volume. Impact on Yandex Search ASN (AS13238): less search crawling The decline on Yandex Search ASN was almost entirely search crawling. Sampled requests showed that 78.1% belonged to verified YandexBot, 11.5% to YandexRenderResourcesBot, and 1.9% to YandexImages. Among websites and services, the industry segment that saw the steepest drop was Publishing and Newspapers, where traffic from Yandex networks fell 44%. Approximately 90% of pre-event traffic to news sites from this ASN was YandexBot indexing articles, with 40.5% of Cloudflare customers returning HTTP 403 Forbidden responses because the site owner blocked the crawler. When the Yandex Search ASN went offline, customers simply saw less indexing from Yandex. Impact on Yandex.Cloud ASN (AS200350): legitimate use and migrating malicious traffic Yandex.Cloud displayed a completely different profile. Following the reported strikes, bot traffic from Yandex.Cloud fell about 40%. Several attack-like categories showed especially sharp declines. For example, WordPress plugin probing fell 90% and scanner-identified requests fell 93%. Interestingly (and tellingly), Cloudflare customer blocks on Yandex.Cloud traffic also fell 70% during the event period, suggesting that proportionately less malicious traffic was even hitting the Cloudflare WAF from this ASN. The computer software bucket was predictable for an ASN meant to host third-party applications and software integrations. In fact, we even saw that api.cloudflare.com is a top hostname for Yandex.Cloud request traffic. An overall majority of that traffic succeeded (requests returned 200s) both before and during the event, pointing to legitimate usage. The gambling sector offers the clearest evidence that malicious automation relocated rather than disappeared. Nearly all of Yandex.Cloud’s bot requests were targeted at one gambling customer account, with traffic shifting among its hostnames. Immediately following the October 8 disruption, bot requests from Yandex.Cloud to this account dropped to zero. Simultaneously, identical requests began appearing from other Russian hosting ASNs, including Radist and Selectel. The incoming traffic maintained an identical fingerprint across providers: The same JA4 TLS client fingerprint The same uncommon request paths and HTTP headers The same custom User-Agent strings Overall, Russian bot traffic targeting gambling accounts remained essentially flat over the week, moving from 167.7 million to 170.1 million requests (+1.4%)

Inside Hyperdrive: How we solved a rare Hyperdrive MySQL caching bug

https://blog.cloudflare.com/hyperdrive-mysql/📅 2026-10-14 21:00 UTC+8

Cloudflare's Hyperdrive MySQL connection pooler has reached general availability after a beta period that required 20 bug fixes, including a rare caching bug that caused empty query responses roughly 0.001% of the time. The root cause was a capability negotiation mismatch between the popular Node.js mysql2 library and Hyperdrive's handling of the MySQL protocol, specifically around how result sets are terminated when the CLIENT_DEPRECATE_EOF flag is absent. The story illustrates how subtle protocol differences between database clients and servers can produce hard-to-reproduce failures in a distributed connection pooler.

Rewinding the exponential: How we keep hardware usage down

https://blog.cloudflare.com/rewinding-the-exponential/📅 2026-10-13 23:51 UTC+8

More than 20% of the web sits behind Cloudflare’s network, and our team, the Data team, owns the data pipeline responsible for plumbing that data (and more) from our global network to applicable central data centers. The Cloudflare network is a globally distributed system supporting tens of thousands of servers. Data that power Cloudflare Logs , SQL API , observability , internal analytics, and more flow through this network and use our data pipeline to serve customers. In May of this year, we were providing data delivery at roughly two billion events per second. This includes customer traffic as well as data sent from internal teams. Year after year, we tackle the same problem — exponential traffic growth against linear hardware growth. We have to react to this increase in order to keep resource costs under control. Over the past five years, we’ve made various improvements to compensate for the data volume increase in comparison to hardware allocation, equivalent to deferring two and a half years of hardware expansion. This post details those changes: breaking up our monolithic data schema in 2022, adjusting compression in 2024, rearchitecting pipeline replication in spring 2026, decentralizing sampling in summer 2026, and introducing aggregations into the pipeline in fall 2026. Background Our pipeline starts with an internal service called Logfwdr, where we collect from highly distributed data sources (think: Workers, FL ). We then feed this data to a set of centralized receivers, which eventually push them onto our buffering system , Buftee. Consumers fetch data from Buftee and distribute data to their final destinations, like ClickHouse, which we rely on heavily . Unpacking the monolith In 2022, we dismantled our one and only schema. Previously, there was a single stream packed with every product's fields, forcing Cloudflare-internal consumers to ingest everything and demux or discard records via filters. At the time, most of Cloudflare’s data products were built on HTTP, Security and CDN metadata. In 2021, our message schema looked like this: Some fields here belonged under the same product, but some were completely irrelevant to each other. The hypothesis was that none of these teams require 100% of fields in the monolithic schema. In other words, we can create small messages for each product’s required fields. Although we were going to duplicate fields, some of them in every message, replacing this firehose with product-specific schemas actually reduced the backbone bandwidth usage by 35-40%, knocking out roughly a year’s worth of compressed traffic growth. Compression By 2024, as traffic steadily grew, we were tackling network congestion issues on a daily basis. To react to this, we changed our compression algorithm from LZ4 to zstd. We had already experimented with zstd compression before, but we noticed a higher CPU usage at the time. Therefore, we were careful to track the metrics to ensure the trade-off was worth it. Initially, we rolled this change out to a few servers. To our relief, we didn’t observe a noticeable difference in CPU usage. With more confidence, we gradually rolled out to our entire fleet, which also showed little impact to CPUs. In the end, there was nearly a 40% reduction in compressed traffic throughput. The uncompressed bytes remained at the same growth, meaning we could support more than 1.5x the amount of traffic with the same size pipe. Or another way to put it, the estimated growth-equivalent reductions put us at about 10 months prior. Now, in 2026, we attempted three strategies to reduce uncompressed bytes (and thus, compressed bytes). Load balancing To make analytics resilient against regional disasters, our systems require redundant data to be stored in multiple centralized regions (data subject to Customer Metadata Boundaries is stored strictly within its designated region). Historically, the simplest way to achieve disaster resilience was double-writing: Logfwdr transmitted two full copies of every non-regional analytics event — one to each centralized region. Each region then independently processed a complete copy through to ClickHouse. We also apply sampling in each region, an incredibly scalable technique that works for all volumes of data during any traffic conditions. We downsample high-volume traffic while rare events are preserved. This infrastructure was manageable when only a handful of streams used secondary regions. However, as we expanded disaster resilience across almost all analytics streams, Logfwdr traffic doubled across the board. Sending every event, twice, over wide-area transit was unsustainable against our exponential traffic growth. We had already tackled a similar challenge for Logpush . For Logpush, Logfwdr treats the centralized data centers as a unified, shared pool of receivers: each message is sent to only one region, failing over to another destination if a machine or facility is unreachable. That model had already proven its worth during large incidents, shifting traffic seamlessly from one location to another without human intervention. This automatic failover was possible because Logpush delivery is stateless, as customer destinations do not care which centralized data center pushes the logs. Analytics was trickier. Unlike stateless logs, ClickHouse clusters in both regions still need a full copy of non-regional data to allow instant, independent query failover. The breakthrough was decoupling pipeline transit from database redundancy: 1. Shared Ingest Pool: We adopted Logpush’s model to the analytics streams. Logfwdr dispatches each event exactly once across wide-area transit to whichever centralized receiver is available. 2. Late Replication from centralized regions: Instead of replicating traffic at source, replication happens as late as possible in the pipeline. Data lands in our distributed buffering layer, Buftee, and remote inserters stream cross-data-center copies downstream. 3. Resilient Failure Handling: If a centralized region goes down, Logfwdrs immediately routes all non-regional traffic to the surviving region. If the inter-data center connection blips, each cluster continues ingesting its local share without backpressure; remote inserters catch up from Buftee's retention buffer once the backbone recovers. For the datasets we migrated, the cutover eliminated approximately 43 GB/s of compressed transit traffic while adding only ~7 GB/s of cross-data-center replication on our pipeline. Two architectural factors explain this asymmetry: 1. Replication happens downstream of sampling : In the old model, Logfwdrs transmitted two copies of raw, unsampled events over wide-area transit. In the new model, Logfwdrs send a single copy to centralized receivers, which immediately apply sampling before buffering into Buftee, carrying only this already-compacted stream. 2. Better compression through uniform partitioning : Data at source is fragmented across thousands of individual servers. After centralization, streams are aggregated and partitioned into uniform batches over wider time windows. This structural uniformity allows compression algorithms to achieve dramatically higher compression ratios than Logfwdr transit batches ever could. Overall, this was a net reduction of 36 GB/s across wide-area networks. We rolled this out initially to two datasets, HTTP analytics and Ready-Analytics, our in-house one-click analytics product . We’ve been actively migrating more datasets to this load-balanced architecture since the spring. Sampling from the get-go Another strategy we employed was further expanding our sampling mechanism so that we pulled critical processing earlier into the pipeline. Sampling was already used in our centralized services, particularly the receivers, easing pressure on downstream systems. We can be more resource efficient if we send less data downstream (without sacrificing data quality). The solution here was to simply sample in Logfwdr, in addition to main

Read full article →

Filter, reshape, and redact logs before export with Transformers

https://blog.cloudflare.com/transformers-on-logpush/📅 2026-10-12 22:44 UTC+8

Logpush delivers logs in batches as quickly as possible, enabling Cloudflare to provide information almost in real time. During our recent Birthday Week , we made Logpush available on all self-serve plans and Transformers generally available. Those changes were among eight major updates to Cloudflare Observability and reflect our commitment to make every Cloudflare feature available to everyone . This post takes a deeper look at how Transformers work inside Logpush's export pipeline. Observability teams use Logpush to export Cloudflare logs to security information and event management systems (SIEMs), analytics platforms, and object storage. Before those logs reach their destination, teams may need to remove health checks, omit sensitive fields, or reshape records for dashboards and investigations. Today, this often requires a separate processing service that teams must deploy, scale, monitor, and maintain. For example, a team that collects logs from multiple CDNs can add a static provider field to normalize records before they reach its analytics platform. Teams also pay to export, ingest, and store records they discard as soon as they arrive, so filtering them before delivery can reduce downstream data volume and cost. With Transformers, that processing moves into the Logpush export workflow.Teams define a SQL query to filter records, redact sensitive data, and reshape the output by adding, renaming, removing, or nesting fields. Logpush applies the query before export, so each destination receives data in the form it needs. We made this workflow simple for customers by embedding a SQL transformation engine in Logpush's high-throughput export pipeline while keeping performance and resource use predictable. How Logpush Transformers work A Transformer is a SQL query written for a specific Logpush dataset. The query references fields from that dataset and defines which records and fields Logpush should export. Logpush applies the query to each batch before export. Records that do not match the WHERE clause are removed. For the remaining records, the output of the SELECT statement becomes the exported record. Transformers can change the structure and contents of each record, and the transformed results are exported in NDJSON format. Customers can create, preview, and manage Transformers in the Cloudflare dashboard through Transformer Studio or programmatically through the API . After saving a Transformer, they can attach it to a compatible NDJSON Logpush job that uses the same dataset. Each update creates a new version, so customers can review how a Transformer has changed over time. The following example reshapes a Cloudflare HTTP request log to match a team-defined schema used across CDN providers. It does not convert between serialization formats: both the input and output use NDJSON. { "ClientIP": "203.0.113.42", "ClientRequestHost": "example.com", "ClientRequestMethod": "POST", "ClientRequestPath": "/api/checkout", "ClientRequestUserAgent": "Mozilla/5.0", "EdgeResponseStatus": 502, "EdgeStartTimestamp": "2026-05-21T15:00:00Z", "RayID": "8e2a1c60ef9e1c9a" } The Transformer renames and nests fields, redacts part of the client IP address, adds provider and error information, removes unused fields, and filters out internal and health-check traffic: SELECT extract(epoch FROM to_timestamp(EdgeStartTimestamp)) AS unix_ts, regexp_replace(ClientIP, '\.\d+\.\d+$', '.XXX.XXX') AS redacted_ip, UPPER(ClientRequestMethod) AS method, EdgeResponseStatus AS status, RayID AS ray_id, 'cloudflare' AS provider, named_struct( 'host', ClientRequestHost, 'path', ClientRequestPath ) AS request, EdgeResponseStatus >= 500 AS is_server_error FROM http_requests WHERE ClientRequestHost NOT IN ( 'internal.example.com', 'health.example.com' ) AND ClientRequestPath NOT LIKE '/healthz%' AND ClientRequestPath NOT LIKE '/metrics%' The resulting NDJSON record uses the normalized schema: { "unix_ts": 1779375600, "redacted_ip": "203.0.XXX.XXX", "method": "POST", "status": 502, "ray_id": "8e2a1c60ef9e1c9a", "provider": "cloudflare", "request": { "host": "example.com", "path": "/api/checkout" }, "is_server_error": true } How Transformers executes SQL with Apache DataFusion and Arrow Cloudflare's data pipeline ingests more than one billion events per second. Its Logpush delivery service, which we call Bufstream, delivers more than 110 million Logpush records per second to customer destinations. It runs across hundreds of Kubernetes pods, each claiming a time slice of data from Buftee , Cloudflare's distributed log-based in-house message broker. This model distributes work horizontally across Logpush jobs. Job volume follows a Pareto-like distribution: most jobs operate at lower volumes, while a small number of high-volume jobs process millions of logs each second. Transformers needed to meet the same operational requirements as the rest of Logpush: reliable delivery, high throughput, and predictable resource use without introducing additional failure scenarios into the export hot path. Our initial prototype ran customer-defined WebAssembly Transformers in Cloudflare Workers, which provides globally distributed, isolated compute. However, invoking a Worker would insert a network call into the Logpush export path. That remote dependency could introduce transient timeouts and retries, reducing delivery throughput and increasing delivery latency. We decided to keep Transformer execution in-process within our delivery service to prioritize reliability and delivery throughput. We evaluated several SQL execution engines and selected Apache DataFusion , which already powers Cloudflare Basin . Apache DataFusion is a Rust query engine built on Apache Arrow, with a SQL planner and vectorized, columnar execution engine. In a benchmark, executing a prepared Transformer against a synthetic 5,000-row http_requests batch from a single Go goroutine took about 3 ms, equivalent to roughly 1.6 million rows per second. Attaching a Transformer to a job adds CPU work to its processing path. Bufstream uses per-job transform parallelism so high-volume jobs can execute transform batches concurrently. Embedding DataFusion in Bufstream lets those batches use the service's existing delivery infrastructure, including its scheduling, batching, and destination controls, without introducing a separate transformation tier or network hop. Choosing DataFusion meant connecting Bufstream, which is written in Go, to an engine written in Rust. The next challenge was moving typed batches efficiently across that boundary. The final design of the in-process Transformer pipeline is shown in the diagram below. From records to columns Bufstream receives compressed Logpush batches containing Cap'n Proto records from Buftee. For a job with a Transformer attached, Bufstream decompresses the batch, parses the eligible logs, and builds a typed Apache Arrow RecordBatch before executing the transform. Apache Arrow is a language-independent, columnar in-memory format designed for efficient analytics on modern CPUs and GPUs. It represents flat and nested data as typed, contiguous columns. Arrow IPC gives Bufstream and DataFusion a standard way to exchange batches across the foreign function interface (FFI) boundary between Go and Rust, exposed through a C application binary interface (ABI) and called from Go using cgo , Go's mechanism for calling C code. The Go and Rust runtimes do not share ownership of transform data. Go passes a pointer to its input Arrow IPC buffer to Rust only for the duration of the cgo call, and Rust does not retain that pointer after returning. Rust owns the transformed output buffer; Go copies it into Go-managed memory and then explicitly releases the Rust allocation. This makes ownership and lifetimes explicit across the language boundary, avoiding cross-runtime memory-management bugs. Using Arrow IPC means the Rust DataFusion wrapper can u

Introducing Clef-omni with full multimodality, plus a faster Clef and a cheaper Clef-flash

https://blog.cloudflare.com/clef-faster-cheaper-multimodal/📅 2026-10-10 02:27 UTC+8

Following last week’s release of Clef and Clef-flash , Cloudflare’s open-weight decision models, we decided to bring forth more gifts. Today, we’re releasing Clef-omni, which takes in audio and video input alongside text and image. We also cut the price of Clef-flash so it is now cheaper than Jev, and we made Clef faster. Although the model game is still early for Cloudflare, innovation and iteration is in our DNA, and we apply these principles to everything we do. In fact, the story of Clef came together over the course of less than a week. We decided we wanted to do something in the decision model space on a Friday evening, trained the model over the weekend, and launched it on Thursday. Even with such a short timeline, we were able to ship performant, high-quality, open-weight models for the community — imagine what more we can do in the future. For today, we’re excited to keep up the momentum with new additions and improvements to our Clef family of models. This is just the beginning, and we’ll continue to get better, faster, cheaper, and more innovative. Clef-omni takes audio, video, image, and text input Our new Clef-omni model is able to take audio, video, image, and text input. This changes the paradigm for decision models, which have been largely text-only since the debut of Jev from TypeSafe. With Clef, we supported images and video frame arrays, but Clef-omni is able to take in audio (wav or mp3) and video (mp4 or webm) alongside text and images. Instead of setting up cascading pipelines of models that transcribe speech-to-text, or splitting audio and image channels from video, you can just call one model to make decisions across any modality. We are now one step closer to a model that is able to interact with the world as we experience it — through audio, visual, and textual communication, all in one. Check out our developer docs for the new Clef-omni model. We also released the model open-weights on HuggingFace . For a quick start, here’s how you can send new modality inputs to Clef-omni: curl https://api.cloudflare.com/client/v4/accounts/$CLOUDFLARE_ACCOUNT_ID/ai/run/@cf/cloudflare/clef-omni \ -X POST \ -H "Authorization: Bearer $CLOUDFLARE_AUTH_TOKEN" \ -H "Content-Type: application/json" \ -d '{ "model": "clef-omni", "state": "Review the installation: a photo of the unit, an audio recording of it running, and a video of the fan.", "images": ["data:image/png;base64,<base64-png>"], "audio": ["data:audio/mpeg;base64,<base64-mp3>"], "videos": ["data:video/mp4;base64,<base64-mp4>"], "questions": { "label_visible": {"type": "noul", "instructions": "Is the model and serial number label visible in the photo?"}, "sounds_normal": {"type": "noul", "instructions": "Does the unit sound like it is running smoothly, without rattling or grinding?"}, "fan_running": {"type": "noul", "instructions": "Is the fan running in the video?"} } }' Clef-omni expands our open-weight decision architecture to natively handle multimodal workflows. We built this on a Qwen3-Omni-30B-A3B-Instruct mixture-of-experts (MoE) foundation, where the model was already capable of processing text, imagery, audio, and video directly within a single pipeline. However, we adopt the primary comprehension backbone while discarding the text-to-speech output components. In production, Clef-omni executes a quick prefill pass across the complete payload, scoring all modalities and valid parameter options simultaneously. Because we skip output token generation since Clef models are not Large Language Models (LLMs), we remove the overhead of transcribing or captioning incoming files. Media elements map straight into the unified sequence, where video and audio are synced with visual frames for joint processing. Clef-omni then pulls candidate values directly from internal embeddings using our two-stage attention routing: every valid option gathers key evidence from the input (regardless if they are buried in text snippets, visual elements, or audio streams) before field vectors cross-attend across the full context to compute confidence scores. We add a built-in lexical grammar that preserves option semantics, so that we can deliver fast, schema-constrained scoring across every input type. We train the model with the same techniques as we did with Clef — freezing the Qwen3 backbone, training low-rank adapters (LoRA), and applying our standard post-training approach: combining label-smoothed cross-entropy loss with Brier score calibration. The result is a model that is resilient and designed to succeed regardless of schema variations, field ordering, and prompt structures. This brings several core upgrades to the Clef model family. Calibrated, schema-bound decisions now seamlessly handle text, images, audio, and synchronized video in a single API call. It's also fast. Text-only decisions return in about 130 ms at the median, image inputs in about 150 ms, and audio clips in a few hundred milliseconds. Even a full 21-second video clip with sound is scored in about 1.5 seconds, all in a single API call. Our benchmarking shows strong performance across benchmarks, even with new modalities and MoE architecture. Benchmark Clef-omni Clef Clef-flash Jev BFCL · case exact 98.2 98.47 98.76 95.75 ToolRet · nDCG@10 66.6 69.19 66.43 65.28 API-Bank · accuracy 92.7 91.93 93.11 88.19 Home appliances · case exact 69.3 82.95 97.73 52.27 When2Call · accuracy 63.3 72.37 65.58 80.97 BANKING77 · macro-F1 94.8 94.20 90.93 79.74 CLINC150+OOS · macro-F1 97.7 97.43 66.77 89.27 BRIGHT · nDCG@10 42.0 45.91 39.26 47.52 Amazon ESCI · macro-F1 57.8 57.48 57.39 55.21 PhishNChips · accuracy 73.2 79.60 75.05 62.55 We also benchmarked against the TypeSafe evals to show how Clef-omni performs . The model weights on Hugging Face are untouched and were trained to support a 256k context window should you choose to self-host it. From our usage data, we see that only 0.24% of requests exceed 24k input tokens. Based on this data, we decided to make the call to drop the context window to 24k for Clef-flash in order to price it at a more accessible entry point. Our Clef model remains at a 64k context window, and we encourage folks with larger context needs to switch to Clef instead of Clef-flash. Introducing on-demand CPU and memory profiling with flamegraphs for Workers and Durable Objects

https://blog.cloudflare.com/workers-on-demand-profiling/📅 2026-10-09 21:00 UTC+8

Understanding why an application is using more resources than expected, whether that is CPU or memory, can be challenging. Logs and aggregate metrics can only get you so far. Thankfully there is a better way: CPU or memory profiling can show you the exact function where your application is using CPU or allocating memory. Today we are happy to announce support for CPU and memory profiling of Workers and Durable Objects. From the Workers Observability page, you can now request an on-demand CPU or memory profile of an active Worker, inspect it as an interactive flamegraph, and download the profile file for further analysis. This method of profiling gives you a useful perspective into what your code is doing and how you can improve it in a real-world setting. That’s because the best way to understand your application is to profile it in production. To try this on one of your Workers, you have a choice of using the CLI or the Cloudflare Dashboard. To use the CLI, make sure you have the cf package installed , then simply run: cf workers versions profile latest \      --worker-id "$WORKER_ID_OR_NAME" \      --duration-ms 5000 \      --profile-type cpu > worker-cpu.pprof To use the dashboard, head to the Cloudflare dashboard and then access the list of Workers on your account by navigating through Build → Compute → Workers & Pages. Select your Worker and navigate to its Observability tab. You can then use the drop down to select “Flamegraph”: You can then request both CPU and memory profiles for your Worker on this page. The duration determines how long the profiler should run for on your Worker. You can also select different versions of your Worker to be profiled. Your Worker needs plenty of traffic to be successfully profiled, so make sure you choose a version that has enough traffic. Once you click the Capture Profile button, the profiler will run for the duration that you have selected, and you will then see a flamegraph representing the results of the profile being rendered. Each rectangle in the flamegraph represents a function call, with the width representing the amount of CPU time or memory being used by that function. You can click on different functions to focus on and hover to see more details about them: Don’t be afraid to capture a couple of profiles, then look for the widest functions to determine what is using the most resources in your Worker. You might also find that the table view is useful to get a quick sense of which functions are most commonly seen in the profile. If your Worker is implemented in TypeScript, then you should make sure that you have source maps enabled for your project , otherwise your profile may show obfuscated function names that aren’t easy to understand. We have already seen multiple teams at Cloudflare using CPU and memory profiling to identify opportunities for optimizing memory and CPU usage, fixing out-of-memory (OOM) errors, and improving performance. Below are some examples of this. Using CPU profiling to find optimizations Let’s look at a real example of how you can use CPU profiling to optimize your Workers. We will look at a CPU profile of the Worker that implements the R2 binding. This Worker receives a lot of traffic and so eliminating even the smallest amount of wasted CPU cycles can have a massive impact on its performance. We set up the profiler to capture CPU traces, with a duration of 50 seconds. Once we received the rendered profile, we saw this: As mentioned previously, the widest boxes are those which are using up most of the CPU time. Some of them aren’t surprising, for example decryptBlock is R2 decrypting object data on the way out, and fillResponse is just moving bytes. There weren’t any clear ways to optimize these functions. As some of the boxes are less visible, it can be a good idea to take a look at the table view. Sorting by Samples points us at genericR2JsonReplacer : This function represented over 5% of the CPU time, but what caught our attention was the function calling itself recursively. This looked like wasted work, and it was. Even though the replacer was being called by JSON.stringify on every node in the JSON tree, the replacer itself was also walking the tree. This meant that a value nested five levels deep was processed five times. Fixing this enabled us to eliminate the duplicate work and make the genericR2JsonReplacer function 2.7x faster. The second candidate in the profile that we looked at was a duplicate call to metrics . When looking at the code, we saw something close to this: function ship() { if (!registry.metrics().length) { return; }

Bridging technical depth and usability: The story behind Radar’s redesign

https://blog.cloudflare.com/radar-redesign/📅 2026-10-08 21:00 UTC+8

Radar provides a real-time view into global Internet trends, powered by Cloudflare’s global network. We support Cloudflare’s mission to help build a better Internet by making web data visible, clear, and actionable for everyone. Since launching in 2020 , Radar has found a natural audience amongst expert users with technical backgrounds, such as network operators and academic researchers who rely on Radar data to observe industry trends and global events. But Radar also has the potential to be a more regular, self-serve resource for journalists, human rights advocates and policymakers, and everyday users. Making Radar accessible to a wider audience is a core part of our vision, so we knew it was time to reevaluate the user experience: How could we make Radar more approachable to broader audiences, while preserving the depth and rigor that we currently have? In this blog post, we’ll introduce the redesigned Radar, share our design process, and outline our broader vision for the platform’s future. Challenges with the previous design While the previous landing page was successful at showing the breadth of Radar’s data, the way that information was presented made it difficult to parse. The bento box layout gave everything similar visual weight, the vertical cards disrupted natural reading patterns, and the styling felt disconnected from Cloudflare’s broader brand. We heard this from users. One external user mentioned that one of his biggest frustrations with Radar was that it was difficult to show to his students. That feedback reinforced the problem that Radar could feel intimidating to people encountering it for the first time. With this in mind, we wanted to create a landing page experience that shaped a clearer path through the content, instead of asking users to interpret everything at once. But making Radar more accessible wasn’t only about reorganizing the content. It was also about making the experience feel more connected to Cloudflare. For external users, consistency directly affects trust and usability. A more cohesive Cloudflare experience helps signal that Radar is an authoritative source, while familiar layout patterns reduce the effort required to move through complex information. Introducing Radar’s new landing page The new and improved Radar uses a visual reference that users of all backgrounds will have a familiarity with — a map. Even though in many ways the Internet operates outside the concept of national borders, its relevance to everyday life is often best understood through geography. The top section focuses on traffic and outages. We lead with outages as they are one of the most concrete, timely, and salient data points about the state of the Internet. Traffic, meanwhile, communicates the scale and breadth of Radar’s data. The map also acts as a navigation point into the insights below, guiding users from the global view into specific countries, trends, and data stories. Below the map, we turned the old bento box chart widgets into a tabbed flow that helps users move through the data more deliberately. The tabs provide summary statistics to give the user a sense of Radar’s capabilities, while also giving them a call to action to explore the data in depth. The tabbed structure creates a clearer hierarchy, keeps the page from feeling overwhelming, and previews the range of information Radar provides. From concept to reality Developing Radar’s design language To develop Radar’s design language, we looked across Cloudflare’s content ecosystem, which exist in these three pillars: Marketing pages (like cloudflare.com) Content experiences, like the Cloudflare Blog and Developer Docs Product experiences Where does Radar fit? Ultimately, we decided that Radar sits at the intersection of all three. At its core, Radar is a data product, but it’s also public-facing and inherently content-rich. The strategy blends design aspects of all three: approachable like marketing, structured and navigable like content, and maintainable by migrating to Kumo components used by Cloudflare’s product system. What we tried along the way From the outset, we knew the landing needed to communicate Radar’s global perspective. Because Radar helps people understand Internet activity across countries and regions, a map felt like the clearest way to make that scope immediately visible. The map went through many rounds of iteration as we tried to strike the right balance between data richness and approachability. Early versions were too dense — we were trying to convey credibility through data-heavy visuals. Later versions moved too far toward marketing-style visuals; they felt more approachable, but risked making Radar’s fresh data feel static or overly summarized. The current direction sits between those two poles: it has the feel of live data, but it’s easier to read. The Radar vision and evolution Because Cloudflare sits in front of roughly 20% of all web traffic, Radar has the opportunity to tell unique and compelling stories about the Internet. To unlock that value, we want to empower every user with real-time insights across security, performance, and traffic. This redesign is a foundational step in that direction. Moving forward, Radar will continue to focus on tools, visualizations, and storytelling frameworks that remove friction for new audiences while doubling down on our deep data infrastructure. By pairing our technical rigor with a cleaner, human-centered experience, we are ensuring that more people can explore, understand, and share the state of the Internet. Help us make Radar better This redesign is our first step toward a more intuitive, scannable Radar. But we're just getting started, and we need your input. What’s working? What isn’t? What insights are still hard to uncover? You can share feedback with us on social media at @CloudflareRadar on X, radar.cloudflare.com on Bluesky, or email us at radar@cloudflare.com .

Read full article →

Staying Evergreen: How we automated secure access and repo hygiene in a shared org

https://github.blog/engineering/platform-security/staying-evergreen-how-we-automated-secure-access-and-repo-hygiene-in-a-shared-org/📅 2026-10-16 02:00 UTC+8

GitHub built Evergreen, a GitHub App that automates quarterly access reviews for outside collaborators and archives stale repositories in a shared organization of over 1,400 employees. The system follows a "fail secure" principle where unresponded access reviews default to revocation, and all state is stored as JSON files in git so the commit history doubles as an audit trail. It matters because manual repo housekeeping becomes unmanageable at scale, and Evergreen shows how native GitHub features can be choreographed into a reliable, transparent automation pattern.

From 2,500 requests to 24: Rebuilding star history without the stargazers

https://github.blog/engineering/architecture-optimization/from-2500-requests-to-24-rebuilding-star-history-without-the-stargazers/📅 2026-10-15 03:11 UTC+8

GitHub has replaced its old stargazer API with a new endpoint that returns daily star counts filtered by calendar week, cutting the number of requests needed to reconstruct a repository's growth curve from roughly 2,500 down to about 24 for large projects like react/react. The change came after GitHub restricted access to individual stargazer lists to stop identity scraping and reduce database load, which broke popular tools like Star History and prompted a collaboration between GitHub engineers and the open-source community to design a purpose-built solution. The key engineering insight was filtering stars by date range before aggregating them, so the database processes each star roughly once instead of rescanning the entire set on every paginated request.

51 words

Improving site performance by shipping more CSS

https://github.blog/engineering/architecture-optimization/improving-site-performance-by-shipping-more-css/📅 2026-09-25 23:00 UTC+8

GitHub's Primer Design System team migrated from CSS-in-JS to CSS Modules to address growing performance problems caused by an explosion of components on pages, achieving a 55% reduction in server-side rendering time and a 25% drop in component initialization. The migration, which spanned from 2023 to 2026, required carefully removing thousands of inline sx props across the codebase using feature flags, wrapper components, and eventually AI-assisted tooling to handle the final stretch. The work culminated in the removal of styled-components, a library that had entered maintenance mode, though the team still faces the challenge of migrating GitHub's seven theme systems that remain dependent on it.

GitHub 嘅 Primer 設計系統團隊由 2023 年開始，將 CSS-in-JS 逐步遷移去 CSS Modules，解決咗因為頁面組件數量暴增而引致嘅效能問題，最終令伺服器端渲染時間減少�

119 words

Rendering huge pull requests in the GitHub Copilot app

https://github.blog/engineering/user-experience/rendering-huge-pull-requests-in-the-github-copilot-app/📅 2026-09-24 02:29 UTC+8

GitHub rebuilt the pull request view in the Copilot app to keep review conversations fast even on enormous diffs, testing against a pull request with 2,200 files, over a million changed lines, and more than 400 inline comments. The core solution was splitting the document's height into two independent geometry domains: a deterministic one for code rows whose heights are known before paint, and a separate one for review threads and other dynamic content whose heights can only be measured lazily after render. A batched, scroll-gated measurement scheduler replaced the naive per-block ResizeObserver approach, preventing the feedback loops that would otherwise cause jank and scroll jumps on large pull requests.

GitHub 喺 Copilot app 入面重新設計咗 pull request 嘅 review 介面，專門處理超大型 diff，測試用嗰個 PR

Read full article →

How we make AI coding more cost efficient without sacrificing task quality

https://github.blog/ai-and-ml/github-copilot/how-we-make-ai-coding-more-cost-efficient-without-sacrificing-task-quality/📅 2026-09-03 02:00 UTC+8

GitHub Copilot engineers found that reducing tokens per individual tool call often backfires, as agents end up repeating work and spending more tokens overall, so they shifted to optimizing for end-to-end task cost instead. They shipped four changes — selective output compression, removing unused line-number prefixes, compressing system prompts via a meta-prompting loop, and delivering background results without extra retrieval — each validated through offline benchmarks and online experiments before release. The work matters because it shows that naive token-saving strategies can increase costs, and that real efficiency requires measuring the full task rather than isolated interactions.

GitHub Copilot 嘅工程師發現，單單減少每次工具調用嘅 token 數量往往會弄巧反拙，因為 agent 會反覆重做嘢，結果反而用多咗 token，所以佢哋轉為以整個任務嘅總成本作為優化目標。佢哋推出咗四項改動，包括選擇性壓縮輸出

Your alt text passes automated checks. That doesn’t mean it’s any good.

https://github.blog/engineering/user-experience/your-alt-text-passes-automated-checks-that-doesnt-mean-its-any-good/📅 2026-08-25 04:56 UTC+8

Automated accessibility tools reliably flag missing alt text but cannot judge whether the text actually describes an image well, a gap that a new GitHub Accessibility Scanner plugin attempts to close using five deterministic rules for obvious failures and an opt-in vision model for contextual quality checks. The team deliberately chose to miss some bad alt text rather than risk false positives that would cause developers to switch off the checker entirely. A key technical insight was that detecting repeated alt text required analysing visual layout on screen rather than document order in the DOM, since images that appear adjacent in markup may be far apart visually.

GitHub has released the Copilot SDK for Java, a framework-agnostic and AI-vendor-neutral client library that lets enterprise Java developers build AI agent sessions directly in their server-side code without depending on Langchain4j or Spring AI. The SDK uses familiar Java idioms such as annotations, CompletableFuture, and virtual threads, and includes a @CopilotTool annotation that lets developers register tools the model can call with minimal boilerplate. This matters because it finally gives the Java ecosystem a native, standards-based path to AI integration that feels like home to long-time enterprise developers.

105 words

Don’t stop early: Case-folding source code at memory speed

https://github.blog/engineering/architecture-optimization/dont-stop-early-case-folding-source-code-at-memory-speed/📅 2026-08-01 00:00 UTC+8

GitHub engineers optimized case-folding for Blackbird, their code search engine that indexes over 480TB of source code, by discovering that removing all branches from the ASCII fast path—including the intuitive early-exit on non-ASCII bytes—allows the compiler to vectorize the loop and achieve over 45 GiB/s throughput, essentially memory bandwidth. The key technique replaces conditional logic with arithmetic: a bitwise OR accumulator detects non-ASCII bytes after the loop finishes, and a wrapping subtraction produces a branchless mask that folds uppercase letters by setting bit 5 unconditionally. The result, open-sourced as a Rust crate called casefold, demonstrates that at extreme scale, the branch itself is the enemy and counterintuitive choices like processing the entire buffer without stopping can be fifteen times faster than the obvious approach.

GitHub 嘅工程師為咗佢哋嘅代碼搜尋引擎 Blackbird 做咗一個 case

Read full article →

Previewing the Model Hardware Standard

https://www.anthropic.com/news/model-hardware-standard-research-preview📅 2026-08-28 02:00 UTC+8

Anthropic and HHMI Janelia Research Campus have opened a research preview of the Model Hardware Standard, a shared specification that lets AI agents safely operate multiple physical devices such as microscopes, liquid handlers, and robotic arms in parallel. The standard reduces hardware integration time from weeks or months down to hours or minutes by introducing a universal driver that makes any programmable device discoverable and controllable through simple read and write commands. Early partners including Genentech, Carnegie Mellon, and QuEra Computing have already used MHS to accelerate lab automation, run dose-response experiments three times faster, and stabilize lasers inside quantum computers.
