103 words

How UTS established a modern curriculum data services foundation on AWS

https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/architecture/how-uts-established-a-modern-curriculum-data-services-foundation-on-aws/📅 2026-10-14 22:29 UTC+8

<p>Students and staff experience the University of Technology Sydney (UTS) as a single institution, not as the collection of curriculum and student systems behind it. They expect the information underpinning teaching, learning, course design and institutional decision-making to be accurate, timely and coherent, irrespective of its source.</p> <p>Behind that experience, UTS had reached a natural inflection point in its integration landscape. Critical teaching and learning capabilities relied upon established vendor platforms with disparate data models, interfaces, stability profiles and scaling characteristics. Although those platforms continued to perform essential operational functions, integrating and operating the surrounding estate had become progressively more intricate and costly.</p> <p>UTS’s purpose remained constant: excellence in teaching, learning and the student experience. Technology exists to advance that purpose, not to supplant it. UTS Information Technology Unit (ITU) therefore sought to remove impediments between authoritative data and the people, processes and digital services that depend upon it.</p> <p>UTS made that transition practical with AWS. With managed, elastic and serverless capabilities, UTS reduced undifferentiated operational effort and improved fault tolerance. This established a sustainable model for governed data services. Curriculum became the first bounded business domain in which UTS applied that model.</p> <h2 id="the-challenge">The challenge</h2> <p>UTS ITU required an architecture that could:</p> <ul> <li>Make authoritative curriculum information available through reusable services without displacing the Curriculum Management System, a third-party software as a service (SaaS) application, as the system of record.</li> <li>Replace tightly coupled, consumer-specific interfaces with governed contracts that insulate consumers from source schemas and implementation detail.</li> <li>Accommodate routine change and transient high-volume activity, including complete domain reloads, without permanently provisioned capacity.</li> <li>Support graph traversal across deeply nested and inverse curriculum relationships, exact and full-text retrieval, and semantic discovery from the same authoritative source.</li> <li>Operate reliably and economically within finite engineering capacity whilst establishing a repeatable pattern for subsequent data domains.</li> </ul> <p>Domain boundaries were non-negotiable. Curriculum describes what the university teaches and remains the responsibility of the Curriculum Management System. Preserving that bounded context prevents ownership, validation and governance from migrating into non-authoritative services.</p> <h2 id="a-domain-oriented-approach">A domain-oriented approach</h2> <p>UTS ITU organised the capability according to domain-driven design. Rather than aligning data services with the applications that produce or consume data, they aligned them with:</p> <ul> <li>the <strong>business domains</strong> defined in CAUDIT’s <a href="https://www.caudit.edu.au/communities/caudit-higher-education-reference-models/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Higher Education Reference Model</a> (HERM).</li> <li>the <strong>information</strong> for which those domains are accountable.</li> </ul> <p>Each data product has explicit ownership, consumers, quality expectations, interface contracts and lifecycle management. Institutional data is therefore treated as a governed asset designed for reuse, not an incidental application by-product.</p> <p>Declarative architecture as code provides the executable realisation of the design and a reproducible source of truth. Configuration is separated from implementation where practicable, delivery is automated and managed AWS services are preferred to bespoke infrastructure software. The discipline is “just enough code”: engineering effort focuses on building differentiated domain logic (the business rules and data services unique to higher education) rather than undifferentiated infrastructure.</p> <p>Stateless execution, asynchronous communication, purpose-built persistence and independently recoverable components avoid a tightly coupled failure domain. Consumption-based services align expenditure with workload demand instead of maintaining an estate permanently sized for intermittent peaks.</p> <h2 id="solution-overview">Solution overview</h2> <p>The architecture comprises five layers: edge, ingestion, persistence, application and business logic, and interface. Each layer has discrete responsibilities, as shown in Figure 1.</p> <p>Identity, encryption, network controls, observability and operational governance apply across the stack. Curriculum data services capture complete changes from the Curriculum Management System as events, preserve them in a durable event log, materialise purpose-built representations and expose governed synchronous and asynchronous services.</p> <div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"> <a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/fc074d501302eb2b93e2554793fcaf50b3bf7291/2026/10/09/ARCHBLOG-1642-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"><img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/fc074d501302eb2b93e2554793fcaf50b3bf7291/2026/10/09/ARCHBLOG-1642-1.png" alt="Five-layer UTS curriculum data services architecture connecting the Curriculum Management System through the edge, ingestion, persistence, applications, and interface layers to consumers, with observability and security spanning all layers" width="800"></a> <p class="wp-caption-text">Figure 1: UTS curriculum data services layered architecture</p> </div> <p>The next five sections examine each layer of the realised architecture in turn.</p> <h2 id="edge-layer">Edge layer</h2> <p>The edge layer is the controlled ingress boundary between the SaaS Curriculum Management System and the curriculum domain. Its remit is deliberately narrow: receive a complete curriculum change, establish the internal event contract and submit it to the ingestion layer with minimal processing.</p> <p>The source system sends change events to an <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/api-gateway/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon API Gateway</a> HTTP API, which invokes an <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/lambda/latest/dg/welcome.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Lambda</a> function acting as a <em>technical adapter</em> because no suitable managed connector satisfies the source system’s proprietary API contract and payload format (for example, vendor-specific REST endpoints, authentication schemes, and data structures). The adapter encapsulates the complete vendor event in a CloudEvent envelope, appends UTS operational metadata and publishes it to the appropriate <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/msk/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Managed Streaming for Apache Kafka</a> (Amazon MSK) Serverless topic.</p> <p>The edge neither reinterprets curriculum nor performs validation or consumer-specific transformation. Validation remains within the system of record. This preserves source fidelity, prevents downstream concerns from contaminating the ingress boundary and allows the stateless edge to scale automatically during complete domain reloads.</p> <h2 id="ingestion-layer">Ingestion layer</h2> <p>Amazon MSK Serverless forms the durable, append-only event backbone. Curriculum changes are segregated into domain-aligned topics by academic item type, retaining the complete source event whilst allowing independent consumers to progress, retry and recover without obstructing one another.</p> <p>MSK Serverless scales on demand. Routine changes consume little capacity. A domain reload fans out across the event backbone and parallel processors, then contracts after completion. Model or schema changes can be accommodated through idempotent reprocessing or a complete reload, without introducing a second curriculum authority.</p> <h2 id="persistence-layer">Persistence layer</h2> <p>The persistence layer uses purpose-built data services selected according to the access patterns and business questions each representation must support. <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/neptune/features/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Neptune Serverless</a> stores curriculum structures and inverse relationships as a property graph, whilst <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/opensearch-service/latest/developerguide/what-is.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon OpenSearch Service</a> stores complete source documents and indices for exact, full-text and vector retrieval.</p> <p>In Neptune, programmes of learning, areas of learning and units of learning are connected academic items. Consumers can traverse from a programme to its constituent structures or begin with a unit and identify every programme and area through which it is taught. These inverse enquiries are native graph traversals rather than application-specific joins repeatedly reconstructed by consumers.</p> <div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"> <a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/fc074d501302eb2b93e2554793fcaf50b3bf7291/2026/10/09/ARCHBLOG-1642-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"><img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/fc074d501302eb2b93e2554793fcaf50b3bf7291/2026/10/09/ARCHBLOG-1642-2.png" alt="Property graph in Amazon Neptune showing programme, major, and subject nodes connected by TEACHES and IS_TAUGHT_BY relationships" width="800"></a> <p class="wp-caption-text">Figure 2: Curriculum structures relationships in Amazon Neptune</p> </div> <p>OpenSearch provides exact lookup, filtering and document retrieval alongside k-nearest-neighbour vector search. Selected data processors implemented as AWS Lambda functions invoke an embedding model through <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/what-is-bedrock.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Bedrock</a> and append vector representations of relevant curriculum text before persistence. Curriculum can consequently be discovered by semantic proximity rather than lexical coincidence. UTS also validated multilingual retrieval against English-language curriculum content.</p> <p>Neptune and OpenSearch are independently and eventually consistent. Failure of one materialisation doesn’t block another, and neither store is exposed directly to consumers. Both are disposable <a href="https://event-driven.io/en/projections_and_read_models_in_event_driven_architecture/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">projections</a> rather than competing systems of record and can be reconstructed from the Curriculum Management System through a complete reload. Data is encrypted with a dedicated domain <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/kms/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Key Management Service</a> (AWS KMS) key whose use is restricted to authorised domain services.</p> <h2 id="applications-layer">Applications layer</h2> <p>The applications layer converts those materialisations into reusable domain capabilities. Dozens of narrowly scoped Lambda-backed services query Neptune or OpenSearch, whilst persistence remains private and is exposed solely through governed contracts.</p> <p>Amazon EventBridge Pipes acts as a set of transport adapters between MSK Serverless and the Lambda functions acting as processors and services consuming the event stream. Pipes provide routing and delivery, not curriculum validation, enrichment or business transformation, keeping processing responsibilities explicit, independently deployable and observable.</p> <p>Stateless Lambda function execution allows services to scale with demand and recover independently through retries and re-invocation. Signature Version 4 (SigV4) authentication and fine-grained AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) roles provide least-privilege access to Amazon Neptune and Amazon OpenSearch Service without distributing or managing database credentials. The services provide authoritative retrieval, graph traversal and semantic discovery without transferring responsibility for curriculum validity into the data domain.</p> <h2 id="interface-layer">Interface layer</h2> <p>The interface layer provides pull-based access through REST and GraphQL and push-based delivery of domain events, whilst keeping persistence and implementation private. Amazon API Gateway supplies the REST boundary. AWS AppSync provides GraphQL access to frequently consumed capabilities with attribute-based access control at schema level. Both interfaces invoke the same governed service contracts.</p> <p>Approved consumers might also receive curriculum events in the internal CloudEvent envelope. Amazon EventBridge Pipes manages last-mile delivery without bespoke routing middleware, and consumers determine which academic item events are relevant rather than UTS maintaining a filtered stream for each consumer.</p> <p><a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cognito/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Cognito</a> authenticates approved external consumers through OAuth 2.0 and OpenID Connect, whilst AWS native workloads use IAM. AWS Key Management Service, AWS Secrets Manager, AWS WAF, and AWS Certificate Manager provide encryption, secrets management, perimeter protection and certificate lifecycle management. <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/route53/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Route 53</a> supplies naming for a single shared vanity domain, and <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cloudwatch/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon CloudWatch</a> and <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sns/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Simple Notification Service (Amazon SNS)</a> provide observability, telemetry and targeted alerting across the architecture.</p> <h2 id="full-solution-architecture">Full solution architecture</h2> <p>The following diagram shows the complete solution architecture, including the identity, encryption, network, observability and governance controls that apply across all five layers.</p> <div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"> <a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/fc074d501302eb2b93e2554793fcaf50b3bf7291/2026/10/09/ARCHBLOG-1642-3.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"><img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/fc074d501302eb2b93e2554793fcaf50b3bf7291/2026/10/09/ARCHBLOG-1642-3.png" alt="End-to-end architecture of the UTS curriculum data services solution across the edge, ingestion, persistence, application, and interface layers, with the security, operational, and ancillary services that span them" width="800"></a> <p class="wp-caption-text">Figure 3: End-to-end architecture of the curriculum data services solution</p> </div> <h2 id="results-and-measurable-outcomes">Results and measurable outcomes</h2> <p>The outcome isn’t a replacement for the Curriculum Management System as the system of record. It’s a governed data foundation that leaves the system of record authoritative whilst making curriculum information reusable through APIs, graph traversal, semantic search and event delivery. Since the production launch in November 2024, the domain has recorded zero production incidents.</p> <ul> <li>Latency – Changes become available to downstream consumers in an average of 1.6 seconds from crossing the edge layer, through synchronous and asynchronous interfaces.</li> <li>Curriculum graph – The domain models 43,794 relationships across approximately 800 programs of learning, 3,500 units of learning and 2,500 areas of learning.</li> <li>Consumer adoption – 11 institutional consumers have adopted the services, replacing bespoke point-to-point integrations with reusable, governed contracts.</li> <li>Operational footprint – One engineer operates the domain. Managed, serverless and self-healing components make routine intervention negligible and remove the need for dedicated Level 3 support.</li> <li>Cost efficiency – UTS achieved a material reduction in the total cost of ownership of curriculum data integration and delivery, consolidating capabilities previously spread across commercial products, bespoke integrations, databases and multiple support teams.</li> <li>Availability and resilience – The platform operates continuously and has maintained downstream access to trusted curriculum information when the upstream system of record was unavailable.</li> </ul> <p>Collectively, these outcomes show how a small engineering contingent can operate an institutional-scale data domain using managed AWS services, whilst increasing capability, reducing operational complexity and aligning expenditure with demand.</p> <h2 id="whats-next">What’s next</h2> <p>The curriculum domain validated the production architecture and operating model. The student data domain followed, providing a single governed point of access for student profiles, admissions, offers, enrolments and related information. UTS is now preparing the learning activities domain, a substantially larger capability built around a lifelong learner graph using Amazon Neptune and Amazon Neptune Analytics, extending the same domain-driven pattern across the teaching and learning landscape.</p> <p>Each bounded context can select the AWS services appropriate to its business questions whilst retaining common controls for identity, security, observability, delivery and operational sustainability.</p> <h2 id="conclusion">Conclusion</h2> <p>UTS’s integration landscape had reached the point at which cumulative complexity and operational overhead constrained institutional change. The response was not technology for its own sake. UTS ITU used AWS to remove the impediments between authoritative institutional data and the teaching, learning and administrative capabilities that depend upon it.</p> <p>The significance of AWS extended beyond technology selection, but the outcome was principally an architectural and operating-model achievement by UTS. Curriculum data services became UTS’s first production data domain. UTS combined domain-driven design, asynchronous processing, purpose-built persistence and governed interfaces with managed AWS capabilities that made those patterns practicable at institutional scale.</p> <p>The resulting service foundation supports event streaming, graph traversal, document and semantic retrieval, compute, identity, security and governed data exposure. UTS operates it without a conventional infrastructure estate. This allowed UTS ITU to convert an ambitious architectural design into a sustainable production capability whilst keeping engineering effort concentrated on domain services rather than undifferentiated infrastructure.</p> <p>Curriculum data services embody the resulting transition: from a burdensome integration model to a domain-aligned data foundation that’s reusable, resilient and operationally sustainable. UTS now possesses a repeatable model through which institutional data can be treated as a strategic asset rather than a by-product of individual systems. The university will continue to operate many systems. Students and staff should never experience the boundaries between them.</p> <p>For more information or support, contact the <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/government-education/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Public Sector team</a>.</p> <p style="clear: both"></p> <hr style="width: 100%"> <h2>About the authors</h2>

Read full article →

Scope workloads and share resilience policies with AWS Resilience Hub

https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/architecture/scope-workloads-and-share-resilience-policies-with-aws-resilience-hub/📅 2026-10-14 05:30 UTC+8

<p>A resilience plan is useful only while it reflects the application you run. After a migration or deployment, a service can acquire a new dependency while its documented recovery objectives stay the same. In a shared Kubernetes cluster, even deciding which resources belong to that service can take investigation.</p> <p>On September 18, 2026, the Next generation of <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/resilience-hub/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Resilience Hub</a> introduced three capabilities to help you keep workload scope and resilience requirements aligned with your running environment:</p> <ul> <li><strong><a href="https://aws.amazon.com/eks/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Elastic Kubernetes Service (Amazon EKS)</a> label support for service input sources</strong> selects Kubernetes resources using the labels that already describe your workloads.</li> <li><strong>Generative AI-powered Dependency Insights</strong> analyzes a service’s discovered dependencies and highlights the patterns worth reviewing.</li> <li><strong>Resilience policy sharing through <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/organizations/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Organizations</a></strong> lets a central team define resilience requirements once and make them available to member accounts.</li> </ul> <p>In this post, you follow a team reviewing the resilience of a sample workload named checkout on Amazon EKS, representing an online store’s checkout service. The team starts by defining which Kubernetes resources belong to checkout, using labels to distinguish it from inventory in the same namespace. This establishes the resource boundary for the resilience review.</p> <p>With that boundary defined, the team examines discovered dependencies and uses Dependency Insights to prioritize findings for investigation. A cross-Region dependency becomes a concrete question about whether checkout’s recovery design accounts for the services it requires to operate. The service owner then works with a central resilience team to validate the recovery requirements and apply a shared policy. Maintaining those requirements centrally gives teams a common definition to adopt across accounts, while policy events help the central team track adoption.</p> <p>You perform these steps using the console or the AWS Command Line Interface (<a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cli/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS CLI</a>). The workload generates synthetic traffic to demonstrate dependency discovery.</p> <p><strong>About the application model:</strong> In the Next generation of Resilience Hub, the primary unit of assessment is a <em>service</em>. Services can be grouped into <em>systems</em>, and <em>user journeys</em> describe paths through them. See <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/resilience-hub/latest/userguide/next-gen-what-changes.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">the key changes</a> and <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/resilience-hub/latest/userguide/next-gen-modeling-overview.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">the overview of the new hierarchy</a> in the Next generation of Resilience Hub.</p> <h2 id="prerequisites">Prerequisites</h2> <p>To follow the examples, you need:</p> <ul> <li>Access to the Next generation of AWS Resilience Hub in a <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/resilience-hub/latest/userguide/next-gen-supported-regions.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">supported AWS Region</a>.</li> <li>An Amazon EKS cluster with sample, labeled workloads and permission to configure access to the cluster.</li> <li>If you follow the CLI examples, install or update the AWS CLI using the <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cli/latest/userguide/getting-started-install.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">installation and update instructions</a>. Your installation must support the resiliencehubv2 commands. These examples use Bash.</li> <li>An AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) role that Resilience Hub can assume, with the <code>AWSResilienceHubV2AssessmentExecutionPolicy</code> managed policy attached. The console can create this role when you create a service.</li> <li>Kubernetes RBAC permissions and an EKS access entry that grant this role access to Kubernetes resources. The <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/resilience-hub/latest/userguide/next-gen-iam-permissions.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">permissions guide</a> provides the manifest and commands.</li> <li>For policy sharing, an organization with all features enabled and access to its management account.</li> </ul> <p>The examples use two accounts: a workload account containing the EKS cluster and Resilience Hub service, and a management account that owns the shared policy.</p> <p>For the CLI examples, configure profiles named <code>workload</code> and <code>management</code> for the respective accounts. Replace the example cluster ARN and other placeholders with your own values, and set the Region:</p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-bash">export AWS_REGION=us-east-1</code></pre> </div> <p>Each command uses <code>--profile</code> to select the account. Follow either the console steps or their CLI equivalents for each operation.</p> <h2 id="amazon-eks-label-support-for-service-input-sources">Amazon EKS label support for service input sources</h2> <p>A <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/resilience-hub/latest/userguide/next-gen-add-input-source.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">service input source</a> tells Resilience Hub where to discover a service’s resources. Input sources can be AWS CloudFormation stacks, Terraform state files, resource tags, or Amazon EKS clusters. For an EKS input source, you select the namespaces to include. With this launch, you can also use Kubernetes label selectors to filter resources within those namespaces.</p> <p>A namespace can contain workloads owned by different teams. With labels, you can select checkout’s resources without including inventory or reorganizing the namespace for an assessment. You use the same labels your teams already maintain.</p> <h3 id="how-it-works">How it works</h3> <p>Specify the cluster and one or more namespaces, then add label selectors to filter resources within each namespace. A selector contains a key, an operator, and values. Selectors within a namespace use <code>AND</code> logic: an object must match all of them. Without a selector, Resilience Hub discovers every supported object in the selected namespaces.</p> <p>Checkout and inventory share the <code>production</code> namespace. Their resources carry <code>app=checkout</code> and <code>app=inventory</code>, respectively. Selecting <code>production</code> with <code>app In checkout</code> includes checkout’s Deployment and ReplicaSet and excludes inventory.</p> <div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"> <a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/fc074d501302eb2b93e2554793fcaf50b3bf7291/2026/10/13/ARCHBLOG-1735-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"><img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/fc074d501302eb2b93e2554793fcaf50b3bf7291/2026/10/13/ARCHBLOG-1735-1.png" alt="A label selector chooses checkout resources from a namespace shared with inventory" width="800"></a> <p class="wp-caption-text">Figure 1: Selecting checkout resources with a Kubernetes label</p> </div> <p>The resource list also includes supporting AWS infrastructure, such as the cluster, node group, virtual private cloud (VPC), and subnets. Labels select Kubernetes objects. They don’t make that shared infrastructure exclusive to checkout.</p> <h3 id="how-to-use-it">How to use it</h3> <p>In the Resilience Hub console:</p> <ol type="1"> <li>Choose <strong>Create service</strong> and enter a name. Under <strong>Permission</strong>, choose <strong>Create default role</strong> to create an IAM role with the required AWS permissions, or choose <strong>Use another role</strong> to select an existing role.</li> <li>Under <strong>Service resource discovery</strong>, choose <strong>Amazon EKS cluster</strong> and enter <a href="https://console.aws.amazon.com/eks/clusters" target="_blank" rel="noopener">the cluster ARN</a>.</li> <li>Choose <strong>Configure namespaces</strong>, enter <code>production</code>, and add a label selector with key <code>app</code>, operator <code>In</code>, and value <code>checkout</code>.</li> <li>Choose <strong>Save</strong>, select <strong>Enable dependency discovery for this service</strong>, then choose <strong>Create service</strong>. Resource discovery starts automatically.</li> </ol> <div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"> <a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/fc074d501302eb2b93e2554793fcaf50b3bf7291/2026/10/13/ARCHBLOG-1735-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"><img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/fc074d501302eb2b93e2554793fcaf50b3bf7291/2026/10/13/ARCHBLOG-1735-2.png" alt="Configure namespaces dialog selecting production with app In checkout" width="800"></a> <p class="wp-caption-text">Figure 2: Scoping the checkout service to one namespace and one label</p> </div> <p>If you prefer to use the AWS CLI instead of the console, run the following commands to create the service and add its EKS input source.</p> <blockquote> <p><strong>Skip this block if you already created the service</strong> using the preceding console steps.</p> </blockquote> <p>The console displays selector operators as <code>In</code>, <code>NotIn</code>, <code>Exists</code>, and <code>NotExists</code>. In the API, use <code>IN</code>, <code>NOT_IN</code>, <code>EXISTS</code>, and <code>DOES_NOT_EXIST</code>, respectively.</p> <p>In the following command, replace <code>ResilienceHubRole</code> with your service’s IAM role name.</p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-bash"># Create the checkout service with dependency discovery enabled and save its ARN SERVICE_ARN=$(aws resiliencehubv2 create-service \ --profile workload \ --name checkout \ --regions "$AWS_REGION" \ --permission-model '{"invokerRoleName": "ResilienceHubRole"}' \ --dependency-discovery ENABLED \ --query 'service.serviceArn' --output text) # Add the EKS input source, selecting resources labeled app=checkout in production aws resiliencehubv2 create-input-source \ --profile workload \ --service-arn "$SERVICE_ARN" \ --resource-configuration '{ "eks": { "clusterArn": "arn:aws:eks:us-east-1:111122223333:cluster/resilience-demo", "namespaces": ["production"], "labelSelector": { "matchExpressions": [ {"key": "app", "operator": "IN", "values": ["checkout"]} ] } } }'</code></pre> </div> <p>After resource discovery completes, you can use the following command to list the discovered resource types. If you created the service on the console, first set <code>SERVICE_ARN</code> to that service’s ARN.</p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-bash"># List discovered resource types after resource discovery completes aws resiliencehubv2 list-resources \ --profile workload \ --service-arn "$SERVICE_ARN" \ --query 'serviceResources[].resource.resourceType'</code></pre> </div> <h2 id="generative-ai-powered-dependency-insights">Generative AI-powered Dependency Insights</h2> <p>Dependency Insights uses generative AI to summarize discovered dependencies and highlight patterns that warrant resilience review. It provides an overview and categorized findings.</p> <p>The <strong>Resources</strong> list describes the service’s components. The <strong>Dependencies</strong> list shows endpoints discovered through DNS activity, including AWS service endpoints in other Regions and external APIs. When that list grows to hundreds of entries, Dependency Insights helps you prioritize what to investigate.</p> <h3 id="how-it-works-1">How it works</h3> <p>Dependency Insights builds on <em>dependency discovery</em>, an optional, <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/resilience-hub/pricing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">separately priced</a> feature. Dependency discovery analyzes DNS activity through <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/route53/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Route 53 Resolver</a> and does not require installing an agent in your workload. It examines up to 35 days of available history and continues monitoring for changes. The dependency list shows each endpoint’s name, location, and when it was first and last seen.</p> <p>Dependency Insights analyzes this list, filters out common service activity that typically does not indicate resilience risk, and highlights patterns such as these:</p> <table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"> <tbody> <tr> <td><strong>Pattern</strong></td> <td><strong>What to investigate</strong></td> </tr> <tr> <td>Cross-Region dependencies</td> <td>Whether a call to another Region is intentional, and how it affects recovery</td> </tr> <tr> <td>New dependencies</td> <td>Whether an endpoint first observed within the last seven days changes the documented architecture</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Third-party dependencies</td> <td>How the service behaves when an external provider is unavailable</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Uneven usage</td> <td>Whether activity reflects a scheduled job or a dependency used only at certain times</td> </tr> </tbody> </table> <div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"> <a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/fc074d501302eb2b93e2554793fcaf50b3bf7291/2026/10/13/ARCHBLOG-1735-3.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"><img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/fc074d501302eb2b93e2554793fcaf50b3bf7291/2026/10/13/ARCHBLOG-1735-3.png" alt="Discovered dependency data feeds a generated overview and findings that a service owner reviews" width="800"></a> <p class="wp-caption-text">Figure 3: Turning dependency data into a focused review</p> </div> <p>Insights prioritize review. They don’t prescribe fixes or list every dependency. Use the full dependency list when you need detail. A new workload’s insights reflect only the history it has generated.</p> <h3 id="how-to-use-it-1">How to use it</h3> <p>In the Resilience Hub console, wait for initial dependency discovery to complete before generating insights:</p> <ol type="1"> <li>Open the service. On the <strong>Assessment</strong> tab, scroll to the <strong>Dependencies</strong> section to review discovered dependencies.</li> <li>Choose <strong>Generate insights</strong>.</li> <li>Read the overview and compare each finding with the intended architecture.</li> </ol> <blockquote> <p>You can regenerate insights once every 24 hours. This limit applies whether you generate them through the console or the AWS CLI.</p> </blockquote> <div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"> <a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/fc074d501302eb2b93e2554793fcaf50b3bf7291/2026/10/13/ARCHBLOG-1735-4.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"><img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/fc074d501302eb2b93e2554793fcaf50b3bf7291/2026/10/13/ARCHBLOG-1735-4.png" alt="Generated Insights overview and categorized findings for the checkout service" width="800"></a> <p class="wp-caption-text">Figure 4: Dependency Insights for the checkout service</p> </div> <p>If you prefer the AWS CLI, use the following commands instead of the console steps. Set <code>SERVICE_ARN</code> to your service’s ARN.</p> <p>Enable dependency discovery only if it is not already enabled. After initial discovery completes, review the dependency list and generate insights. If you already generated insights on the console, skip <code>start-dependency-insights</code> and use <code>get-dependency-insights</code> to retrieve the existing result.</p> <p>Generation can complete asynchronously. Run <code>get-dependency-insights</code> again if the returned status is <code>IN_PROGRESS</code>.</p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-bash"># Enable dependency discovery if it is not already enabled aws resiliencehubv2 update-service \ --profile workload \ --service-arn "$SERVICE_ARN" \ --dependency-discovery ENABLED # Review the dependency list aws resiliencehubv2 list-dependencies \ --profile workload \ --service-arn "$SERVICE_ARN" \ --query 'dependencySummaries[].[dependencyName, location, dependencyId]' \ --output table # Start generating dependency insights aws resiliencehubv2 start-dependency-insights \ --profile workload \ --service-arn "$SERVICE_ARN" # Retrieve insights; if status is IN_PROGRESS, run this command again later aws resiliencehubv2 get-dependency-insights \ --profile workload \ --service-arn "$SERVICE_ARN"</code></pre> </div> <p>The checkout service produced five insights across 29 dependencies. The following response shows the generated overview and all five findings:</p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-json">{ "overview": "5 insights generated for service 'checkout' across 29 dependencies, surfacing cross-region, third-party, and uneven usage patterns.", "insights": [ { "category": "CROSS_REGION", "description": "DynamoDB in us-west-2 is reached cross-region with hard criticality, coupling this service's availability to a remote region. This dependency carries medium DNS query volume with consistent usage." }, { "category": "CROSS_REGION", "description": "PagerDuty (api.pagerduty.com) is reached cross-region to us-west-2, creating an availability dependency on that remote region." }, { "category": "THIRD_PARTY", "description": "Three third-party dependencies — example.com, example.net, and example.org — are present at medium DNS query volume with consistent usage patterns." }, { "category": "THIRD_PARTY", "description": "GitHub is a third-party dependency with low DNS query volume." }, { "category": "UNEVEN_USAGE", "description": "Two in-region dependencies show highly uneven daily usage: an S3 bucket (amzn-s3-demo-bucket) and an ECR registry (602401143452.dkr.ecr.us-east-1.amazonaws.com)." } ], "status": "COMPLETED", "createdAt": "2026-10-08T14:24:27.372000-05:00" }</code></pre> </div> <p>The first finding identifies a cross-Region Amazon DynamoDB dependency. If checkout requires that dependency to accept orders, a disruption in the remote Region could prevent it from operating even when its Amazon EKS resources remain healthy. The service owner should verify which operations require the table and review how those operations behave when it is unavailable. That review determines whether the dependency fits the recovery design or whether the architecture needs to change. DNS activity alone cannot answer that question.</p> <p>The uneven-usage finding points to container image infrastructure. Image downloads during pod startup can produce bursts of DNS activity. This is a pattern to investigate, rather than evidence that your application has an outage.</p> <p>Classify a dependency as hard if the service fails without it, or soft if the service degrades but keeps running. In the sample environment, we had classified the DynamoDB dependency as hard using the following command. The comment describes an illustrative failure assumption, which requires validation through testing:</p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-bash"># Classify the selected dependency as hard and record the reason aws resiliencehubv2 update-dependency \ --profile workload \ --service-arn "$SERVICE_ARN" \ --dependency-id "&lt;dependency-id&gt;" \ --criticality HARD \ --comment "Checkout writes orders to this table and cannot accept orders without it."</code></pre> </div> <h2 id="resilience-policy-sharing-through-aws-organizations">Resilience policy sharing through AWS Organizations</h2> <p>After reviewing checkout’s dependencies, the service owner can validate its recovery requirements with the business and the central site reliability engineering (SRE) team. Dependency findings inform that discussion. They don’t automatically determine the policy targets.</p> <p>The central SRE team can codify the agreed requirements into a resilience policy, defining availability and recovery targets for critical services. The management account or a delegated administrator can share that policy across the organization so service owners can apply the centrally maintained requirements without creating separate copies.</p> <p>The policy owner can review attachment and detachment events to track adoption. If a service stops using the policy, the SRE team can engage its owner to understand why and determine whether different requirements are appropriate. Application owners remain responsible for meeting the targets.</p> <h3 id="how-it-works-2">How it works</h3> <p>A policy can combine four target types: availability, recovery from an Availability Zone disruption, recovery from a Regional disruption, and data recovery. The recovery time objective (RTO) specifies the targeted recovery time. The recovery point objective (RPO) specifies the tolerable period of data loss.</p> <p>When sharing is on, the policy appears in every member account alongside the account’s own policies, and its ARN shows which account owns it. Each service owner chooses whether to apply it. If a requirement must apply regardless of that choice, associate the policy with a system containing the relevant services instead, as described in <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/resilience-hub/latest/userguide/next-gen-org-policies.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Applying resilience policies across accounts</a>.</p> <p>The owning account can review a timeline of the services that start or stop using the policy. Turning sharing off stops evaluating member services against the policy and removes it from the services that used it, so coordinate changes with the teams involved.</p> <div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"> <a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/fc074d501302eb2b93e2554793fcaf50b3bf7291/2026/10/13/ARCHBLOG-1735-5.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"><img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/fc074d501302eb2b93e2554793fcaf50b3bf7291/2026/10/13/ARCHBLOG-1735-5.png" alt="A centrally owned resilience policy is shared with service owners in two member accounts" width="800"></a> <p class="wp-caption-text">Figure 5: One policy definition, shared with member accounts</p> </div> <h3 id="how-to-use-it-2">How to use it</h3> <p>The organization integration is a one-time setup in the management account:</p> <ol type="1"> <li>In the Resilience Hub console, open <strong>AWS Organizations settings</strong>.</li> <li>Select <strong>Enable trusted access and create a service-linked role</strong>, then choose <strong>Create integration</strong>.</li> </ol> <p>To confirm that the service-linked role exists in a member account:</p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-bash"># Confirm the service-linked role exists in the workload account aws iam get-role \ --profile workload \ --role-name AWSServiceRoleForResilienceHub \ --query 'Role.Arn'</code></pre> </div> <p>Next, create the policy in the management account with sharing turned on. Policy names can contain letters, numbers, hyphens, and underscores, but no spaces. The following targets are examples. Agree on real values with the business and service owners. This example uses an availability target of 99.9 percent.</p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-bash"># Create an organization-shared resilience policy and save its ARN POLICY_ARN=$(aws resiliencehubv2 create-policy \ --profile management \ --name tier-1-critical \ --availability-slo '{"target": 99.9}' \ --multi-az '{"rtoInMinutes": 5, "rpoInMinutes": 1, "disasterRecoveryApproach": "ACTIVE_ACTIVE"}' \ --multi-region '{"rtoInMinutes": 15, "rpoInMinutes": 5, "disasterRecoveryApproach": "WARM_STANDBY"}' \ --data-recovery '{"timeBetweenBackupsInMinutes": 60}' \ --sharing-enabled \ --query 'policy.policyArn' --output text)</code></pre> </div> <p>In the workload account, find the shared policy and apply it to the service. On the console, choose <strong>Change policy</strong> on the service and select the shared policy. With the AWS CLI:</p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-bash"># List policies available in the workload account, including shared policies aws resiliencehubv2 list-policies \ --profile workload \ --query 'policySummaries[].[name, policyArn, sharingEnabled]' \ --output table # Apply the shared resilience policy to the checkout service aws resiliencehubv2 update-service \ --profile workload \ --service-arn "$SERVICE_ARN" \ --policy-arn "&lt;shared-policy-arn&gt;"</code></pre> </div> <div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"> <a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/fc074d501302eb2b93e2554793fcaf50b3bf7291/2026/10/13/ARCHBLOG-1735-6.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"><img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/fc074d501302eb2b93e2554793fcaf50b3bf7291/2026/10/13/ARCHBLOG-1735-6.png" alt="Lifecycle panel showing Policy associated and four targets pending assessment" width="800"></a> <p class="wp-caption-text">Figure 6: Policy targets are defined. Assessment of those targets is still pending</p> </div> <p>Back in the management account, the policy owner can confirm adoption:</p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-bash"># Review service attachment events for the shared policy aws resiliencehubv2 list-policy-events \ --profile management \ --policy-arn "$POLICY_ARN" \ --event-types POLICY_ATTACHED_TO_SERVICE</code></pre> </div> <p>An attachment event confirms adoption, not compliance. After we applied the shared policy, the console changed each target from <strong>Not defined in policy</strong> to <strong>Pending failure mode assessment</strong>. A failure mode assessment and appropriate resilience testing are needed to determine whether the service meets those targets.</p> <h2 id="things-to-know">Things to know</h2> <ul> <li><strong>Shared clusters.</strong> Labels decide which Kubernetes objects belong to a service. On a cluster that several workloads share, review the discovered dependencies to confirm they belong to the service, as described in the <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/resilience-hub/latest/userguide/next-gen-troubleshoot-k8s-attribution.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">guidance for shared EKS clusters</a>.</li> <li><strong>What DNS-based discovery can’t see.</strong> Connections made directly by IP address aren’t discovered, AWS Lambda functions need VPC connectivity to be covered, and very infrequent calls might not appear in initial results. See <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/resilience-hub/latest/userguide/next-gen-discovery-limitations.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Coverage and known limitations</a>.</li> <li><strong>Timing.</strong> Results reflect the history available to the service. Give a new workload time to build history before drawing conclusions from its insights. You can regenerate insights once every 24 hours.</li> </ul> <h2 id="cleanup">Cleanup</h2> <p>If you created resources to try these capabilities, remove them when you finish. Turning sharing off also removes the policy from every member service that uses it, so only do this for a demonstration policy:</p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-bash"># Stop sharing the demo policy; this also removes it from member services aws resiliencehubv2 update-policy --profile management --policy-arn "$POLICY_ARN" --no-sharing-enabled # Delete the demo policy aws resiliencehubv2 delete-policy --profile management --policy-arn "$POLICY_ARN" # Delete the demo Resilience Hub service aws resiliencehubv2 delete-service --profile workload --service-arn "$SERVICE_ARN"</code></pre> </div> <p>Delete the EKS cluster as well if you created it only for this walkthrough.</p> <h2 id="conclusion">Conclusion</h2> <p>Start with a service whose architecture and ownership you know. Use EKS labels to check its Kubernetes resource boundary, then review Dependency Insights against its intended behavior. If several teams maintain the same requirements separately, consider a shared policy.</p> <p>You can use the capabilities together or independently. Label support applies to EKS workloads. Dependency Insights and policy sharing also support services beyond EKS. Insights focus the investigation. Your architecture review determines what to change.</p> <h2 id="further-reading">Further reading</h2> <ul> <li><a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/resilience-hub/latest/userguide/next-gen-what-changes.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">What changes with Next generation Resilience Hub</a></li> <li><a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/resilience-hub/latest/userguide/next-gen-viewing-dependencies.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Viewing discovered dependencies</a></li> <li><a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/resilience-hub/latest/userguide/next-gen-insights-considerations.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Dependency Insights considerations</a></li> <li><a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/resilience-hub/latest/userguide/next-gen-policy-sharing.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Sharing a policy across your organization</a></li> <li><a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/resilience-hub/latest/userguide/next-gen-delegated-admin-setup.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Setting up Organizations integration</a></li> </ul> <p style="clear: both"></p> <hr style="width: 100%"> <h2>About the author</h2>

Stopping business email compromise with AI-driven email security: How VIPRE protects modern enterprises on AWS

https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/architecture/stopping-business-email-compromise-with-ai-driven-email-security-how-vipre-protects-modern-enterprises-on-aws/📅 2026-10-13 22:32 UTC+8

VIPRE has developed an AI-powered Integrated Email Security solution built on AWS that uses machine learning models, including custom BERT models with NVIDIA GPUs, to detect and block business email compromise attempts that traditional gateways miss. The solution integrates natively with Microsoft 365 through secure APIs without requiring MX record changes, processes over half a million messages daily, and employs semantic analysis, contextual language models, and dynamic behavioral analysis to catch sophisticated impersonation and social engineering attacks. This matters because nearly half of all spam emails are now BEC scams targeting executives and IT staff, and legacy email gateways remain blind to internal traffic and thread hijacks.

VIPRE 喺 AWS 上面開發咗一套用 AI 同機器學習嘅電郵安全方案，專門對付傳統防火牆擋唔到嘅商業電郵詐騙，佢直接接入 Microsoft 365 唔使改 MX

Read full article →

Bulk Executor for Amazon DynamoDB, Part 2: Lessons we learned the hard way

https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/architecture/bulk-executor-for-amazon-dynamodb-part-2-lessons-we-learned-the-hard-way/📅 2026-10-13 22:23 UTC+8

<p>In the <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/database/introducing-open-source-bulk-executor-for-amazon-dynamodb/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Introducing open source Bulk Executor for Amazon DynamoDB</a> blog post, you learned about the purpose and usage of the <em>Bulk Executor </em>tool. In this Part 2 post, we share the story of how we built it: the design tradeoffs, surprises, gotchas, and eventual workarounds. Think of it as your travel guide for working with Amazon DynamoDB and AWS Glue.</p> <h2 id="design-requirements">Design requirements</h2> <p><em>Bulk Executor</em> is a project designed to run actions in bulk against many or all items in a table. For example you can mass delete old items or compare two tables for equality.</p> <p>Three core requirements shaped most of the decisions that follow: running a bulk command should feel like a local script (real-time output, interruptible with <code>^C</code>), it should scale to hundreds of parallel workers without the user managing infrastructure, and it should be straightforward to add new commands without tight coupling to the framework.</p> <h2 id="selecting-aws-glue">Selecting AWS Glue</h2> <p>We selected AWS Glue to drive the parallel execution. It’s a serverless service where you submit <em>jobs</em> that get distributed across <em>workers</em> (compute instances), each running <em>tasks</em> in parallel. For more information, see <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/prescriptive-guidance/latest/tuning-aws-glue-for-apache-spark/key-topics-apache-spark.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Key topics in Apache Spark</a>. Our command-line client starts a job and AWS Glue handles distributing the work across potentially hundreds of workers.</p> <p>We also considered Amazon Simple Queue Service (Amazon SQS) with AWS Lambda, AWS Fargate, Amazon EMR, and Ray. We decided to use AWS Glue because it gave us both serverless parallelism and a built-in DynamoDB connector, so we got much of the hard stuff at no cost. The following short code sample shows how to read or write items:</p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-python"># Read from DynamoDB into a Spark DataFrame dataframe = ( spark.read .format("dynamodb") .option("dynamodb.input.tableName", "source") .option("dynamodb.throughput.read", "40000") .option("dynamodb.consistentRead", "false") .load() ) # Write to DynamoDB from a Spark DataFrame ( dataframe.write .format("dynamodb") .option("dynamodb.output.tableName", "sink") .option("dynamodb.throughput.write", "40000") .option("dynamodb.item.size.check.enabled", "true") .save() )</code></pre> </div> <p>The Spark DataFrame gave us the built-in capabilities that we needed. Specifically:</p> <ul> <li>A <a href="https://spark.apache.org/docs/latest/api/python/reference/pyspark.sql/api/pyspark.sql.DataFrame.filter.html#pyspark.sql.DataFrame.filter" target="_blank" rel="noopener">where/filter</a> that accepts a Spark SQL condition to filter the data.</li> <li>An <a href="https://spark.apache.org/docs/latest/api/python/reference/pyspark.sql/api/pyspark.sql.DataFrame.orderBy.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">orderBy</a> that sorts the data.</li> <li>A <a href="https://spark.apache.org/docs/latest/api/python/reference/pyspark.sql/api/pyspark.sql.DataFrame.limit.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">limit</a> that controls the number of items.</li> </ul> <p>On the back of these capabilities, we built the <code>find</code> command as our first command with its <code>--where</code>, <code>--orderby</code>, and <code>--limit</code> parameters requiring almost no coding on our part. If you want to print the 100 most recent undelivered orders, you can issue a bulk command like this:</p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-bash">./bulk find --table orders --where "status = 'undelivered'" --orderby timestamp desc --limit 100</code></pre> </div> <p>The <code>count</code> command uses the same code but counts the items instead of printing them. The <code>delete</code> command does the same, but after finding the items runs parallel batch delete calls to remove them.</p> <p>One downside of the Spark connector is that it doesn’t fully represent the source table data types. It simplifies the data and, for example, makes no distinction between a string set and a list of strings.</p> <h2 id="estimating-costs">Estimating costs</h2> <p>When working against large tables, we wanted to give a cost estimate before starting the work. Predicting the future is always hard. Here’s how we did it.</p> <p>For reads, we look at the table’s metadata (updated approximately every 6 hours, so beware a table that you recently loaded can read as smaller than it really is) to learn its recent size and calculate the read request units necessary for a full scan. For on-demand tables the math is straightforward: call the Pricing API to get the cost per million read request units for the region, multiply, done.</p> <p>For provisioned mode, cost depends on the provisioned level, auto scaling headroom, and job duration, making estimation harder. Our heuristic is to take two-thirds of the on-demand price (divide by 1.5). The reasoning: a table provisioned at 100 percent utilization costs about 30 percent of the equivalent on-demand throughput. However, 100 percent is rarely achievable in practice. At 50 percent utilization, the cost is about 60 percent. We use 67 percent because it’s better to quote a little high.</p> <p>For the <code>delete</code> command, write costs depend on how many items match, which we don’t know until after scanning. So <code>delete</code> calculates pricing in two phases: first the read cost estimate up front, then after finding the matches, the write cost based on the size of the deletion set.</p> <p>For the <code>fill</code> command, which mass loads synthetic data according to a custom generator, we first generate some sample items to know the approximate size of the items, then price accordingly.</p> <p>We don’t estimate AWS Glue Data Processing Unit (DPU) costs for two reasons. First, knowing how much compute will be required can’t be done simply. Second, we observed that for large jobs, DPU costs tend to be a small fraction of the DynamoDB costs. This makes sense, because an AWS Glue worker running at a small hourly cost can drive a huge amount of database activity. For small jobs, DPU is a larger fraction because of the overhead of getting things started, but the absolute dollars are low. You can specify <code>--XWaitForDPU</code> to see actual DPU costs reported after execution.</p> <p>Every <code>--X</code> parameter is a parameter to the Bulk Executor itself, as opposed to the command being run. We modeled this after the Java Virtual Machine convention.</p> <h2 id="job-termination-c">Job termination (^C)</h2> <p>We needed a way for the caller to terminate processing, for example if the pricing estimate comes back higher than expected. Pressing <code>^C</code> stops the client Python script immediately but leaves the AWS Glue job running until its timeout.</p> <p>We solved this by having the Python code catch the interrupt signal programmatically. An interrupt handler sees the <code>^C</code> and sets an <em>interrupted</em> flag. This flag gets noticed by a watcher loop that’s always running. Usually it’s watching for things like AWS Glue state changes. But when it sees the interrupt, it actively directs the AWS Glue job to stop before proceeding with a clean exit of the client-side program. Now one <code>^C</code> cleanly stops both sides.</p> <h2 id="mandating-point-in-time-recovery-pitr">Mandating point-in-time recovery (PITR)</h2> <p>A command like <code>delete</code> has the potential to delete more than anticipated. We made the design choice to require point-in-time recovery (PITR) to be enabled on any table receiving a mutating command. That way if your <code>--where</code> clause doesn’t do exactly what you wanted, you have a way to roll back.</p> <p>This is one reason we built out the <code>revert-export</code> command to undo writes in place based on a PITR-driven incremental export. See our previous blog post <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/database/recover-from-accidental-dynamodb-changes-using-bulk-executor/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">“Recover from accidental DynamoDB changes using Bulk Executor”</a>.</p> <h2 id="watching-execution-cloudwatch-live-tail">Watching execution (CloudWatch Live Tail)</h2> <p>In our first version, the client polled Amazon CloudWatch Logs for AWS Glue worker output. This was inefficient and meant output appeared delayed and in chunks. Switching to <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/mt/announcing-live-tail-feature-for-amazon-cloudwatch-logs/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon CloudWatch Live Tail</a> let job output stream to the client in near real-time.</p> <p>This introduced a problem: when <code>find</code> returned only a small number of matches things worked fine, but with large result sets we got incomplete output. The root cause: Live Tail displays <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cli/latest/reference/logs/start-live-tail.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">at most 500 events per second</a>. Beyond that, it streams only a sample of matching events.</p> <p>Our solution was to write large output (from <code>find</code> or <code>diff</code>) to an Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3) bucket and only stream the first 10 results to the terminal, plus a link to the full data set.</p> <p>Live Tail had two more surprises. Events can arrive out of order, so we built a reordering layer. Also, AWS Glue jobs can complete before their final log events appear in CloudWatch (there are a few seconds of propagation delay), so we couldn’t stop listening the moment the job stopped. We implemented a “microwave popcorn monitor”: stop listening only after a few seconds of silence.</p> <h2 id="fast-and-useful-error-handling">Fast and useful error handling</h2> <p>When an AWS Glue task hits an error, it logs an exception and by default the stack trace goes into the logs for the client to see. If the error is in only one task, that’s manageable, if a bit verbose. But if it’s something that hits every task (like a bad <code>--where</code> clause), you get hundreds of identical stack traces flooding the terminal. We addressed this with four patterns.</p> <p>On the client side, each command validates parameters before starting the AWS Glue job. A typo gets caught locally without AWS Glue ever spinning up.</p> <p>Inside AWS Glue, we create an <code>error_accumulator</code> that’s a <a href="https://spark.apache.org/docs/latest/api/python/reference/api/pyspark.Accumulator.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Spark accumulator</a>. Instead of letting exceptions propagate up from tasks en masse, each task adds its exception to the accumulator. The Spark driver then extracts the first error and prints only that: one clean message instead of hundreds of duplicates, followed by a graceful job shutdown.</p> <p>Continuously, on the client side, the Live Tail stream of events goes through a pattern matcher looking for error patterns that will never self-resolve (like <code>AccessDeniedException</code>, <code>ModuleNotFoundError</code>, <code>OutOfMemoryError</code>) and can trigger an immediate job stop rather than letting every task fail with the same error.</p> <p>Also, to prevent “scary” stack traces for something as simple as a typo in a <code>--where</code> clause, we use a special app-specific exception type that a command can choose to throw when wanting to give an error and not a stack trace. Only developer-type errors should generate stack traces.</p> <p>It took a while but we developed an approach to error handling that we liked: catch errors as quickly as possible, simplify them down to show the user only what’s necessary, and have the final line of each execution indicate if the job succeeded or failed (and why). We also added color coding (red for errors, yellow for warnings) to make issues easier to spot.</p> <h2 id="dynamodb-connector-compared-to-direct-access">DynamoDB connector compared to direct access</h2> <p>The AWS Glue connector reads and writes DataFrames, but it has no way to delete items. For the <code>delete</code> command we had no choice but to make direct DynamoDB API calls. The command logic uses the connector to first find the matching items (giving a DataFrame), then iterates over the items to perform deletes using batch writes. Here’s a simplified version:</p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-python">def delete_partition(partition, table_name, keys): table = dynamodb_resource.Table(table_name) with table.batch_writer() as batch: for record in partition: item = json.loads(record) key = {k: item[k] for k in keys} batch.delete_item(Key=key) # Distribute deletes across workers records.toJSON().foreachPartition( lambda partition: delete_partition(partition, table_name, keys) )</code></pre> </div> <p>Each Spark partition gets a slice of the matching items, and <code>foreachPartition()</code> runs the delete function in parallel across workers. The <code>batch_writer()</code> handles batching the individual deletes into efficient <code>batch_write_item</code> calls under the hood.</p> <p>We built <code>fill</code> and <code>load-export</code> to write items directly by using <code>batch_writer</code> also, instead of constructing a DataFrame.</p> <p>This pattern of going direct worked so well that it inspired us to rethink how we approached the first commands we wrote. We built a <code>scancount</code> command to explore whether counting could be faster without the connector.</p> <p>The answer: consistently faster, because of less overhead. The connector-based <code>count</code> has to load every item into the AWS Glue context to then filter them and count what survived the filter. With <code>scancount</code>, we use DynamoDB <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/amazondynamodb/latest/developerguide/Scan.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">segmented scan</a> with push-down predicates and <code>Select: COUNT</code> so nothing comes out of the database except the count itself with the filter already having been applied. Each worker scans its own segment of the table in parallel:</p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-python">def _count(table_name, index_name, filter_expression, segment, total_segments, total_matched_accumulator, error_accumulator): table = dynamodb_resource.Table(table_name) scan_kwargs = { "Select": "COUNT", "Segment": segment, "TotalSegments": total_segments } if index_name: scan_kwargs["IndexName"] = index_name if filter_expression: scan_kwargs["FilterExpression"] = filter_expression # Error handling code removed local_count = 0 while True: response = table.scan(**scan_kwargs) local_count += response.get("Count", 0) if "LastEvaluatedKey" not in response: break scan_kwargs["ExclusiveStartKey"] = response["LastEvaluatedKey"] total_matched_accumulator.add(local_count) return local_count parallelize_count = 100 rdd = spark_context.parallelize(range(parallelize_count), parallelize_count) rdd.foreach(lambda worker_id: _count(table_name, index_name, filter_expression, worker_id, parallelize_count, total_matched_accumulator, error_accumulator))</code></pre> </div> <p>The tradeoff: <code>scancount</code> accepts a raw <code>--filter-expression</code> instead of the higher-level <code>--where</code> Spark SQL syntax, since there’s no DataFrame involved.</p> <p>The code for <code>diff</code> and <code>copy</code> also does direct reads instead of using the connector. With these commands, it wasn’t for speed but to make sure they keep perfect data type fidelity.</p> <p>A side note on <code>diff</code>: it was important for each task to write its subset of output directly to its own Amazon S3 object, without ever centralizing the output back to the driver, which would not scale.</p> <h2 id="rate-limiting">Rate limiting</h2> <p>The connector provides built-in rate limiting. However, for our commands making direct API calls (such as <code>delete</code>, <code>fill</code>, <code>copy</code>, and <code>scancount</code>), there’s no rate limiting. With enough AWS Glue workers running loops unconstrained, it usually generates unwanted throttles.</p> <p>We built a distributed rate limiter that coordinates across all workers. The approach is described in detail in Rate-limiting calls to Amazon DynamoDB using Python Boto3, <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/database/rate-limiting-calls-to-amazon-dynamodb-using-python-boto3-part-1/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Part 1</a> and <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/database/rate-limiting-calls-to-amazon-dynamodb-using-python-boto3-part-2-distributed-coordination/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Part 2: Distributed Coordination</a>.</p> <p>Users control this with <code>--XMaxReadRate</code> and <code>--XMaxWriteRate</code> parameters. If unspecified, we investigate the environment to infer the appropriate rates based on account and table settings and quotas.</p> <p>Recently, we added a feature to issue a (yellow) warning if the max read or write rate won’t allow the work to complete within the <code>--XTimeout</code> (which defaults to one hour). Better to warn the user early: if even the maximum rate can’t finish within the timeout, the user can restart with a higher rate or a longer timeout.</p> <h2 id="building-a-library-of-commands">Building a library of commands</h2> <p>We started with the idea that commands should be straightforward to drop in, like new Unix commands. The loose coupling has paid off in contributions from outside the original core team. So far we’ve seen:</p> <ul> <li>The <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/database/efficiently-compare-items-across-two-amazon-dynamodb-tables/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">diff command</a> contributed by Chris Gillespie.</li> <li>The <code>sql</code> command contributed by John Terhune.</li> <li>The <code>load</code> command contributed by Arjan Schaaf.</li> <li>The <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/database/filter-transform-and-load-your-dynamodb-table-exports-using-aws-glue/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">load-export</a> and <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/database/recover-from-accidental-dynamodb-changes-using-bulk-executor/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">revert-export</a> commands contributed by Ruskin Dantra.</li> </ul> <p>Each command is its own Python module loaded by name. The contract is lightweight: export a <code>run(job, spark_context, glue_context, parsed_args)</code> function and use the shared conventions (the <code>error_accumulator</code> for exceptions, the distributed rate limiter for DynamoDB calls, and a shared library to help with cost estimates).</p> <h2 id="upgrading-to-new-aws-glue-versions-with-the-help-of-ai">Upgrading to new AWS Glue versions, with the help of AI</h2> <p>A few months ago, we migrated from AWS Glue 4 to <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/big-data/introducing-aws-glue-5-0-for-apache-spark/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Glue 5</a>. The benefits: faster job startup and a new DynamoDB connector that loads directly into a DataFrame (the old connector required a DynamicFrame intermediate step, adding overhead and complexity).</p> <p>The migration also gave us a chance to measure the new connector against the old one. We used AI tooling to generate a suite of stress tests comparing both connectors under AWS Glue 5, varying table sizes and read/write patterns. The AI ran these tests for hours, collected the results, and produced a comparison report that gave us the confidence the upgrade was worthwhile.</p> <p>This migration had been on our backlog for a while and we kept postponing it. With AI handling the test generation and execution, we finished it in one interactive working session.</p> <p>Since then we’ve upgraded to <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/big-data/introducing-aws-glue-6-0-for-apache-spark/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Glue 6</a>, this time to capture the 30 percent lower costs for DPU-hours.</p> <h2 id="implementing-an-ai-linter">Implementing an AI linter</h2> <p>We noticed some things are hard to test using either unit tests or end-to-end tests. For example, we have some data that exists in three places in three different formats, and we want to make sure those three definitions do not drift apart.</p> <p>First, we have a README that explains some aspects of security. You can either let us generate a new AWS Glue job role for you, or you can pass in your own custom role by name. It explains, in English prose, the minimum AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) permissions required by the AWS Glue job role if you create your own. That’s one definition.</p> <p>The second definition is that when we create the role, we attach the right set of permissions on the role. The third definition is that when you provide the role we check the role’s effective permissions (partly through the IAM policy simulator and partly by reading its attached policies) to confirm that you’ve given the role the proper minimum. This follows the principle that we should error out early here if not, instead of on first execution.</p> <p>It would be a bad and subtle bug if, as the permissions requirements change, any of the three differently-formatted statements of the same core facts diverge. It’s not easy to compare English prose with role-creating logic with IAM-checking logic.</p> <p>Our solution was to create an AI linter. It’s a <a href="https://github.com/awslabs/amazon-dynamodb-tools/tree/main/tools/bulk_executor/ai_lint" target="_blank" rel="noopener">folder in the project</a> with a README that defines the linter behavior and a subfolder with a set of rules each held as a markdown file. When desired, we can ask AI to run the linting. No coding required.</p> <p>Other rules we have say that every command (including commands in the future) should impose rate limiting, whether through the connector or Python library. That’s not easy to test, but easy for AI to reason about. Another rule is every command with large output must write the output to Amazon S3 (following a particular location selection convention) and output to the terminal a bounded preview with a link.</p> <p>Any false alarms are only informative to us as a development team, and we can refine the rules to remove them. Of course, AI authored the markdown files themselves too.</p> <h2 id="conclusion">Conclusion</h2> <p>None of the problems we solved were individually massive. They were all little things: Live Tail sampling events, <code>^C</code> not reaching the remote job, error messages repeating hundreds of times, not knowing costs in advance, unbounded read and write rates. But each one made the tool frustrating to use until we fixed it. If you’re building your own tooling on AWS Glue, we hope these solutions save you some discovery time. We also hope our experience using AI for long-running or hard-to-run tests gives you some ideas.</p> <p>The project is open source on <a href="https://github.com/awslabs/amazon-dynamodb-tools/tree/main/tools/bulk_executor" target="_blank" rel="noopener">GitHub</a> and we welcome contributions. Now that you’ve seen the internals, you have everything you need to write your own commands. Write the <code>run()</code> function and rely on the harness for the rest. If you think it’s generally useful, send us a pull request!</p> <p>&nbsp;</p> <p style="clear: both"></p> <hr style="width: 100%"> <h2>About the authors</h2>

Read full article →
