Agentic antimicrobial resistance surveillance with AWS HealthOmics and Amazon Bedrock AgentCore

https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/architecture/agentic-antimicrobial-resistance-surveillance-with-aws-healthomics-and-amazon-bedrock-agentcore/📅 2026-10-12 22:34 UTC+8

<p><em>Turn public sequencing data into plain-language answers about resistance genes, without writing bioinformatics code or SQL.</em></p> <p>Bacterial antimicrobial resistance (AMR) is one of the top public health threats of our time. A systematic analysis published in The Lancet estimated that AMR was directly responsible for 1.27 million deaths in 2019 and associated with 4.95 million (<a href="https://www.thelancet.com/journals/lancet/article/PIIS0140-6736(21)02724-0/fulltext" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Murray et al., 2022</a>). Surveillance programs work to slow that trend by tracking which resistance genes circulate, in which organisms, and how the picture shifts over time.</p> <p>The raw material for that work is already public. Sequencing archives such as the NCBI Sequence Read Archive (SRA) hold hundreds of thousands of bacterial whole-genome sequencing runs, and the data is mirrored on the <a href="https://registry.opendata.aws/ncbi-sra/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Registry of Open Data on AWS</a>. The constraint isn’t access to data. It’s the work of turning a raw sequencing run into an answer. That path normally means assembling a genome, screening it for resistance genes with specialized tools, loading the results into an analytical store, and writing SQL to explore them. Each step assumes bioinformatics skills, so the people who ask the epidemiological questions often depend on a specialist to answer them.</p> <p>In this post, we present a reference architecture that shortens that path. A researcher signs in to a web application, submits a public SRA accession, and after the pipeline finishes asks, in plain language, “What AMR genes were found in Salmonella?” The system answers with the genes detected, the tool that found each one, and a confidence value taken from the data. The architecture is defined entirely in <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cdk/v2/guide/home.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Cloud Development Kit (AWS CDK)</a> and deploys into an account with a single command. This is a demonstration architecture rather than a clinical system, and the accompanying code is available for you to deploy and adapt.</p> <div style="width: 640px;" class="wp-video"> <video class="wp-video-shortcode" id="video-18747-1" width="640" height="360" preload="metadata" controls="controls"><source type="video/mp4" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/artifacts/DBSBlogs/ARCHBLOG-1710/AMR+DEMO.mp4?_=1"></video> </div> <h2 id="how-this-architecture-differs-from-variant-interpretation">How this architecture differs from variant interpretation</h2> <p>An earlier AWS post, <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/accelerating-genomics-variant-interpretation-with-aws-healthomics-and-amazon-bedrock-agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Accelerating genomics variant interpretation with AWS HealthOmics and Amazon Bedrock AgentCore</a>, established a pattern that this solution shares: a genomics pipeline feeds <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/s3-tables.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon S3 Tables</a>, a capability of Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3), in Apache Iceberg format, <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/athena/latest/ug/what-is.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Athena</a> queries the tables, and a Strands agent on <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/what-is-bedrock-agentcore.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Bedrock AgentCore</a> turns questions into SQL. We reuse that querying pattern rather than reinvent it. What’s different here is the genomics problem and the shape of the pipeline around it.</p> <p>The variant interpretation solution starts from a Variant Call Format (VCF) file, where variants have already been called, and annotates them. This solution starts a step earlier, from raw sequencing reads, and performs read quality control, de novo assembly, and gene detection before results exist to store. It adds an ingestion stage that pulls reads directly from a public archive and deduplicates work across runs, and it merges detection output into Iceberg idempotently so that reprocessing an isolate updates its rows in place. Those differences, public-data ingestion, assembly-based detection, and idempotent storage, are where the engineering effort of this architecture sits.</p> <h2 id="solution-overview">Solution overview</h2> <p>The solution is a set of loosely coupled stages, and data moves in one direction from ingestion through processing and storage to a conversational query layer. The following stages map to the numbered zones in Figure 2.</p> <div style="width: 731px" class="wp-caption alignnone"> <a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/fc074d501302eb2b93e2554793fcaf50b3bf7291/2026/10/08/ARCHBLOG-1710-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"><img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/fc074d501302eb2b93e2554793fcaf50b3bf7291/2026/10/08/ARCHBLOG-1710-2.png" alt="Solution architecture diagram showing five stages: ingestion, genomics processing, storage, analytical store, and agent" width="721"></a> <p class="wp-caption-text">Figure 2: Solution architecture, organized as the five stages the data passes through</p> </div> <ol type="1"> <li><strong>Ingestion:</strong>&nbsp;An <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/scheduler/latest/UserGuide/what-is-scheduler.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon EventBridge Scheduler</a> rule or an on-demand REST call starts an <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/step-functions/latest/dg/welcome.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Step Functions</a> workflow. An Amazon Elastic Container Service (Amazon ECS) task on AWS Fargate fetches and converts the sequencing run for the requested accession from the NCBI SRA mirror on the Registry of Open Data, and Amazon DynamoDB records processed isolates so that repeat runs skip work that’s already done.</li> <li><strong>Genomics processing:</strong>&nbsp;<a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/omics/latest/dev/what-is-healthomics.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS HealthOmics</a> runs a single managed Nextflow workflow that chains read quality control with fastp, de novo assembly with SKESA, and resistance gene detection with AMRFinderPlus. Container images come from <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonECR/latest/userguide/what-is-ecr.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Elastic Container Registry (Amazon ECR)</a>, and the AMRFinderPlus reference database is built into the image because HealthOmics tasks run without internet access.</li> <li><strong>Harmonization and storage:</strong>&nbsp;An <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/lambda/latest/dg/welcome.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Lambda</a> function normalizes the detection output into the community <a href="https://github.com/pha4ge/hAMRonization" target="_blank" rel="noopener">hAMRonization</a> schema (a standard from PHA4GE for reporting AMR detection results), and an <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/glue/latest/dg/what-is-glue.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Glue</a> job merges it into Iceberg tables on Amazon S3 Tables. The merge is idempotent, so rerunning an isolate updates rows rather than duplicating them.</li> <li><strong>Analytical store:</strong>&nbsp;Amazon Athena queries the Iceberg tables through the <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/glue/latest/dg/catalog-and-crawler.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Glue Data Catalog</a>, which gives the agent a fast SQL surface over the harmonized results.</li> <li><strong>Conversational agent and web application:</strong> A Strands agent hosted on AgentCore runtime answers questions through a set of Athena-backed tools, with <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/guardrails.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Bedrock Guardrails</a> keeping responses grounded. A React single-page application on <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/Welcome.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon S3</a> and <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonCloudFront/latest/DeveloperGuide/Introduction.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon CloudFront</a>, protected by <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/waf/latest/developerguide/what-is-aws-waf.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS WAF</a> and authenticated with <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cognito/latest/developerguide/what-is-amazon-cognito.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Cognito</a>, is the front door. <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/apigateway/latest/developerguide/welcome.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon API Gateway</a> serves REST calls and streams the chat over WebSocket.</li> </ol> <h2 id="ingesting-public-sequencing-data">Ingesting public sequencing data</h2> <p>Ingestion has one job: given an accession, get the reads into the account without manual downloads or credentials. Because the SRA mirror is part of the Registry of Open Data, an anonymous Amazon S3 read is enough. A Step Functions workflow drives the process. It starts a Fargate task that downloads the sequencing run for the accession from the public bucket and converts it to paired-end FASTQ in a staging bucket, then hands off to the genomics stage.</p> <p>Two design choices keep the stage predictable. A DynamoDB table records every isolate that has been processed, so a scheduled weekly run or a repeated manual submission doesn’t reprocess the same accession. Fargate runs the copy as a long-running task because fetching and converting a large sequencing run can take well beyond a few minutes. The result is a loop that runs on a schedule or on demand from the web application, without an operator in the middle.</p> <h2 id="detecting-resistance-genes-with-aws-healthomics">Detecting resistance genes with AWS HealthOmics</h2> <p>Turning reads into a list of resistance genes is the compute-heavy part of the pipeline. AWS HealthOmics runs Nextflow workflows without a cluster to provision or scale, which suits teams that do not run a bioinformatics system of their own.</p> <p>The workflow is a single HealthOmics run with three tasks connected by Nextflow channels. fastp trims adapters and low-quality bases from the raw reads. SKESA assembles the trimmed reads into contigs. AMRFinderPlus screens those contigs for known resistance genes. Combining assembly and screening in one run avoids a fragile handoff between two separate runs and matches how community AMR pipelines are structured.</p> <p>Running third-party bioinformatics tools on HealthOmics calls for a few adaptations worth stating plainly:</p> <ul> <li>HealthOmics tasks have no internet access, so the AMRFinderPlus database is baked into the container image rather than downloaded at runtime.</li> <li>Container image URIs are passed as workflow parameters instead of hardcoded, which keeps the workflow independent of account and AWS Region and lets HealthOmics verify image access before a run starts.</li> <li>Task outputs are published to the run output path that HealthOmics exports to Amazon S3, rather than to arbitrary locations.</li> </ul> <p>Each container image is built from official tool releases with pinned versions and scanned with Amazon ECR image scanning, so the pipeline doesn’t depend on a floating <code>latest</code> tag.</p> <h2 id="harmonizing-and-storing-results">Harmonizing and storing results</h2> <p>AMRFinderPlus emits a tab-separated file per isolate, which is awkward to analyze across many samples. A Lambda function first normalizes that output into the hAMRonization schema. An AWS Glue job then reshapes the harmonized data into two Iceberg tables on Amazon S3 Tables: <code>amr_profiles</code>, with one row per detected gene, and <code>isolate_metadata</code>, with one row per isolate carrying its provenance and license.</p> <p>The AWS Glue job writes with a MERGE keyed on the isolate and gene, so processing the same isolate twice updates the existing rows instead of appending duplicates. That idempotency matters for surveillance, where the same accession can arrive again through a scheduled run, and it keeps the tables consistent without a separate deduplication step. Storing the results as Iceberg gives the agent a columnar, transactional surface to query with low latency, and because Amazon S3 Tables registers the schema in the AWS Glue Data Catalog, Athena reads the same tables without extra setup. Converting per-sample files into a structured store is what makes conversational querying across the whole corpus possible.</p> <h2 id="asking-questions-in-natural-language">Asking questions in natural language</h2> <p>The conversational layer uses the <a href="https://strandsagents.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Strands Agents SDK</a> and runs on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, a platform to build, connect, and optimize agents at scale, with any framework or model. This solution uses AgentCore runtime, its serverless agent-hosting environment. It gives each user session an isolated environment, integrates with AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM), and traces the agent’s reasoning and tool calls. The team runs the agent without managing servers or scaling logic.</p> <p>The agent reasons over four tools, and each one turns a natural-language request into a targeted Athena query against the Iceberg tables:</p> <ul> <li><code>query_amr_profiles</code> finds resistance genes for an organism over an optional date range.</li> <li><code>get_resistance_trends</code> summarizes how gene detections trend month over month.</li> <li><code>compare_isolates</code> compares resistance profiles across isolates.</li> <li><code>lookup_gene_info</code> returns detail on a specific resistance gene.</li> </ul> <p>Grounding is enforced in two places. The system prompt requires the agent to cite the gene ID, the detecting tool, and a confidence value for every claim. It must also say plainly when no data supports an answer rather than guess. Amazon Bedrock Guardrails add a second layer, with a contextual grounding check and denied topics that keep responses tied to the returned rows and off medical or patient advice. The agent uses Anthropic Claude Opus 4.7 on <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/what-is-bedrock.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Bedrock</a>, invoked through a cross-Region inference profile, for the reasoning and synthesis those rules depend on. Model availability varies by AWS Region. See the <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/models-regions.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Bedrock model support by Region</a> documentation for current availability.</p> <p>After an isolate has been processed, a researcher explores it through conversation instead of SQL. Asked “What AMR genes were found in Salmonella?”, the agent calls <code>query_amr_profiles</code>, retrieves the matching rows from Iceberg through Athena, and replies with the detected genes. Each gene is annotated with the detecting tool and its confidence value, along with a short reading of what the genes represent. A follow-up such as “Tell me more about mdsB” triggers <code>lookup_gene_info</code> without the user thinking about tables or queries.</p> <p><img loading="lazy" class="alignleft size-full wp-image-18793" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/fc074d501302eb2b93e2554793fcaf50b3bf7291/2026/10/08/Figure-3.png" alt="" width="1125" height="618"></p> <h2 id="benefits">Benefits</h2> <p>The architecture turns four recurring pain points into managed, repeatable steps.</p> <table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"> <tbody> <tr> <td><strong>Challenge</strong></td> <td><strong>How the architecture addresses it</strong></td> </tr> <tr> <td><strong>Fetching and assembling public sequencing data by hand</strong></td> <td>A Step Functions workflow pulls reads from the AWS Open Data SRA mirror and runs assembly and screening on managed HealthOmics compute.</td> </tr> <tr> <td><strong>Reconciling detection output across many samples</strong></td> <td>A Lambda function harmonizes results and AWS Glue merges them idempotently into Iceberg tables on Amazon S3 Tables.</td> </tr> <tr> <td><strong>SQL and bioinformatics skills needed to ask a question</strong></td> <td>A Strands agent on AgentCore turns plain-language questions into Athena queries and answers with cited evidence.</td> </tr> <tr> <td><strong>Reproducing the environment in another account</strong></td> <td>The solution is defined in AWS CDK with no hardcoded identifiers, so it deploys with a single command.</td> </tr> </tbody> </table> <p>Because this is a reference architecture rather than a customer deployment, its proof is that anyone can stand it up. The CDK application provisions every component described here, from the ingestion workflow to the Iceberg tables to the agent runtime and the web front end. It needs no hardcoded account identifiers or manual setup steps. Running costs depend on how many isolates you process and how often, because HealthOmics runs, AWS Glue jobs, and agent calls are billed per use. The architecture stays inexpensive when idle.</p> <h2 id="conclusion">Conclusion</h2> <p>Pairing AWS HealthOmics for managed genomics processing with Amazon Bedrock AgentCore for grounded interpretation lets a surveillance team move from a public accession number to an answer about resistance genes in one continuous flow. Assembly, gene detection, harmonization, and storage happen without manual steps, and the analytical layer is reached through a conversation rather than a query console. The pattern reuses the S3 Tables and agent querying approach from earlier work and applies it to a different genomics problem. That problem begins at raw reads and depends on public-data ingestion and idempotent storage.</p> <p>To go deeper, see the <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/healthomics/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS HealthOmics</a> and <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Bedrock AgentCore</a> product pages, and the related post <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/accelerating-genomics-variant-interpretation-with-aws-healthomics-and-amazon-bedrock-agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Accelerating genomics variant interpretation with AWS HealthOmics and Amazon Bedrock AgentCore</a>.</p> <p>The solution is defined entirely in AWS CDK and deploys into your own account. Explore the <a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-amr-genomic-agentic-platform" target="_blank" rel="noopener">accompanying repository</a> on GitHub and adapt the pattern to your own surveillance and life-sciences use cases.</p> <p style="clear: both"></p> <hr style="width: 100%"> <h2>About the authors</h2>

Announcing the AWS Digital Sovereignty Lens for the Well-Architected Framework

https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/architecture/announcing-the-aws-digital-sovereignty-well-architected-lens/📅 2026-10-06 01:54 UTC+8

<p><em><strong>October 2026: This post was reviewed and updated for accuracy.</strong></em></p> <p>Today we’re launching the <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/wellarchitected/latest/digital-sovereignty-lens/digital-sovereignty-lens.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"><strong>AWS Digital Sovereignty Lens</strong></a>, a new Well-Architected lens providing customers with extended guidance for designing, building, and operating sovereign workloads on AWS.</p> <p>Customers tell us that digital sovereignty brings a unique set of challenges that existing architecture guidance doesn’t fully address. Sovereignty requirements don’t arrive as a single brief. They come from multiple teams, including privacy, legal, compliance, and security. These requirements sometimes conflict. For example, a localization mandate requires in-country storage, whereas resilience might call for replication across jurisdictions.</p> <p>Sovereignty also depends on coordinated choices across compute, storage, networking, identity, encryption, and operations. A decision in one layer can weaken a control in another. Third-party dependencies, from software libraries to external APIs, carry jurisdictional exposure of their own.</p> <p>The lens builds on the <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/security/aws-digital-sovereignty-pledge-control-without-compromise/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Digital Sovereignty Pledge</a> we made in 2022, our commitment to giving you sovereignty controls and features in the cloud without compromising on capabilities, performance, innovation, and scale.</p> <h2 id="five-questions-that-define-a-sovereign-workload">Five questions that define a sovereign workload</h2> <p>The lens treats sovereignty as an architectural concern. It uses five questions to help you determine which concerns apply to your workload and to what degree:</p> <ol type="1"> <li><strong>Where is the workload located?</strong> (locality)</li> <li><strong>Who can reach it?</strong> (access control)</li> <li><strong>Will it keep working when conditions change?</strong> (continuity)</li> <li><strong>Can it be transferred elsewhere?</strong> (portability and interoperability)</li> <li><strong>Can you prove sovereignty controls are in place?</strong> (transparency and auditability)</li> </ol> <p>The first four concerns can trade off or influence one another. Mandates that restrict technology choices to a narrow pool of providers, for example, can constrain your recovery and exit options. You decide which concern takes precedence, which risks to accept, and why. The fifth question plays a different role. It asks what controls and evidence demonstrate that the other four concerns are addressed.</p> <h2 id="whats-in-the-lens">What’s in the lens</h2> <p>If you’ve run a Well-Architected review the structure is familiar: pillars, questions, and best practices. The lens covers four of the six pillars, with 21 questions and 37 best practices:</p> <ul> <li>The operational excellence pillar covers sovereignty governance, compliance automation, continuous auditability, compliance monitoring and remediation, and regulatory change. It answers the fifth question (of the Five questions that define a sovereign workload): can you prove sovereignty controls are in place?</li> <li>The security pillar covers secure foundations, access control, control verification, threat detection, operator access, data protection, and event response. It answers the first two questions: where is the workload located, and who can reach it?</li> <li>The reliability pillar covers continuity planning, third-party risk, portability and interoperability, and disruptions beyond technical failures. It answers questions three and four: will it keep working, and can it be transferred?</li> <li>The performance efficiency pillar covers sovereign deployment choices and software-dependency assessment, weighing all five questions against each other.</li> </ul> <p>For the remaining two pillars, cost optimization and sustainability, the existing Well-Architected guidance applies to sovereign workloads.</p> <h2 id="what-are-sovereignty-controls">What are sovereignty controls?</h2> <p>Sovereignty controls build on capabilities that builders already use, including service control policies (SCPs), resource control policies (RCPs), AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) policies, firewall rules, and backup policies. No single control meets data residency, data sovereignty, or continuity requirements on its own. They layer together in a defense-in-depth approach. <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/controltower/latest/controlreference/digital-sovereignty-controls.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Control Tower</a> groups these controls under its digital sovereignty category, covering data residency, granular access restriction, encryption, and resiliency.</p> <p>The lens also covers controls you build yourself. These include validating infrastructure templates with <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cfn-guard/latest/ug/what-is-guard.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS CloudFormation Guard</a>, verifying access policies with <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/IAM/latest/UserGuide/what-is-access-analyzer.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">IAM Access Analyzer</a>, and detecting configuration drift with <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/config/latest/developerguide/WhatIsConfig.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Config</a>.</p> <p>Not every sovereignty control is technical. Deciding which personnel can provide operational support, from which locations, and under what authority is an operational control. Reviewing that access on a regular schedule is another. Together, technical and operational controls combine to produce continuous evidence of your sovereignty posture.</p> <p>This is what <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/security/tag/sovereign-by-design/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">sovereign-by-design</a> means in practice. AWS builds sovereignty foundations, such as the <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ec2/nitro/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Nitro System</a>, which enforces access restrictions so that nobody, including AWS employees, can access customer data running in Amazon Elastic Compute Cloud (Amazon EC2). Your controls express jurisdictional requirements on top of those foundations, using familiar policy tools reinforced by operational controls.</p> <h2 id="where-sovereignty-requirements-arise">Where sovereignty requirements arise</h2> <p>Sovereignty requirements extend beyond public sector workloads. The lens describes seven scenarios where they commonly arise. Four of them illustrate the range:</p> <ul> <li>You’re expanding a workload into a new jurisdiction and need to identify which obligations apply before you deploy.</li> <li>Regulation restricts who can provide operational support for your workloads, from where, and under what authority.</li> <li>Residency constraints limit your disaster recovery options, and you need recovery paths that hold within approved jurisdictional boundaries.</li> <li>You’re adopting emerging technology such as generative AI, and need to confirm that new data flows and dependencies don’t undermine existing sovereignty controls.</li> </ul> <p>If one or more scenarios match your workload, the lens applies to you.</p> <h2 id="how-to-use-the-lens">How to use the lens</h2> <p>The lens works with the <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/wellarchitected/latest/userguide/intro.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Well-Architected Tool</a>. Import it as a custom lens, select a workload, and <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/wellarchitected/latest/userguide/running-a-wafr.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">run a review</a>. The review <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/wellarchitected/latest/userguide/identify-and-understand-risks.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">identifies high- and medium-risk issues</a>, which you prioritize and address through <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/wellarchitected/latest/userguide/improving-your-workload.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">targeted improvement plans</a>. This grounds sovereignty decisions in evidence rather than assumptions.</p> <p>For your first review, include participants from engineering, legal, compliance, and security. Together, they can connect applicable obligations to workload evidence, prioritize risks, and agree on remediation plans. Record assumptions, accepted risks, owners, and follow-up actions. Repeat the review when regulations, workloads, dependencies, or operating conditions change.</p> <h2 id="getting-started">Getting started</h2> <p>The Digital Sovereignty Lens is available today in the <a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-well-architected-custom-lens" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Well-Architected custom lens GitHub repository</a>. Download the <a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-well-architected-custom-lens/blob/main/digital-sovereignty-lens/digital-sovereignty-lens.json" target="_blank" rel="noopener">lens definition</a>, import it into the AWS Well-Architected Tool, and run your first review.</p> <p>To learn how AWS builds sovereign-by-design infrastructure and controls, visit <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/compliance/digital-sovereignty/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Digital Sovereignty</a>. For help addressing technical and operational sovereignty requirements, consider engaging <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/compliance/digital-sovereignty/partners/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Digital Sovereignty Competency Partners</a>.</p> <p>In 2022, we pledged control without compromise. The Digital Sovereignty Lens is how you architect for it.</p> <hr> <h3 id="about-the-authors">About the authors</h3>

Read full article →

Deploy open source Regional availability tools in your VPC

https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/architecture/deploy-open-source-regional-availability-tools-in-your-vpc/📅 2026-10-03 01:50 UTC+8

<p>When you build multi-Region architectures on AWS, one question comes up early: “What services are available in each AWS Region?” The answer shapes architecture decisions, from which AWS Regions to expand into, to how you design for resilience. For teams navigating data residency requirements and compliance reporting, getting the answer wrong has real consequences: deployment failures, compliance gaps, and delayed launches.</p> <p>AWS publishes Regional availability data covering services, features, APIs, and <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cloudformation/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS CloudFormation</a> resource types across all AWS Regions. You can explore this data on the <a href="https://builder.aws.com/build/capabilities/explore?tab=service-feature" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Capabilities by Region</a> page, access it through <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/s3/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3)</a> for pipeline integration, or query it using the <a href="https://awslabs.github.io/mcp/servers/aws-knowledge-mcp-server/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Knowledge MCP server</a>. These options work well for exploration and automation. However, teams told us they need this data deployed as infrastructure they own, refreshing on their schedule, inside their network, filtered to their workload. That means running availability data the same way you run the rest of your stack: in your <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/vpc/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Virtual Private Cloud (Amazon VPC)</a>, under your governance.</p> <p>In this post, we introduce two open source solutions that deliver that ownership. <a href="https://github.com/aws/capability-insights-for-aws" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Capability Insights for AWS</a> deploys a Regional availability dashboard into your VPC that auto-refreshes every 24 hours. The dashboard, its API, and availability data all run in your own account, and the scheduled refresh makes the only call that leaves your account when it reads the dataset that AWS publishes. <a href="https://github.com/aws/capability-insights-for-aws" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Workload Analysis</a> scans your <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cloudtrail/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS CloudTrail</a> logs and CloudFormation stacks, then narrows 200+ services to the 20–30 your account actually runs, significantly reducing the scope of a Regional gap analysis.</p> <p>Together with the <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/storage/building-automated-aws-regional-availability-checks-with-amazon-s3/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Capabilities by Region S3 Access Point</a>, you now have three levels of control: consume from Amazon S3, deploy into your VPC, or filter to your workload. We walk through how to deploy each solution, run a workload analysis, and view personalized results during your Regional expansion planning. Whether you’re building multi-Region recovery strategies, standardizing compliance reporting, or accelerating expansion timelines, these solutions put the data and the decisions it drives inside your perimeter.</p> <p>Figure 1 shows how Capability Insights for AWS integrates with the VPC and subnets in your existing AWS account. A client in the public subnet accesses the dashboard through an S3 gateway endpoint and calls the private API through an API Gateway VPC endpoint.</p> <div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"> <a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/fc074d501302eb2b93e2554793fcaf50b3bf7291/2026/09/30/ARCHBLOG-1522-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"><img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/fc074d501302eb2b93e2554793fcaf50b3bf7291/2026/09/30/ARCHBLOG-1522-1.png" alt="Capability Insights for AWS architecture showing a client in the public subnet reaching the dashboard through an S3 gateway endpoint and the private API through an API Gateway VPC endpoint" width="800"></a> <p class="wp-caption-text">Figure 1: Capability Insights for AWS architecture, including private dashboard access and scheduled Regional availability data refreshes.</p> </div> <p>An Amazon EventBridge schedule invokes the data-fetch Lambda function every 24 hours. The data-fetch function runs outside your VPC, so it reads the S3 access point published by AWS over the AWS network. The function pulls Regional availability data from the Capabilities by Region S3 bucket published by AWS and writes it to the website bucket within your account. The dashboard application accesses the data within the website through the S3 gateway endpoint from your VPC. The API Lambda can also invoke the data-fetch function on demand through the Lambda VPC endpoint. The deployment assets bucket supplies the Lambda code during deployment only.</p> <h2 id="prerequisites">Prerequisites</h2> <p>To follow along, you need:</p> <ul> <li>An AWS account with permissions to deploy the CloudFormation stacks, including permission to create named <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/iam/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM)</a> roles. At runtime, the roles created by the stacks use scoped permissions for Amazon S3 object read and write access, AWS CloudFormation read operations, <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/athena/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Athena</a> queries, AWS Glue and AWS Lake Formation catalog access, AWS Lambda invocation, and AWS Step Functions execution. For starting-point deployment policies, see the <a href="https://github.com/aws/capability-insights-for-aws/tree/main/docs" target="_blank" rel="noopener">documentation</a> folder in the repository.</li> <li><a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cli/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Command Line Interface (AWS CLI)</a> installed and configured.</li> <li>A VPC with DNS resolution and DNS hostnames enabled, one public subnet (routed to an internet gateway) for dashboard and API access, and one private subnet (no internet route) for the in-VPC Lambda function. Both subnets need a route to Amazon S3 through a gateway VPC endpoint.</li> <li><a href="https://nodejs.org/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Node.js v24.18.0</a> (includes npm and npx) for automated deployment.</li> <li>An S3 bucket for deployment assets.</li> <li>An active CloudTrail configuration where you store logs in an S3 bucket (required for Part 2).</li> </ul> <h2 id="part-1-deploy-a-self-hosted-dashboard-capability-insights-for-aws">Part 1: Deploy a self-hosted dashboard (Capability Insights for AWS)</h2> <p>Capability Insights for AWS deploys a searchable Regional availability dashboard into your own AWS account. The solution pulls data from the AWS Capabilities by Region S3 bucket, stores it inside your VPC, and serves it through a static website backed by <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/api-gateway/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon API Gateway</a>, <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/lambda/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Lambda</a>, and <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/eventbridge/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon EventBridge</a>.</p> <p>If your organization has access to additional data sources beyond the public dataset, the solution incorporates those as well, giving you a unified view across all AWS partitions you have access to. To learn more about accessing additional data, work with your AWS representative.</p> <p>The dashboard covers:</p> <ul> <li><strong>Services and features</strong>: availability status, expected launch dates, and expansion plans per Region.</li> <li><strong>API operations</strong>: individual API action availability per Region for each AWS service.</li> <li><strong>CloudFormation resource types</strong>: which resource types each Region supports.</li> </ul> <p>You provide your own VPC, subnets, and S3 bucket so the solution integrates with your existing infrastructure and security controls.</p> <h3 id="clone-and-install">Clone and install</h3> <div class="hide-language"> <p

Accelerating airline retailing innovation: how Datalex modernized with AWS Experience-Based Acceleration and agentic AI

https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/architecture/accelerating-airline-retailing-innovation-how-datalex-modernized-with-aws-experience-based-acceleration-and-agentic-ai/📅 2026-10-01 23:37 UTC+8

<p><a href="https://www.datalex.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Datalex</a>, a leader in airline ecommerce solutions, set out to answer a question facing every established product-based business: how do you build for where your industry is going, not only where it is today? For more than two decades, Datalex has powered digital retailing for many of the world’s leading airlines, capability built up over years and encoded in a substantial, mission-critical system that runs shopping, pricing, and booking at scale. That depth is a considerable asset, and it is also what makes evolution demanding. The airline industry is moving decisively toward Modern Airline Retailing, an offers-and-orders model with richer integrations and AI-native experiences, and Datalex set out to build the system for that future while carrying forward the proven retail logic its customers rely on every day. The system’s foundations had served that mission reliably for years. The goal now was to modernize the runtime and delivery model so the team could ship the next generation of retailing capability faster, without disrupting the airline operations running on it today. Datalex framed a considered roadmap, Project Phoenix, to get there, and the open question was how much of that journey could be accelerated.</p> <p>The company’s CTO, Brian Lewis, sponsored the modernization effort and brought together teams across engineering, product, and operations. As Brian Lewis put it, “This is Datalex’s most important project.” The system’s richness was precisely what made the task substantial: years of sophisticated, tightly integrated retail logic that airlines depend on around the clock, built on a mature Java and EJB2 architecture. The team’s central question was never whether the system had value to carry forward, it clearly did, but how to evolve a system of this depth incrementally, at speed and without disruption to airline customers’ operations.</p> <p>In December 2025, Datalex partnered with AWS for a three-day <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/experience-based-acceleration/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Experience-Based Acceleration (EBA)</a> workshop. The pace surprised even the system’s own engineers, a measure of how much sophisticated logic they knew sat beneath the surface. As Eric Pitkeathly, Tech Lead, put it: “I did not believe going into the EBA that a migration from EJB/Java 8 to Spring/Java 21 was possible in 3 days! But it was.” This breakthrough did not happen by chance. Following a Modernization Assessment (MODA), the AWS team identified that most of the technological challenges could be accelerated through comprehensive support and the strategic use of agentic AI tools like <a href="https://kiro.dev/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Kiro</a> and <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/transform/custom/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Transform Custom</a>.</p> <p>This post shares how Datalex used the AWS <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/experience-based-acceleration/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Experience-Based Acceleration (EBA)</a> methodology to prove modernization feasibility, establish repeatable migration patterns, and integrate generative AI capabilities, all while maintaining their commitment to serving airline customers without disruption.</p> <h2 id="solution-overview">Solution overview</h2> <p>The AWS Experience-Based Acceleration workshop brought together 16 Datalex engineers with 6 AWS specialists for an intensive three-day engagement at the AWS Dublin offices. Rather than attempting to modernize the entire system at once, the teams focused on proving feasibility through four parallel workstreams, each tackling an important aspect of the modernization journey.</p> <ol type="1"> <li><strong>Stream 1: Microservice prototyping</strong> extracted a slice of the Reservation component from the existing n-tier architecture and refactored it into a modern Spring Boot microservice running on Java 21. This workstream proved that migration was technically feasible and established reusable patterns for the remaining code base. The Modernization Assessment revealed a decisive insight: by building a compatible runtime environment, the team could run the system’s existing code on modern technologies with minimal modifications. This was clear evidence that Datalex’s foundations were fundamentally sound and could serve as the stepping stone to the modern system rather than something to be rebuilt from scratch. The remaining code changes were then automated through AI-powered coding assistants like <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/q/developer/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Q Developer</a> and <a href="https://kiro.dev/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Kiro</a>, accelerating the transformation.</li> <li><strong>Stream 2: DevSecOps pipeline</strong> built an end-to-end continuous integration and continuous delivery (CI/CD) pipeline using AWS services including <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ecs/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Elastic Container Service (Amazon ECS)</a>, <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ecr/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Elastic Container Registry (Amazon ECR)</a>, and <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/security-hub/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Security Hub</a>. The pipeline embedded security scanning at every stage, from code commit through container deployment. This implemented a shift-left security approach that validates infrastructure as code before deployment.</li> <li><strong>Stream 3: QA and observability</strong> added proactive, real-time monitoring across the system. The team implemented comprehensive observability using Amazon CloudWatch Container Insights, CloudWatch Logs, and Datadog for application performance monitoring. Using the <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/prescriptive-guidance/latest/cloud-design-patterns/strangler-fig.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Strangler Fig pattern</a>, the AWS team advised implementing a gateway that could route requests to either the existing REST API or the modernized API through a simple parameter change. This architectural approach supported rapid non-regression testing and real-time validation of the modernization strategy, all within the three-day timeframe. The QA team also created custom dashboards that provide real-time visibility into system health and performance metrics.</li> <li><strong>Stream 4: Agentic AI proof of concept</strong> demonstrated how generative AI could enhance the system. Using <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Bedrock AgentCore</a>, the team built an AI-powered natural language interface for booking retrieval integrated with the existing REST API. The multi-agent orchestration system included specialized agents for authentication, data retrieval, and reporting, all secured through <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cognito/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Cognito</a> and integrated with <a href="https://konghq.com/products/kong-gateway" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Kong API Gateway</a>.</li> </ol> <p>The target architecture uses Amazon ECS for container orchestration, with Kong API Gateway providing protocol translation between REST and SOAP while supporting dynamic routing between existing and modernized services. With this approach, Datalex can modernize incrementally without disrupting existing airline operations.</p> <h2 id="architecture-overview">Architecture overview</h2> <p>Modernizing an airline retail system requires integrating new capabilities while maintaining existing operations. Datalex’s architecture shows how to layer generative AI agents, modern microservices, and enhanced observability onto an established, proven system without disrupting customer-facing services. The architecture uses a business service proxy to route traffic between existing and modernized components while maintaining backward compatibility.</p> <p>Datalex structured their modernization

Running multi-day AZ evacuation drills with ARC Zonal Shift

https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/architecture/running-multi-day-az-evacuation-drills-with-arc-zonal-shift/📅 2026-10-01 03:01 UTC+8

<p>Running a multi-day Availability Zone evacuation drill with ARC Zonal Shift is an effective way to prove your application can withstand a sustained impairment. Multi-Availability Zone deployment is an <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/wellarchitected/latest/reliability-pillar/welcome.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">architectural best practice for building resilient applications</a> on AWS, but there is a gap between deploying multi-AZ and proving it works under sustained stress. Traditional disaster recovery (DR) tests validate the failover mechanism. A typical test shifts traffic, confirms targets respond, and rolls back within minutes. These short exercises don’t surface the issues that only appear over hours or days.</p> <p>A multi-day AZ evacuation forces time-dependent behaviors to play out completely, exposing failure modes that brief tests miss:</p> <ul> <li>Auto Scaling policies that aren’t tuned for sustained N-1 operation over a full day.</li> <li>Deployment pipelines that don’t validate AZ health before placing new workloads.</li> <li>Stale DNS or cached database endpoints.</li> <li>Time-based routine operational processes tested against an N-1 architecture (certificate rotations, credential and secret rotations, maintenance windows, log rotation, backup automation, and cron jobs).</li> <li>Long-lived database connections pinned to a specific AZ that are only used infrequently.</li> <li>Recovery after a sustained multi-day shift, which is a different operational procedure than rolling back to a warm, nearly identical AZ within minutes.</li> </ul> <p>By shifting all traffic away from a single AZ for 48–72 hours using <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/r53recovery/latest/dg/what-is-route53-recovery.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Application Recovery Controller (ARC) Zonal Shift</a>, you force your architecture to sustain full production load on N-1 zones. This proves capacity sufficiency, database stability, and client reconnection behavior, but most importantly, that your teams can operate normally for days on N-1 capacity.</p> <p>This post shows you how to plan and run a multi-day AZ evacuation drill across Amazon Elastic Container Service (Amazon ECS), Amazon Elastic Kubernetes Service (Amazon EKS), Amazon Relational Database Service (Amazon RDS) for PostgreSQL, and Amazon Aurora PostgreSQL, with step-by-step CLI commands, a prerequisites section, observability metrics, and restore procedures.</p> <h2 id="why-financial-services-institutions-are-doing-this-already">Why financial services institutions are doing this already</h2> <p>Financial services organizations face unique regulatory pressure to demonstrate, not just document, their disaster recovery capabilities. Across the world, there is an increasing focus on building and demonstrating operational resilience within regulated entities. This is shifting the mindset from “show us your runbook” to “show us the evidence”. This uplift in control environments is driving financial services companies to conduct live DR testing under realistic conditions and produce auditable proof of recovery within defined timeframes. Some insurers and banks are now running periodic AZ evacuation drills as part of their operational resilience programs, shifting from “we have multi-AZ” to “we have proven multi-AZ”. Using <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/r53recovery/latest/dg/arc-zonal-shift.how-it-works.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">ARC Zonal Shift</a>, you can shift traffic at the infrastructure layer without changing application code. It works natively across Application Load Balancer, Network Load Balancer, Amazon Elastic Compute Cloud (Amazon EC2) Auto Scaling groups, and Amazon EKS clusters.</p> <h2 id="solution-overview">Solution overview</h2> <p>In this walkthrough, we demonstrate how to evacuate an Availability Zone for a multi-tier digital platform running on AWS.</p> <p>We deliberately include both ECS and EKS, and two database engines, to show the evacuation procedure for each major service type readers are likely to run. The architecture is illustrative only.</p> <p>The following table outlines the architecture:</p> <table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"> <tbody> <tr> <td><strong>Layer</strong></td> <td><strong>Components</strong></td> <td><strong>Multi-AZ Configuration</strong></td> </tr> <tr> <td>Traffic ingress</td> <td>Application Load Balancer (ALB) fronting ECS</td> <td>Deployed across 3 AZs, cross-zone load balancing activated</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Compute (containers)</td> <td>Amazon ECS (Fargate)</td> <td>Stateless tasks distributed across 3 AZ subnets</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Traffic ingress</td> <td>Network Load Balancer (NLB) fronting EKS</td> <td>Deployed across 3 AZs, cross-zone load balancing activated</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Compute (Kubernetes)</td> <td>Amazon EKS or EKS Auto Mode</td> <td>Stateless services with topology spread constraints across 3 AZs</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Database</td> <td>Amazon RDS for PostgreSQL</td> <td>Multi-AZ: primary in AZ A, standby in AZ B</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Database</td> <td>Amazon Aurora PostgreSQL</td> <td>Writer in AZ A, reader in AZ B. Storage replicated across all 3 AZs</td> </tr> </tbody> </table> <p>Note that the Aurora storage layer differs from standard RDS Multi-AZ. Aurora synchronously replicates data to <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonRDS/latest/AuroraUserGuide/Concepts.AuroraHighAvailability.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">six storage nodes across Availability Zones</a> independently of compute instances, so only the writer or reader instance needs to be failed over as the storage remains fully available throughout the drill.</p> <p>In this walkthrough, we evacuate AZ A, the zone hosting the RDS primary and Aurora writer.</p> <figure> <a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/fc074d501302eb2b93e2554793fcaf50b3bf7291/2026/08/24/ARCHBLOG-1571-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"><img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/fc074d501302eb2b93e2554793fcaf50b3bf7291/2026/08/24/ARCHBLOG-1571-1.png" alt="Multi-tier architecture spanning three Availability Zones: an Application Load Balancer fronting Amazon ECS and a Network Load Balancer fronting Amazon EKS, with Amazon RDS for PostgreSQL and Amazon Aurora PostgreSQL databases, before evacuating AZ A." width="800"></a> <figcaption aria-hidden="true">Multi-tier architecture spanning three Availability Zones: an Application Load Balancer fronting Amazon ECS and a Network Load Balancer fronting Amazon EKS, with Amazon RDS for PostgreSQL and Amazon Aurora PostgreSQL databases, before evacuating AZ A.</figcaption> </figure> <h2 id="how-arc-zonal-shift-works">How ARC Zonal Shift works</h2> <p>When you initiate a zonal shift, ARC takes two coordinated actions for Amazon Route 53 and Elastic Load Balancers:</p> <ol type="1"> <li>DNS removal: The load balancer’s IP address in the affected AZ is removed from DNS. New client queries don’t resolve to that endpoint.</li> <li>Cross-zone traffic blocking: Load balancer nodes in the remaining AZs stop routing requests to targets in the shifted AZ, even when cross-zone load balancing is enabled.</li> </ol> <p>For Amazon EKS clusters with zonal shift enabled, ARC goes further. It performs the following actions:</p> <ul> <li>Cordons all nodes in the impacted AZ, preventing new pod scheduling.</li> <li>Removes pod endpoints in the impacted AZ from EndpointSlice resources, redirecting east-west service-to-service traffic to healthy AZs.</li> <li>Suspends AZ rebalancing for managed node groups and updates ASGs to launch instances only in healthy AZs.</li> <li>Preserves nodes and pods in the shifted AZ (they are not terminated), keeping full capacity available for when the shift ends.</li> </ul> <p>Combined with service-specific procedures for ECS task redistribution and database failover, this cre

Build adaptive AI interfaces with the AG-UI protocol, agent swarms, and Nova Act on AWS

https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/architecture/build-adaptive-ai-interfaces-with-the-ag-ui-protocol-agent-swarms-and-nova-act-on-aws/📅 2026-09-29 21:44 UTC+8

<p>Your generative AI applications produce different results each time they run. One medical scan shows a single fracture, and another reveals twenty ambiguous regions that require expert review. Static interfaces can’t adapt to this variability.</p> <p>In this post, we show you how to build interfaces that automatically adapt to your AI’s variable outputs. This approach reduces interface development time and removes the need for custom integration code by using the AG-UI protocol, the <a href="https://strandsagents.com/latest/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Strands Agents Software Development Kit</a> (SDK), and <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/nova-act/latest/userguide/what-is-nova-act.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Nova Act</a>. You learn to build adaptive interfaces using the agent-to-UI (AG-UI) protocol for dynamic UI generation, the Strands Agents SDK Swarm pattern for multi-agent collaboration, and Amazon Nova Act for legacy system integration. After reading this post, you understand when to use adaptive interfaces and how to deploy them in your applications.</p> <h2 id="the-problem-with-static-interfaces">The problem with static interfaces</h2> <p>You design screens with fixed layouts, predetermined controls, and static data binding. This works well when your application’s output space is known and consistent. An ecommerce checkout page needs the same fields for every transaction. A dashboard displays the same metrics regardless of the data. Static interfaces excel at these predictable scenarios.</p> <p>AI-driven applications introduce new requirements. Consider two scenarios when you review bone X-rays. In the first scenario, one image shows a single obvious fracture requiring minimal interface controls. In the second scenario, another image reveals three subtle regions where multiple AI agents must debate findings, track confidence progression, and reach consensus before presenting results. A static interface optimized for the first scenario lacks the controls needed for the second. An interface built for the second scenario presents unnecessary complexity in the first scenario with empty panels and unused controls.</p> <p>This problem appears in multiple domains. Fraud detection systems encounter variable evidence chains. Legal document review surfaces unpredictable numbers of relevant clauses. Security event response reveals different threat patterns requiring different analysis tools. Domains where AI discovers things dynamically rather than classifying into predetermined categories face this architectural challenge.</p> <p>The core challenge is that AI agents discover and reason about the world dynamically, while traditional interface design assumes static, predetermined outputs.</p> <h2 id="business-impact">Business impact</h2> <p>This mismatch costs development teams significant time and creates poor user experiences. You spend weeks building interface variations to handle different scenarios, then maintain multiple code paths as your AI models evolve. Your users face either overwhelming complexity when AI finds simple results, or insufficient controls when AI discovers complex patterns requiring deeper analysis. The development cost compounds as you add new AI capabilities. Each new agent or model requires rethinking your entire interface architecture.</p> <h2 id="solution-overview">Solution overview</h2> <p>Three AWS technologies address this challenge, so you can build interfaces that adapt to what AI agents discover.</p> <p><strong>The AG-UI protocol offers standardized streaming for agent-to-user interface (UI) communication.</strong> Before AG-UI, connecting agents to interfaces required custom code for each framework. You built custom WebSocket formats, polling mechanisms, and bespoke integration code every time you switched agent frameworks or added new capabilities. AG-UI removes this work by providing a standard format of typed events that stream over Server-Sent Events (SSE). Agent frameworks emit AG-UI events, and frontends consume them, creating a universal contract so you can swap frameworks without rewriting integration code.</p> <p><strong>The Strands Agents SDK offers the Swarm pattern for peer-to-peer multi-agent collaboration.</strong> In swarm patterns, your agents operate as peers that share hypotheses and iteratively refine findings until reaching consensus. This debate process, visible to you in real time, builds trust and catches errors that single-agent systems miss. For medical imaging, the swarm pattern mirrors how radiologists consult specialists, with multiple expert perspectives converging on accurate diagnoses.</p> <p><strong>Amazon Nova Act offers browser-based automation using natural language commands</strong>, so your AI agents can interact with legacy systems through their web interfaces. Your agent navigates login screens, searches for related records, fills form fields, and captures confirmation numbers, while streaming actions back to the primary interface so you can observe the process.</p> <p>These three technologies work together naturally: AG-UI adapts your interface to swarm findings, the swarm produces explainable multi-agent analysis, and Nova Act bridges the gap with legacy systems that lack API access.</p> <h2 id="prerequisites">Prerequisites</h2> <p>Before starting, verify you have:</p> <p><strong>Required AWS Resources:</strong></p> <ul> <li>AWS account with <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Bedrock</a> access in a supported region (us-east-1, us-west-2, or eu-west-1)</li> <li>Your Identity and Access Management (IAM) user or role needs these specific permissions: <ul> <li><code>bedrock:InvokeModel</code> – For calling foundation models.</li> <li><code>bedrock:CreateAgent</code> and <code>bedrock:CreateAgentActionGroup</code> – For agent deployment.</li> <li><code>lambda:CreateFunction</code> and <code>lambda:InvokeFunction</code> – For serverless compute.</li> <li><code>s3:PutObject</code> and <code>s3:GetObject</code> – For file storage.</li> <li><code>dynamodb:PutItem</code> and <code>dynamodb:GetItem</code> – For state management.</li> <li><code>secretsmanager:GetSecretValue</code> – For credential retrieval.</li> <li><code>logs:CreateLogGroup</code> and <code>logs:PutLogEvents</code> – For <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cloudwatch/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon CloudWatch</a> logging.</li> </ul></li> </ul> <p><strong>Development Environment:</strong></p> <ul> <li>Python 3.9+ with pip installed.</li> <li>Node.js 16+ and React 18+ installed.</li> <li>AWS Command Line Interface (CLI) configured with your credentials.</li> </ul> <p><strong>Technical Skills:</strong></p> <ul> <li>Intermediate Python programming experience.</li> <li>Familiarity with event-driven architectures (your interface listens for messages from agents and updates in real time, similar to how chat applications work).</li> <li>Basic understanding of Representational State Transfer (REST) APIs and Server-Sent Events (SSE).</li> </ul> <p><strong>Data protection and HIPAA compliance</strong></p> <p>This solution processes Protected Health Information (PHI) including medical images, patient MRN, and clinical findings. Apply the following safeguards before deploying to any environment handling real patient data.</p> <p><strong>Encryption at rest</strong> — Configure SSE-KMS with a customer managed key on all Amazon S3 buckets storing medical images. Enable encryption with a customer managed AWS KMS key on all DynamoDB tables storing session state, conversation history, and analysis findings.</p> <p><strong>Encryption in transit</strong> — Enforce TLS 1.2+ on all connections. Attach a bucket policy denying all S3 actions when aws:SecureTransport is false. Do not override DynamoDB SDK endpoints to HTTP. Do not set ignore_https_errors=True on Nova Act workflows. If the legacy system uses self-signed certificates, add its CA to your runtime trust store.</p> <p><strong>S3 Block Public Access</strong> — Enable Block Public Access at the account level and on every bucket in this solution. Medical images must never be exposed through public bucket policies or ACLs.</p> <p><strong>HIPAA-eligible services and BAA</strong> — All AWS services in this architecture (Amazon Bedrock, Amazon S3, DynamoDB, Lambda, API Gateway, CloudFront, Cognito, Secrets Manager, CloudWatch) are HIPAA-eligible. Before processing PHI, execute a Business Associate Agreement (BAA) with AWS covering these services.</p> <p><strong>PHI minimization</strong> — Never write MRN, patient name, or clinical findings to plaintext logs. Enable CloudWatch Logs data protection policies to detect and mask PHI patterns automatically. Suppress Nova Act trajectory logging during steps that display patient data. Verify Cognito JWT on the SSE endpoint before emitting any PHI-bearing event.</p> <p><strong>Important:</strong> Code samples in this post are for educational purposes. Review all configurations against your organization’s HIPAA Security Rule implementation before production deployment.</p> <h2 id="architecture">Architecture</h2> <p>You implement an orchestrator pattern where your frontend interacts with a single entry point that internally coordinates specialized sub-agents. The solution follows this pattern: your React app sends requests to Amazon API Gateway, which triggers AWS Lambda functions that coordinate AI agents through Amazon Bedrock, then streams results back over Server-Sent Events. We use <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/agentcore-get-started-toolkit.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Bedrock AgentCore</a>, a platform to build, connect, and optimize agents at scale with any framework or model.</p> <div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"> <a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/fc074d501302eb2b93e2554793fcaf50b3bf7291/2026/09/28/ARCHBLOG-1366-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"><img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/fc074d501302eb2b93e2554793fcaf50b3bf7291/2026/09/28/ARCHBLOG-1366-1.png" alt="Logical view of the radiology portal: the React AG-UI client streams over SSE
