Bedrock</p> </div> <div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"> <a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/fc074d501302eb2b93e2554793fcaf50b3bf7291/2026/09/28/ARCHBLOG-1366-2.jpeg" target="_blank" rel="noopener"><img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/fc074d501302eb2b93e2554793fcaf50b3bf7291/2026/09/28/ARCHBLOG-1366-2.jpeg" alt="Detailed AWS architecture with 16 numbered components spanning Amazon Cognito and AWS STS, Amazon CloudFront and Amazon S3, Amazon API Gateway, AWS Lambda, Amazon DynamoDB, Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, the Strands agent swarm, Amazon Bedrock, Amazon OpenSearch Serverless, Amazon Nova Act, and AWS Secrets Manager" width="800"></a> <p class="wp-caption-text">Figure 2: End-to-end AWS architecture showing the 16 components that deliver authentication, content delivery, agent orchestration, foundation model inference, and legacy system automation</p> </div> <h3 id="architecture-components">Architecture components</h3> <p>The architecture consists of 16 integrated components working together:</p> <p><strong>① User authentication</strong> You authenticate through <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cognito/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Cognito</a>, which provides secure identity management and generates JWT tokens for accessing the radiology portal application.</p> <p><strong>② Content delivery</strong> <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cloudfront/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon CloudFront</a> serves the React application and static assets from <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/s3/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon S3</a>, providing global low-latency access and caching for optimal performance.</p> <p><strong>③ API Gateway</strong> <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/api-gateway/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon API Gateway</a> handles both REST API requests and Server-Sent Events (SSE) connections, providing the entry point for client-server communication.</p> <p><strong>④ Authorization of user API request with token validation</strong></p> <p><strong>⑤ Backend processing</strong> <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/lambda/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Lambda</a> functions act as the AG-UI handler, managing authentication, invoking agents through <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/gateway.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway</a>, and formatting responses as SSE streams.</p> <p><strong>⑥ Image storage</strong> Amazon S3 stores medical images with SSE-KMS encryption using a customer managed key. S3 Block Public Access is enabled at the bucket level. Lambda generates short-lived, tightly scoped pre-signed URLs for secure direct uploads, pinned to the PUT method, scoped to a per-user key prefix, restricted to the <code>application/dicom</code> content type, and set to expire in 300 seconds. A bucket policy enforces maximum upload size through the <code>s3:content-length-range</code> condition and denies requests where <code>aws:SecureTransport</code> is <code>false</code>.</p> <p><strong>⑦ Session management</strong> <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/dynamodb/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon DynamoDB</a> maintains session state, conversation history, analysis findings, and agent registry data for stateful multi-turn interactions.</p> <p><strong>⑧ AgentCore Gateway</strong> <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Bedrock</a> AgentCore Gateway serves as the orchestration layer, routing agent requests, managing sessions, coordinating multi-agent workflows, and load balancing across runtime instances.</p> <p><strong>⑨ AG-UI handler</strong> A specialized component within AgentCore that manages AG-UI protocol events, formatting agent responses as standardized events for dynamic UI rendering.</p> <p><strong>⑩ AgentCore runtime</strong> AgentCore Runtime provides the execution environment for agent instances, running the Strands Agent Swarm with isolated runtime instances for each agent type.</p> <p><strong>⑪ Agent swarm execution</strong> The Strands Agent Swarm consists of three specialized agents (Image Analysis, Clinical Reasoning, Reporting) that collaborate through iterative debate rounds to reach consensus.</p> <p><strong>⑫ Foundation model inference</strong> Amazon Bedrock provides access to the Claude Sonnet foundation model for reasoning, interpretation, and natural language generation across agents using a large language model (LLM).</p> <p><strong>⑬ Knowledge base retrieval</strong> <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/opensearch-service/features/serverless/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon OpenSearch Serverless</a> stores medical knowledge base with vector embeddings, providing semantic search for relevant medical literature and clinical guidelines.</p> <p><strong>⑭ Legacy system automation</strong> Amazon Nova Act performs browser-based automation to submit validated findings to a legacy hospital’s Radiology Information System (RIS) and Electronic Medical Record (EMR) systems, with each action streamed back to your UI.</p> <p><strong>⑮ Credential management</strong> <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/secretsmanager/latest/userguide/intro.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Secrets Manager</a> securely stores and rotates credentials for legacy system access, providing Nova Act with authentication details at runtime.</p> <p><strong>⑯ Observability and monitoring</strong> <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cloudwatch/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon CloudWatch</a> captures logs and metrics, with data protection policies enabled to detect and mask PHI. AWS Distro for OpenTelemetry (ADOT) provides distributed tracing with AWS X-Ray-compatible trace export. AWS Security Token Service (AWS STS) manages temporary security credentials.</p> <h3 id="key-architectural-decisions">Key architectural decisions</h3> <p>Your frontend sees one agent (radiology-assistant), not three, through the orchestrator pattern. This simplifies integration and encapsulates workflow complexity. The orchestrator internally coordinates the swarm based on analysis stage.</p> <p>Rather than generating arbitrary HTML, agents select from themed, accessible components (ROICard, DebatePanel, ConfidenceMeter). This balances flexibility with design consistency and security through the predefined component library approach.</p> <p>SSE provides real-time updates as agents work through the streaming protocol. You see agent contributions character by character, creating transparency into the reasoning process.</p> <p>Your frontend exposes state (current findings, validation decisions) to agents through bidirectional state synchronization. Agents update state through actions. This synchronization supports human-in-the-loop workflows where agents pause for validation before proceeding.</p> <h2 id="solution-walkthrough">Solution walkthrough</h2> <p>The following steps walk through the solution, from authentication and swarm configuration to the AG-UI endpoint, legacy system integration, and deployment.</p> <h3 id="step-0-configure-authentication">Step 0: Configure authentication</h3> <p>Add JWT validation to your API endpoint to enforce authentication before processing requests containing PHI.</p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-python">from fastapi import Depends, HTTPException, Request import os COGNITO_USER_POOL_ID = os.environ["COGNITO_USER_POOL_ID"] COGNITO_APP_CLIENT_ID = os.environ["COGNITO_APP_CLIENT_ID"] async def verify_token(request: Request): """Validate Cognito JWT from Authorization header.""" token = request.headers.get("Authorization", "").replace("Bearer ", "") if not token: raise HTTPException(status_code=401, detail="Missing authorization") # Validate token against Cognito JWKS endpoint # See: https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cognito/latest/developerguide/amazon-cognito-user-pools-using-tokens-verifying-a-jwt.html return validate_jwt(token, COGNITO_USER_POOL_ID, COGNITO_APP_CLIENT_ID) @app.post("/api/agui") async def agui_endpoint(request: Request, user=Depends(verify_token)): ...</code></pre> </div> <p>Full Amazon Cognito user pool setup (pool creation, hosted UI, and token exchange) is covered in the <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cognito/latest/developerguide/cognito-user-identity-pools.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Cognito Developer Guide</a>. This post focuses on the agent architecture layer.</p> <h3 id="step-1-install-the-strands-agents-sdk">Step 1: Install the Strands Agents SDK</h3> <p>Install required packages by running the following command:</p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-bash">pip install strands-agents ag-ui-strands fastapi uvicorn</code></pre> </div> <p>This command installs the required packages for building your multi-agent swarm, including the Strands framework, AG-UI integration, and web server components.</p> <h3 id="step-2-configure-your-swarm-agents">Step 2: Configure your swarm agents</h3> <p>Create specialized agents for your swarm by adding the following code to your project:</p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-python">from strands import Agent from strands.models import BedrockModel from typing import Dict, List, AsyncGenerator import os region = os.environ.get("AWS_REGION", "us-east-1") class RadiologySwarm: """Coordinates multi-agent analysis with visible debate.""" def __init__( self, consensus_threshold: float = 0.80, max_rounds: int = 5, ): self.consensus_threshold = consensus_threshold self.max_rounds = max_rounds self.agents = self._initialize_agents() self.hypotheses = [] def _initialize_agents(self) -&gt; List[Agent]: """Create specialized agents for swarm.""" model = BedrockModel( model_id=os.environ.get("BEDROCK_MODEL_ID", "us.anthropic.claude-sonnet-4-20250514-v1:0") ) return [ Agent( name="image_analysis", system_prompt="Detect abnormal patterns in medical images...", model=model, ), Agent( name="clinical_reasoning", system_prompt="Validate findings with clinical context...", model=model, ), Agent( name="reporting", system_prompt="Structure findings in clinical format...", model=model, ), ] async def analyze_with_debate_stream( self, image_data: bytes, patient_context: Dict, ) -&gt; AsyncGenerator[Dict, None]: """Stream swarm analysis events for real-time UI updates.""" # Implementation continues in next steps... pass</code></pre> </div> <p><strong>Note:</strong> The agents use a foundation model accessed through Amazon Bedrock. The example defaults to Claude Sonnet 4, but you should select a currently active model from the Amazon Bedrock model lifecycle page for your AWS Region. Read the model ID from an environment variable so you can update it without code changes as newer models become available. Amazon Bedrock retires models on a published lifecycle schedule, so hardcoding a model ID risks failure when that model reaches end of life. Check the <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/model-lifecycle.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Bedrock model lifecycle page</a> and <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/models-regions.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">supported Regions</a>, and use an environment variable or AWS Systems Manager Parameter Store to manage the model ID externally.</p> <p>This code creates three specialized agents (Image Analysis, Clinical Reasoning, Reporting) that work together as peers in a swarm pattern, sharing hypotheses and refining findings through iterative debate until reaching consensus.</p> <h3 id="step-3-implement-consensus-mechanism">Step 3: Implement consensus mechanism</h3> <p>Configure your swarm to continue debate rounds until agents reach consensus or exhaust maximum iterations:</p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-python">from typing import Dict, List class RadiologySwarm: """Consensus mechanism implementation.""" def __init__( self, consensus_threshold: float = 0.80, max_rounds: int = 5, ): self.consensus_threshold = consensus_threshold self.max_rounds = max_rounds self.hypotheses = [] async def analyze_with_debate_stream( self, image_data: bytes, patient_context: Dict, ): """Stream swarm analysis with consensus checking.""" yield {"type": "swarm_start", "max_rounds": self.max_rounds} for round_num in range(1, self.max_rounds + 1): # Each agent contributes based on current hypotheses for agent in self.agents: try: context = { "image_data": image_data, "patient_context": patient_context, "round": round_num, } async for chunk in agent.stream_response(context): yield { "type": "agent_contribution_chunk", "agent": agent.name, "round": round_num, "text": chunk, } except Exception as e: yield { "type": "agent_error", "agent": agent.name, "round": round_num, "error": str(e), } # Check if consensus reached after all agents contribute if self._check_consensus(): yield {"type": "consensus_reached", "round": round_num} break yield {"type": "swarm_complete", "findings": self.hypotheses} def _check_consensus(self) -&gt; bool: """Verify hypotheses meet confidence threshold.""" if not self.hypotheses: return False # Check if all hypotheses have confidence above threshold for hypothesis in self.hypotheses: if hypothesis.get("confidence", 0) &lt; self.consensus_threshold: return False return True</code></pre> </div> <p>Your swarm reaches consensus when proposed findings achieve confidence scores above your configured threshold (80% for this example). Confidence progresses as agents validate or debate each other.</p> <p>In the first example of subtle fracture detection, Round 1 shows the Image Agent proposing a Region of Interest (ROI) with confidence above 80%. Round 2 shows the Clinical Agent validating with anatomical context, maintaining confidence above the threshold. Round 3 shows agents agreeing on clinical significance, reaching consensus when the three agents reach confidence scores above the configured threshold.</p> <p>In the second example of false positive challenge, Round 1 shows the Image Agent proposing an ROI with confidence above the threshold. Round 2 shows the Clinical Agent challenging it as an artifact, with confidence dropping below 80%. Round 3 shows the Image Agent acknowledging the challenge, with confidence dropping further. Round 4 shows agents continuing debate without consensus. Round 5 shows maximum rounds reached, with the finding marked as disputed.</p> <p>This iterative refinement, visible to you in real time, provides explainability that black-box AI systems can’t match.</p> <h3 id="step-4-set-up-the-ag-ui-protocol-endpoint">Step 4: Set up the AG-UI protocol endpoint</h3> <p>Create a streaming endpoint by adding the following code:</p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-python">from fastapi import FastAPI, Request from fastapi.responses import StreamingResponse import json app = FastAPI() @app.post("/api/agui") async def agui_endpoint(request: Request): """AG-UI streaming endpoint emitting typed events.""" body = await request.json() async def event_stream(): """Stream standardized AG-UI events.""" swarm = RadiologySwarm() async for event in swarm.analyze_with_debate_stream( image_data=body["image_data"], patient_context=body["patient_context"], ): yield format_sse_event(event) return StreamingResponse( event_stream(), media_type="text/event-stream", ) def format_sse_event(event: Dict) -&gt; str: """Format as Server-Sent Event.""" return f"data: {json.dumps(event)}\n\n"</code></pre> </div> <p>This code creates a streaming endpoint that emits standardized AG-UI events, so your frontend receives real-time updates as agents work without requiring custom integration code for each agent framework.</p> <p>The AG-UI protocol defines event types that cover agent-to-UI communication needs. Instead of custom formats, agents emit structured events over SSE, and frontends subscribe to this stream and react to each event type. <code>TEXT_MESSAGE_CONTENT</code> streams agent reasoning or responses token by token for real-time visibility into agent thinking. <code>STATE_DELTA</code> provides incremental state updates for bidirectional synchronization between agent and interface. <code>TOOL_CALL_START</code> and <code>TOOL_CALL_END</code> show tool execution when agents invoke external functions or APIs. With <code>UI_COMPONENT_SPEC</code>, agents control which UI components appear and how they’re configured through interface element specifications.</p> <p>Configure your frontend to subscribe to this stream and handle each event type:</p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-javascript">// Frontend event handler eventSource.onmessage = (event) =&gt; { const data = JSON.parse(event.data); switch (data.type) { case "text_message_content": appendAgentText(data.agent, data.text); break; case "state_delta": updateApplicationState(data.path, data.value); break; case "ui_component_spec": renderComponent(data.component, data.props); break; } };</code></pre> </div> <h3 id="step-5-implement-real-time-agent-debate-visualization">Step 5: Implement real-time agent debate visualization</h3> <p>Create interface components that make multi-agent collaboration visible to you by implementing the following visualization features:</p> <p>A key benefit of the swarm pattern combined with AG-UI is making multi-agent collaboration visible. Rather than presenting you with final results from a black-box system, the interface shows agents debating findings in real time.</p> <p>Your interface includes specialized components for visualizing agent collaboration. The agent contribution display shows each agent’s reasoning streaming character by character with a typing effect, indicating which agent is currently “thinking.” Color-coding distinguishes agents (blue for Image Analysis, purple for Clinical Reasoning, cyan for Reporting). The confidence timeline uses a line graph to show how confidence evolves across rounds. Increasing confidence (72% → 78% → 85%) indicates agents converging on consensus. Decreasing confidence (68% → 52% → 45%) shows successful challenge of a false positive. Evidence cards display each region of interest with a status badge (Challenged, Consensus, Disputed) based on the debate outcome, an AI-generated summary of key reasoning points, and an expandable section showing complete agent contributions for radiologists who want detailed analysis.</p> <p>This visibility serves multiple purposes. For explainability, you see why agents reached conclusions, not only what they concluded. For error detection, visible debate helps you spot flawed reasoning. For confidence calibration, watching agents debate each other helps you assess reliability. For educational value, radiologists learn from agent reasoning, improving their own analysis.</p> <h3 id="step-6-integrate-amazon-nova-act-for-legacy-systems">Step 6: Integrate Amazon Nova Act for legacy systems</h3> <p>Implement browser automation to submit findings to legacy systems by adding the following code:</p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-python">import json import boto3 from typing import Dict class LegacyRISSubmission: """Browser automation for legacy RIS submission.""" def __init__( self, secret_name: str = "ris-credentials", region_name: str = "us-east-1" ): """Initialize with AWS Secrets Manager configuration.""" self.secret_name = secret_name self.secretsmanager = boto3.client("secretsmanager", region_name=region_name) def _get_credentials(self) -&gt; Dict: """Retrieve credentials from AWS Secrets Manager.""" response = self.secretsmanager.get_secret_value(SecretId=self.secret_name) return json.loads(response["SecretString"]) def submit_report(self, report_data: Dict, ris_url: str) -&gt; Dict: """Submit validated findings to legacy RIS.""" from nova_act import NovaAct from nova_act.types.workflow import Workflow creds = self._get_credentials() with Workflow( model_id="us.amazon.nova-act-v1:0", workflow_definition_name="radiology-submission" ) as workflow: with NovaAct( starting_page=f"{ris_url}/login", headless=True, workflow=workflow, ) as nova: # Authentication nova.act("Click on the username input field") nova.type_text(creds["username"], sensitive=True) nova.act("Click on the password input field") nova.type_text(creds["password"], sensitive=True) nova.act("Click Sign In button") # Patient lookup mrn = report_data["patient_mrn"] nova.act(f"Search for patient MRN '{mrn}'") nova.act("Click View Record button") # Report submission nova.act("Click New Report button") findings_text = report_data["findings_summary"] nova.act(f"Fill findings textarea with: {findings_text}") nova.act("Upload annotated image file") nova.act("Click Submit Report button") # Capture confirmation result = nova.act("Find and return the RPT- confirmation number") return {"status": "success", "confirmation": result, "mrn": mrn}</code></pre> </div> <p><strong>Security note</strong>: Nova Act captures prompts and screenshots as trajectory data. Never interpolate credentials or PHI into act() commands. Use type_text with sensitive=True to prevent credential capture in logs and trajectories. In production, suppress trajectory capture entirely for authentication steps, or route trajectory storage to a KMS-encrypted, access-controlled bucket subject to your BAA</p> <p>Many enterprise systems, particularly in healthcare, lack modern APIs. Hospital RIS and EMR systems often run on decades-old technology stacks that can’t be modified without significant effort. Amazon Nova Act offers a pragmatic solution: browser-based automation using natural language commands.</p> <p>This code implements browser automation that interacts with legacy systems through their existing web interfaces, retrieving credentials securely from AWS Secrets Manager and streaming each action back to your interface for transparency.</p> <p>Each Nova Act command streams back to the radiology portal, so you can observe the submission process. Your interface displays a live action log showing authentication, navigation, form filling, and confirmation capture. This transparency helps you understand what the automation is doing and intervene if issues arise.</p> <p>Browser automation requires careful credential management. The implementation stores credentials in AWS Secrets Manager, retrieves them at runtime, and never exposes them to the frontend. Audit logging captures Nova Act actions for compliance and troubleshooting. For production deployment, additional controls include IP allowlisting, session timeout enforcement, and multi-factor authentication where supported by legacy systems.</p> <h3 id="step-7-deploy-to-aws">Step 7: Deploy to AWS</h3> <p>Configure DynamoDB tables with customer-managed KMS encryption:</p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-python">import boto3 dynamodb = boto3.client("dynamodb") dynamodb.create_table( TableName="radiology-sessions", KeySchema=[{"AttributeName": "session_id", "KeyType": "HASH"}], AttributeDefinitions=[{"AttributeName": "session_id", "AttributeType": "S"}], BillingMode="PAY_PER_REQUEST", SSESpecification={ "Enabled": True, "SSEType": "KMS", "KMSMasterKeyId": "arn:aws:kms:us-east-1:ACCOUNT:key/YOUR-KEY-ID" }, )</code></pre> </div> <p>Deploy your solution using Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, which offers managed runtime for agents with built-in identity, memory, and observability. AgentCore supports long-running tasks (up to 8 hours), asynchronous tool execution, and native CloudWatch integration, making it ideal for production deployments requiring minimal operational overhead.</p> <h2 id="pattern-selection-guidance">Pattern selection guidance</h2> <p>Choosing the right architecture pattern depends on problem characteristics and requirements.</p> <h3 id="when-to-use-dynamic-agent-generated-uis">When to use dynamic agent-generated UIs</h3> <table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"> <tbody> <tr> <td><strong>Characteristic</strong></td> <td><strong>Dynamic UI (AG-UI)</strong></td> <td><strong>Static UI (Traditional)</strong></td> </tr> <tr> <td>Output Variability</td> <td>High – unpredictable number/structure of results</td> <td>Low – consistent data structure</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Multi-Agent Value</td> <td>Visible collaboration builds trust</td> <td>Single agent or no collaboration to show</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Interface Complexity</td> <td>Varies per case</td> <td>Consistent across cases</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Explainability Needs</td> <td>Important – users must understand reasoning</td> <td>Less important – results speak for themselves</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Development Effort</td> <td>Higher – protocol integration, component library</td> <td>Lower – standard REST API</td> </tr> </tbody> </table> <h3 id="swarm-compared-to-other-multi-agent-patterns">Swarm compared to other multi-agent patterns</h3> <p>Use swarm when multiple perspectives improve accuracy (peer review, consensus building), debate process offers value (explainability, error detection), no clear hierarchy exists (agents are peers, not supervisor/worker), and iterative refinement is beneficial (findings improve through rounds).</p> <p>Use agents as tools when clear task decomposition exists (supervisor delegates to specialists), subtasks are independent (parallel execution possible), hierarchy is natural (manager coordinating experts), and no need for peer debate exists (each agent’s output stands alone).</p> <p>Use sequential workflow when strict ordering is required (step B needs step A’s output), checkpoints are needed (validate before proceeding), process is well-defined (known stages, dependencies), and no benefit from parallel exploration exists (linear pipeline).</p> <h3 id="use-cases-beyond-medical-imaging">Use cases beyond medical imaging</h3> <p>This architectural pattern applies to domains with high output variability and multi-agent value. For fraud detection, you encounter variable evidence chains (1-20 suspicious transactions), multiple analyst perspectives (financial, behavioral, network analysis), and legacy banking systems requiring browser automation. For legal document review, you face unpredictable numbers of relevant clauses, expert opinions from different legal domains (contract law, regulatory compliance, risk assessment), and integration with legacy case management systems. For security event response, you deal with variable threat indicators, team collaboration (network analysis, malware analysis, threat intelligence), and legacy Security Information and Event Management (SIEM) systems without modern APIs.</p> <h3 id="anti-patterns">Anti-patterns</h3> <p>Avoid this approach when you have simple classification (predetermined categories with fixed confidence scores), deterministic calculations (no uncertainty or debate needed), low-latency requirements (multi-round debate adds latency), cost-sensitive scenarios with predictable outputs (swarm pattern increases token usage), or minimal explainability needs (users trust results without seeing reasoning).</p> <h2 id="clean-up">Clean up</h2> <p>To avoid incurring future charges, delete the resources you created while following this walkthrough. Delete them in the following order. The sequence removes the frontend and compute layers first, then the data stores, and finally the encryption keys and IAM roles, so no deletion fails because another resource still depends on it. Because this solution can process PHI, this teardown also removes any stored medical images, patient identifiers, and findings.</p> <ol type="1"> <li><strong>Amazon CloudFront —</strong> Disable the distribution, let it propagate, then delete it. This stops traffic and releases the S3 app bucket.</li> <li><strong>Amazon API Gateway —</strong> Delete the REST API and SSE endpoint.</li> <li><strong>Amazon Bedrock AgentCore —</strong> Delete the Gateway, then the Runtime instances (and their identity, memory, and session resources). This stops all agent activity against the downstream services.</li> <li><strong>AWS Lambda —</strong> Delete the AG-UI handler and any other functions.</li> <li><strong>Amazon Bedrock model access —</strong> Confirm nothing is still invoking the models. Revoke access to Claude Sonnet or Amazon Nova Act if enabled only for this walkthrough.</li> <li><strong>Amazon OpenSearch Serverless —</strong> Delete the knowledge base collection and its access, network, and encryption policies. OCUs bill hourly.</li> <li><strong>Amazon DynamoDB —</strong> Delete the radiology-sessions table and any other tables you created.</li> <li><strong>Amazon S3 —</strong> Empty (including all object versions) and delete the medical-image and app-hosting buckets.</li> <li><strong>AWS Secrets Manager —</strong> Delete the legacy RIS/EMR credential secrets. A 7 to 30 day recovery window applies unless you force deletion.</li> <li><strong>Amazon Cognito —</strong> Delete the user pool and app client.</li> <li><strong>AWS KMS —</strong> Only now, schedule deletion of the customer managed keys used for Amazon S3 and DynamoDB. Deleting them earlier can make encrypted data unrecoverable.</li> <li><strong>AWS IAM —</strong> Delete the roles and policies created for Lambda and AgentCore.</li> <li><strong>Amazon CloudWatch —</strong> Delete the log groups, dashboards, and alarms (kept until last for troubleshooting).</li> </ol> <p>Finally, review the AWS Billing and Cost Management console to confirm no unexpected charges remain.</p> <h2 id="conclusion">Conclusion</h2> <p>In this post, we showed you how to build adaptive AI interfaces using three AWS technologies. You learned to use the AG-UI protocol for dynamic UI generation, apply Strands swarm patterns for multi-agent collaboration, and integrate Amazon Nova Act for legacy systems. Through a complete radiology assistant implementation, you saw how these technologies work together to handle variable AI outputs while maintaining explainability and practical enterprise integration.</p> <p>The AG-UI protocol creates interfaces that adapt to what your agents discover, not what you anticipated. Use it when output variability is high and you can’t predetermine interface requirements. The Strands swarm pattern creates explainability through visible multi-agent debate. Use when multiple perspectives improve accuracy and you benefit from seeing reasoning processes. Amazon Nova Act bridges the gap with legacy systems lacking APIs. Use when browser automation is the only viable integration path and action transparency matters.</p> <p>These technologies work together naturally because they address different aspects of the same problem: building AI systems that are flexible, explainable, and practical for real-world enterprise environments.</p> <h2 id="next-steps">Next steps</h2> <ul> <li>Explore the <a href="https://github.com/ag-ui-protocol/ag-ui" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AG-UI protocol on GitHub</a>.</li> <li>Read the <a href="https://strandsagents.com/latest" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Strands Agents SDK documentation</a>.</li> <li>Learn more about <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/what-is-bedrock-agentcore.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Bedrock AgentCore</a>.</li> </ul> <p>Read related AWS blogs:</p> <ul> <li>Multi-Agent Collaboration Patterns with Strands Agents and Amazon Nova.</li> <li>Strands Agents SDK: A Technical Deep Dive into Agent Architectures and Observability.</li> <li>Build a Drug Discovery Research Assistant using Strands Agents and Amazon Bedrock.</li> </ul> <p>&nbsp;</p> <p style="clear: both"></p> <hr style="width: 100%"> <h2>About the authors</h2>

ReadyOn’s Four Walls of tenant isolation on Amazon EKS

https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/architecture/readyons-four-walls-of-tenant-isolation-on-amazon-eks/📅 2026-09-19 05:33 UTC+8

ReadyOn, an AWS Partner serving Fortune 100 enterprises, has built a four-layer tenant isolation architecture on Amazon EKS to protect highly sensitive workforce data including payroll and organizational hierarchies. The model stacks Kubernetes namespace isolation, Karpenter-managed dedicated node pools, per-tenant VPC security groups, and dedicated Aurora database clusters so that an attacker would need to breach all four independent layers simultaneously to cross a tenant boundary. This approach matters because default Kubernetes deployments are not secure out of the box, and a single namespace boundary is insufficient when the threat model shifts from unauthorized access to cross-tenant data leakage across multiple regulatory jurisdictions.

How CSIRO built scalable, cost-optimized genomic variant querying on AWS

https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/architecture/how-csiro-built-scalable-cost-optimized-genomic-variant-querying-on-aws/📅 2026-09-18 22:35 UTC+8

<p><em>This is a guest post by Denis Bauer, Yatish Jain, Anuradha Wickramarachchi, Brendan Hosking, and Nick Edwards of CSIRO, in collaboration with the ASP Prototyping and Scaling Team at AWS.</em></p> <p>In this post, we describe how researchers at <a href="https://www.csiro.au/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">CSIRO</a>, Australia’s national science agency, built Serverless Beacon (sBeacon), a scalable serverless solution for securely querying genomic variant data on AWS, underpinning production-scale clinical and research applications.</p> <p>The <a href="https://docs.genomebeacons.org/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Beacon protocol</a> is the widely adopted standard for exchanging genomic and phenotypic data developed by the <a href="https://www.ga4gh.org/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Global Alliance for Genomics and Health (GA4GH)</a>. It uses an API to define how data is shared, with the goal of enabling efficient and secure data discovery across international research and clinical networks.</p> <p>sBeacon is a production-ready implementation of this standard, built using AWS services: <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/s3/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3)</a>, <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/lambda/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Lambda</a>, <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/dynamodb/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon DynamoDB</a>, and <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/athena/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Athena</a>. By using these foundational AWS serverless services, sBeacon is able to provide the following benefits to researchers and clinicians needing to perform genomic variant querying:</p> <ul> <li><strong>Highly scalable for large cohorts:</strong> sBeacon can scale to support hundreds of millions of individuals (and billions of genomic locations), which makes it suitable even for mega-biobank-scale datasets.</li> <li><strong>Low cost to run:</strong> Because it uses a serverless, cloud-native architecture, sBeacon can operate for approximately USD 0.40 per month for a 1000 Genomes-scale dataset. The following case study breaks down ingestion, query, and storage costs in detail.</li> <li><strong>High performance and fast query response:</strong> Real-world queries return in seconds (about 5 seconds) because of the serverless compute and efficient architecture, for near real-time data lookups.</li> <li><strong>No heavy data ingestion or transformation needed:</strong> sBeacon can directly consume standard VCF files (a common format for genomic variant data), which reduces the need to load data into databases or transform it to different data structures.</li> <li><strong>Rapid onboarding of new data:</strong> Genomic data generation is accelerating because it underpins clinical diagnosis and treatment, and because its complexity demands ever-larger cohorts to study complex traits. As a result, both clinical services and research cohorts must continuously onboard new data, and Beacon supports real-time generation-to-use life cycles (about 18 seconds).</li> <li><strong>Improved privacy, data ownership, and decentralization:</strong> Because sBeacon doesn’t require central databases and supports federated networks, data stays under the control of original holders, which can help data custodians address privacy and ethical considerations in sensitive genomic and medical data sharing.</li> <li><strong>Lower barrier to entry for broader participation:</strong> Its affordability, simplicity, and small operational footprint can help make it more accessible for smaller or resource-limited institutions and countries, which can increase participation from underrepresented populations and improve data diversity.</li> <li><strong>Zero trust model:</strong> sBeacon enforces explicit authentication, least-privilege data access, ephemeral compute isolation, and strict cloud-native boundary controls that help confirm no component, user, or request is implicitly trusted.</li> </ul> <h2 id="prerequisites">Prerequisites</h2> <p>sBeacon is deployed as a container that sets up the necessary development environment, with Terraform defining the resources for the deployment. To get started, clone the <a href="https://github.com/aehrc/terraform-aws-serverless-beacon/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">terraform-aws-serverless-beacon repository</a> on the GitHub website.</p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-bash">git clone https://github.com/aehrc/terraform-aws-serverless-beacon.git</code></pre> </div> <p>Make sure that your development environment contains Docker and has the necessary permissions for you to use it without super user access. Press <strong>Ctrl+Shift+P</strong> (<strong>Cmd+Shift+P</strong> on macOS) to open the command palette in VS Code, and then choose <strong>Reopen in Container</strong>. This opens the workspace in the container environment that we have defined.</p> <p>Now, run the following command to initialize the necessary libraries and Lambda layers.</p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-bash">bash init.sh</code></pre> </div> <p>Next, run the following command to initialize the Terraform environment.</p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-bash">terraform init</code></pre> </div> <p>Optionally, you can define a backend by following the instructions in the repository. After the preceding command runs successfully, you can run the deployment command.</p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-bash">terraform apply</code></pre> </div> <p>Enter <code>yes</code> when prompted to proceed with the deployment. After the deployment is complete, you receive information such as the API URL and the command to sign in as the admin or guest user. To shut down the entire service, run <code>terraform destroy</code>. Any created datasets are lost (but not the VCFs on which they are based).</p> <h2 id="solution-walkthrough">Solution walkthrough</h2> <p>CSIRO developed sBeacon for sharing and querying genomic and medical data. sBeacon uses AWS serverless technology for the elastic scaling of compute resources.</p> <p>The architecture of sBeacon performs two broad processes:</p> <ol type="1"> <li>Data onboarding: the ingestion and indexing of genomic metadata into sBeacon.</li> <li>Data querying: the querying of the genomic metadata by end users.</li> </ol> <h3 id="data-onboarding">Data onboarding</h3> <p>During the onboarding process, you define where the genomic data and the metadata (such as disease status, age, and location) is located. Note that genomic data is not copied out of its original location but rather is referenced when needed. In contrast, metadata is loaded to sBeacon’s storage mechanisms because it is necessary to perform indexing that allows efficient querying. The user will need to ensure no sensitive or privacy-revealing data is disclosed. The example details the approach using CSIRO’s Ontoserver. However, sBeacon supports the API schema of the Ensembl OLS V4 specification.</p> <div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"> <img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/fc074d501302eb2b93e2554793fcaf50b3bf7291/2026/09/09/Figure-1-Data-Onboarding.png" alt="Data onboarding architecture for sBeacon, showing genomic data location submitted to an API Gateway endpoint, AWS Lambda functions handling indexing, metadata written to Amazon S3 in ORC format, CSIRO Ontoserver building the ontology index, and Amazon Athena building the metadata tables." width="800"> <p class="wp-caption-text">Figure 1. Data onboarding.</p> </div> <p>The data onboarding process is summarized by the following steps:</p> <ol type="1"> <li>The onboarding starts with the user submitting the location of the genomic data as request payloads to an API Gateway endpoint.</li> <li>The request payloads are forwarded to an AWS Lambda function that handles the data indexing.</li> <li>The metadata is written to an Amazon S3 bucket in the ORC format, to allow future querying and processing by Athena.</li> <li>An AWS Lambda function is called to orchestrate the indexing process.</li> <li>The <a href="https://www.ontoserver.csiro.au/site/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">CSIRO Ontoserver</a> is called to build the ontology index for advanced metadata queries.</li> <li>The resulting index files are written to Amazon S3.</li> <li><code>CREATE TABLE AS SELECT</code> (CTAS) queries are run on Amazon Athena to build the metadata tables.</li> <li>Athena loads the metadata from Amazon S3 into the metadata tables.</li> <li>The metadata tables are written back to Amazon S3 in ORC format.</li> </ol> <h3 id="data-querying">Data querying</h3> <p>Querying in sBeacon is flexible, catering to a wide range of applications from human genetic disease to pathogen queries. We achieved this by designing the query architecture modularly. This approach let us separate the querying logic into several Lambda functions based on their querying scope, while maintaining a similar architecture.</p> <p>The following architecture diagram describes the workflow for metadata querying, which uses the Variant Querying Module described later in this section.</p> <div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"> <img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/fc074d501302eb2b93e2554793fcaf50b3bf7291/2026/08/27/ARCHBLOG-1307-2.png" alt="Metadata querying architecture for sBeacon, showing a user query sent to an API Gateway endpoint, the Microservice Lambda function looking up ontology terms in Amazon DynamoDB, querying metadata tables in Amazon Athena, and querying the Variant Querying Module before returning a Beacon-formatted result." width="800"> <p class="wp-caption-text">Figure 2. Data querying.</p> </div> <ol type="1"> <li>The user submits their query to the API Gateway endpoint.</li> <li>API Gateway calls the Microservice Lambda function.</li> <li>The Microservice Lambda function looks up the relevant query ontology terms in an Amazon DynamoDB table.</li> <li>The matching ontology descendent terms (and their codes) are returned to the Microservice Lambda function. The descendent terms are those that match a hierarchical descendent of each term, or each term itself, from the query.</li> <li>Using the ontology codes from step 3, the metadata tables on Athena are queried.</li> <li>The metadata associated with the query is returned from Athena.</li> <li>If required by the query, the Microservice Lambda function queries the Variant Querying Module.</li> <li>The variant data associated with the genomic conditions in the query is returned to the Microservice Lambda function.</li> <li>The result is formatted according to the Beacon protocol and is returned to the user through Amazon API Gateway.</li> <li>The response is received by the user.</li> </ol> <div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"> <img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/fc074d501302eb2b93e2554793fcaf50b3bf7291/2026/09/09/Figure-3-Variant-Querying-Module.png" alt="Variant Querying Module architecture for sBeacon, showing an Initiator Lambda function fanning out the splitQuery and performQuery Lambda functions across VCF files in Amazon S3, and optionally querying metadata from Amazon Athena before returning results to the Microservice Lambda function." width="800"> <p class="wp-caption-text">Figure 3. Variant Querying Module.</p> </div> <p>Genomic variant queries are performed using the Variant Querying Module. The workflow of this module is as follows:</p> <ol type="1"> <li>The Microservice Lambda function calls an Initiator Lambda function.</li> <li>The Initiator Lambda function fans out the <code>splitQuery</code> Lambda function across the VCF files.</li> <li>The <code>performQuery</code> Lambda function is then fanned out across the VCF regions in each of the files involved in the query.</li> <li>The <code>performQuery</code> Lambda function fetches the VCF files from Amazon S3.</li> <li>The query results are synchronously returned to the parent Initiator Lambda function.</li> <li>If requested by the user, metadata can optionally be queried, where the Initiator Lambda function queries the metadata from Athena.</li> <li>Athena queries the metadata from Amazon S3 (through an external table).</li> <li>The metadata results are returned to Athena.</li> <li>The Initiator Lambda function receives the metadata from Athena.</li> <li>All the query results, including any optional metadata, are returned to the calling Microservice Lambda function.</li> </ol> <h2 id="case-study-1000-genomes-dataset">Case study: 1000 Genomes dataset</h2> <p>We demonstrate sBeacon on chromosome 1 of the <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/marketplace/pp/prodview-bcuxxyvo4twb2#resources" target="_blank" rel="noopener">1000 Genomes Project</a> to report how it handles large-scale variant queries. We measure ingestion efficiency, query scalability, and cost for typical population-scale analyses, such as identifying SNP variants across defined genomic regions. The case study uses chromosome 1 (chr1, 8% of the genome) from the 1000 Genomes Project, which contains 2504 samples. This multi-sample VCF is approximately 1.1 GB compressed, with data stored in Amazon S3. Note that sBeacon can also process cohorts of single-sample VCF files. All costs in this section are for the Asia Pacific (Sydney) Region (ap-southeast-2), exclude applicable taxes, and reflect pricing at the time of writing.</p> <p>sBeacon can ingest chromosome 1 from the 2504 individuals in 18 seconds, for less than 1 cent (USD 0.00052). This is because sBeacon does not copy the large genomic information but instead creates index files that enable random access. Cost is therefore driven predominantly by storing the copied metadata. After ingestion, sBeacon can be maintained for USD 0.000025 per month (1 MB of compressed metadata stored for 2504 samples in ORC format, plus genomic index files). If you store the genomic data as well, this would be USD 0.032 for chr1 (at USD 0.025 per GB in ap-southeast-2) or about USD 0.425 for the whole genome.</p> <p>Query time is similarly near real time. For example, querying across a region of 10,000 base pairs to determine the genotypes in this region takes 1.52 seconds across the 2504 individuals. This would serve a query such as “Fetch all individuals with a specific BRCA1 mutation who have stage 3 cancer.” The cost for such a query is USD 0.00013. Note how the query time stays constant even with an increasing number of variants returned (for example, from 4 to 400).</p> <p><em>Table 1. Query example costing and times (whole chromosome 1).</em></p> <table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"> <tbody> <tr> <td><strong>Query region size (bases)</strong></td> <td><strong>Number of variants found</strong></td> <td><strong>Average Time</strong></td> <td><strong>Compute Cost (per query in USD)</strong></td> </tr> <tr> <td>10</td> <td>4</td> <td>1.51 s (+- 0.26)</td> <td>0.00013</td> </tr> <tr> <td>100</td> <td>18</td> <td>1.52 s (+- 0.25)</td> <td>0.00013</td> </tr> <tr> <td>1,000</td> <td>84</td> <td>1.62 s (+- 0.24)</td> <td>0.00014</td> </tr> <tr> <td>5,000</td> <td>229</td> <td>1.65 s (+- 0.29)</td> <td>0.00014</td> </tr> <tr> <td>10,000</td> <td>400</td> <td>1.52 s (+- 0.11)</td> <td>0.00013</td> </tr> </tbody> </table> <p><em>Table 2. Cost for ingestion, querying, and idling (whole chromosome 1 for 2504 genomes with less than 10 MB of metadata).</em></p> <table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"> <tbody> <tr> <td><strong>Scenario</strong></td> <td><strong>Metric</strong></td> <td><strong>Cost (USD) per month</strong></td> </tr> <tr> <td>Ingestion Cost</td> <td>per 1000 ingestions</td> <td>0.53 (32.82 GB seconds of Lambda)</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Query compute cost</td> <td>per 1000 queries</td> <td>0.28 (9.8 GB seconds of Lambda)</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Query Athena Cost</td> <td>per 1000 queries</td> <td>0.05</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Idle Cost (Storage Cost)</td> <td>1.1 GB</td> <td>0.03</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Query DynamoDB Cost</td> <td>Per 1000 queries</td> <td>0.0005</td> </tr> </tbody> </table> <h2 id="security-features">Security features</h2> <p>Security and compliance is a shared responsibility between AWS and the customer. AWS is responsible for protecting the infrastructure that runs the AWS services described in this post, and you are responsible for your use of those services, including how you configure them, which identities you grant access to, and which data you choose to onboard. Consider the services you choose carefully, because your responsibilities vary depending on the services used, how you integrate those services into your IT environment, and applicable laws and regulations. For more information, see the <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/compliance/shared-responsibility-model/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Shared Responsibility Model</a>.</p> <h3 id="zero-trust-model">Zero trust model</h3> <ul> <li><strong>Explicit authentication and authorization</strong> – Every API request must carry a valid JWT issued by the Amazon Cognito user pool (<code>aws_api_gateway_authorizer.BeaconUserPool-authorizer</code>, type <code>COGNITO_USER_POOLS</code>). The authorizer runs at API Gateway before any Lambda function is invoked, so requests do not reach a handler without Cognito validation. Token validation includes signature, expiry, and audience (Cognito app client ID). You can disable authentication during the first deployment with <code>BEACON_ENABLE_AUTH = false</code> for intentionally public or open beacons. This is an explicit operator decision, not a default.</li> </ul> <p>Authorization (what a valid user can do) is enforced inside the Lambda layer, not in Amazon API Gateway:</p> <ul> <li>Group membership (<code>sbeacon-record-access-user-group</code>, and so on) controls the maximum granularity returned.</li> <li>Admin-only operations (dataset submission, deletion) check for <code>sbeacon-admin-group</code> membership before proceeding.</li> <li><strong>Least-privilege data access</strong> – sBeacon implements role-based access control (RBAC) through Cognito groups that map directly to disclosure tiers. You assign each user one or more of the following:</li> </ul> <table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"> <tbody> <tr> <td><strong>Cognito group</strong></td> <td><strong>Maximum disclosure</strong></td> </tr> <tr> <td><code>sbeacon-boolean-access-user-group</code></td> <td>exists: true/false only</td> </tr> <tr> <td><code>sbeacon-count-access-user-group</code></td> <td>aggregate counts</td> </tr> <tr> <td><code>sbeacon-record-access-user-group</code></td> <td>full variant details and sample names</td> </tr> <tr> <td><code>sbeacon-admin-group</code></td> <td>preceding tiers plus dataset management</td> </tr> </tbody> </table> <p>The JWT carries the user’s group memberships as claims. The query Lambda function reads these claims to determine <code>requested_granularity</code> and <code>include_details</code>, then passes both flags to <code>performQuery</code>. <code>performQuery</code> computes only what was requested. A boolean-tier user’s request does not cause sample-level data to be computed or returned, even if it exists in the VCF.</p> <ul> <li><strong>Ephemeral compute isolation</strong> – Lambda execution environments are stateless by design. Each cold start is a fresh container, <code>/tmp</code> (1,024 MB for <code>performQuery</code>) is cleared between cold starts, and concurrent invocations run in separate sandboxes with no shared memory. The <code>bcftools</code> subprocess inside <code>performQuery</code> runs and exits within the Lambda function lifetime (10 second timeout). No state persists after invocation.</li> <li><strong>Cloud-native boundary controls</strong> – API Gateway is the public entry point in this architecture. Amazon S3 buckets, DynamoDB tables, Athena, and Amazon SNS topics have no public resource policies. Amazon S3 buckets are created with private ACLs and <code>BucketOwnerPreferred</code> ownership controls. Lambda functions run on AWS-managed VPCs with no inbound network access. Amazon SNS topics are account-private (no external principal grants).</li> </ul> <h3 id="privacy-and-data-ownership">Privacy and data ownership</h3> <p>Each institution deploys the entire Terraform stack into its own AWS account, so there is no shared infrastructure, no central data lake, and no cross-account trust. VCF files live in the deploying institution’s Amazon S3 bucket and do not leave it. <code>performQuery</code> passes the Amazon S3 URL directly to <code>bcftools</code> as a subprocess argument, which uses <code>htslib</code> HTTP byte-range requests to read only the tabix-indexed region of interest (about 1 KB per query). The raw genomic sequence bytes do not pass through Lambda memory as returnable data. What the query returns upstream (exists as a boolean, <code>call_count</code> as an integer, and variant representations) is aggregate result data, not source sequence.</p> <p>Decentralization in sBeacon is achieved at the storage layer, not the compute layer. The <code>_vcfLocations</code> registered for a dataset are Amazon S3 URIs, and these can point to buckets owned by entirely different organizations. When a query runs, <code>performQuery</code> passes each URI directly to <code>bcftools</code>, and <code>htslib</code> issues HTTP byte-range requests (<code>Range: bytes=X-Y</code>) against the Amazon S3 REST API of whichever organization owns that bucket. The raw VCF bytes do not leave the source organization’s Amazon S3 bucket. Only the query result (exists, count, or variant record) is returned.</p> <h3 id="data-onboarding-privacy">Data onboarding privacy</h3> <p>The <code>submitDataset</code> endpoint sits behind the same API Gateway Cognito authorizer as all other endpoints. An unauthenticated request receives a <code>401</code> response before reaching any Lambda function. Beyond authentication, the handler also checks that the caller is a member of <code>sbeacon-admin-group</code>. A valid token from a user in only record-access or count-access is rejected. This means the beacon operator explicitly controls the set of people who can introduce data into the system, so onboarding is not a self-service capability.</p> <h2 id="further-considerations">Further considerations</h2> <p>We chose AWS Lambda over AWS Step Functions in this architecture because it can process much larger payloads. Given the size and complexity of genomic data and the fan-in and fan-out architecture for parallel handling, AWS Lambda emerged as the lower-cost and more flexible approach for this workload.</p> <p>As demonstrated in the sBeacon publication, the architecture can cater to population-scale datasets. However, if you accidentally attempt to run a range query of the entire genome, the architecture times out at the Amazon API Gateway level. Applying functional operations over the whole genome requires further architectural considerations.</p> <p>Because a single fan-out query spawns many parallel Lambda invocations, you need to monitor concurrency consumption to confirm that burst queries do not exhaust the account’s concurrency pool and starve other functions. Tracking the <code>ConcurrentExecutions</code> metric at both the account and function level provides early visibility into capacity pressure.</p> <p>Similarly, because synchronous Lambda invoke does not automatically retry on throttle, a <code>429</code> response from a <code>performQuery</code> invocation means the result is silently lost unless the application handles it explicitly. Setting Amazon CloudWatch alarms on the <code>Throttles</code> metric for <code>performQuery</code> allows you to take corrective action, such as requesting a concurrency limit increase, before throttles affect query accuracy. Alternatively, we have produced a separate architecture that sends alert email with diagnostic information when Lambda functions fail, available in the <a href="https://github.com/bhosking/error-catcher" target="_blank" rel="noopener">error-catcher repository</a> on the GitHub website. You can implement this in the repository or set it up as a standalone service to catch Lambda errors thrown by sBeacon.</p> <p>After idle periods, simultaneous <code>performQuery</code> invocations might encounter cold starts that add latency to query responses. Enabling provisioned concurrency on the query-path Lambda functions helps reduce this cold-start latency during burst fan-out scenarios at the price of increasing the idle cost.</p> <h2 id="conclusion">Conclusion</h2> <p>In this post, we described how CSIRO built sBeacon, a fast, scalable, and low-cost way to run genomics workloads on AWS. sBeacon implements the GA4GH Beacon standard with a fully serverless and modular architecture. This publicly available solution supports near real-time querying of standard VCF data, scales to mega-biobank cohorts, minimizes ingestion effort, and supports privacy and zero-trust security. If you are considering genomics on AWS, you can deploy sBeacon on existing Amazon S3-hosted VCF data, integrate it with clinical or research workflows through the Beacon API, and progressively federate with other Beacons for secure, cross-institutional genomic data discovery. <a href="https://bioinformatics.csiro.au/serverless-beacon/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Set up sBeacon</a> to query your genomic data and explore the possibilities of securely sharing insights with your collaborators. You can read more about sBeacon in our publication: <a href="https://www.nature.com/articles/s41587-023-01972-9" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Scalable genomic data exchange and analytics with sBeacon</a>. The source code for sBeacon can be downloaded from our <a href="https://github.com/aehrc/terraform-aws-serverless-beacon" target="_blank" rel="noopener">GitHub repository</a>.</p> <hr> <h2>About the authors</h2>

Read full article →

How Equinix cut operational overhead with a shared services architecture on Amazon EKS

https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/architecture/how-equinix-cut-operational-overhead-with-a-shared-services-architecture-on-amazon-eks/📅 2026-09-18 02:00 UTC+8

Equinix, the world's largest digital infrastructure company, migrated from a self-managed Kubernetes environment on Amazon EC2 to a shared services architecture on Amazon EKS to eliminate the operational sprawl caused by dozens of application teams independently managing their own clusters. The new multi-account design centralizes governance, networking, CI/CD, and data services under a dedicated cloud operations team while application teams focus solely on their business workloads. The shift delivered four times faster deployments and significantly reduced operational overhead across the organization.
